1. Executive Summary
1.1. Application Introduction
On October 15, 2021, Omeros Corporation (the Applicant) submitted a Biologics License
Application (BLA) 761152 for Yartemlea (narsoplimab-wuug), a monoclonal antibody (mAb) that
inhibits mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of
the lectin pathway of complement for the proposed indication of the treatment of patients with
hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA). The
proposed dosage of narsoplimab is 4 mg/kg administered as an intravenous infusion once a
(b) (4)
week
. On October 15, 2021, the Agency issued a Complete Response
Letter (CRL). On March 26, 2025, the Applicant submitted a complete response to the CRL,
seeking approval for narsoplimab for the treatment of patients with hematopoietic stem cell
transplantation thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA). For the purposes of this review,
narsoplimab-wuug will be referred to as narsoplimab.
The basis of the CR was that the data submitted as part of the original BLA failed to
demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness for narsoplimab for the proposed indication.
For details regarding the specific review issues identified during initial review and contributing
to the decision to issue a CRL see Section 0. For complete details of the original review see
summary review uploaded to DARRTS on October 15, 2021. For a brief summary of the
regulatory history see Section 2 of this review.
New information submitted with this resubmission include:
• Response to Deficiencies Cited in the Complete Response Letter, External Control Data,
and Summary
• Compassionate Use Expanded Access Data which included pediatric patients
On May 20, 2025, and May 27, 2025, the Applicant submitted new data from the external
control, which included individual patient data registered on the Kyoto Stem Cell
Transplantation Group (KSCTG) registry. This data had not been previously submitted to or
reviewed by the Agency, on July 10, 2025; therefore, the Agency notified the Applicant that
these submissions constituted a major amendment to the application and that the goal date
would be extended by three months to the current PDUFA date, in order to provide time for a
full review of the new data.
Based on the data included with this resubmission, the entire multidisciplinary review team
recommends approval of narsoplimab for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients two
years of age and older with hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic

microangiopathy (TA-TMA). The proposed dosing regimen is:
• Greater than or equal to 50kg: 370mg by intravenous infusion over 30 minutes once
weekly. Increase frequency to twice weekly if there is inadequate improvement in TATMA signs and symptoms.
• Less than 50kg: 4mg/kg by intravenous infusion over 30 minutes once weekly. Increase
frequency to twice weekly if there is inadequate improvement in TA-TMA signs and
symptoms.

1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE)
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) of narsoplimab in adults with TA-TMA was
established based on one adequate and well controlled clinical investigation with confirmatory
evidence. The adequate and well-controlled investigation is Study OMS-721-TMA-001 (Study
TMA-001). Confirmatory evidence was provided by efficacy data from patients treated under an
expanded access program for whom patient level data was available (EAP Subgroup 1).
Study TMA-001 is a single-arm, multicenter trial of narsoplimab in adult patients with TA-TMA,
with a control provided by a historical comparator rate in untreated patients based on the
literature.
Study TMA-001 enrolled 28 patients with TA-TMA if they had diagnosis of TA-TMA that
persisted at least 2 weeks following modification or discontinuation of treatment with
calcineurin inhibitors (CNI) or at least 30 days after transplantation, with platelet count
<150,000/µL, evidence of microangiopathic hemolysis (presence of schistocytes, serum LDH >
upper limit of normal (ULN), or haptoglobin < lower limit of normal), and renal dysfunction. All
patients also met criteria for high-risk TMA, based on the high-risk criteria as defined by recent
proposed consensus guidelines (Schoettler et al., 2023). The primary efficacy endpoint was the
proportion of TA-TMA patients who responded to narsoplimab treatment. Treatment response
was defined as an improvement in TMA- associated laboratory markers (platelet count and
LDH) as well as improvement in clinical status (improvement in organ function at any time point
post-baseline during the study or freedom of transfusion).
In Study TMA-001, 17 of 28 patients (61%; 95% CI: 40.6, 78.5) achieved a TMA response. This
was compared to a historic response rate of 23.3% (95% CI: 15.1, 34.2), which was based on
analysis of 149 patients with TA-TMA identified in the literature who had not received
narsoplimab or other pharmacologic treatment for TA-TMA and who had a response in one of
the laboratory assessments or a clinical response. The review team concluded that calculating a
response rate based on all patients from the selected literature who had not received prior
pharmacologic treatment, including those from studies with less stringent response criteria
than used in Study TMA-001, would likely overestimate an historic response rate, and therefore
could be considered as an acceptable benchmark for study TMA-001 response rate to be
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

9

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
measured against. The TMA-001 response rate of 61% is higher than the selected literature
based historic control response rate 23.3% (95% CI: 15.1, 34.2) with non-overlapping
confidence intervals.
Confirmatory evidence of effectiveness was demonstrated in 19 patients (13 adult, 6 pediatric)
who were treated under the EAP program and for whom patient level data was available (EAP
Subgroup 1). The TA-TMA response rate for EAP Subgroup 1 overall was 13/19 (68%),
consistent with the findings for the primary endpoint of Study TMA-001.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for narsoplimab in pediatric patients aged 2 years and
older with TA-TMA was based on partial extrapolation of clinical benefit demonstrated in
adults. This extrapolation is reasonable given the similarity in the pathophysiology, clinical signs
and symptoms, and course of TA-TMA in adults and pediatric patients. Partial extrapolation is
supported by response to treatment data from 6 patients in the EAP Subgroup 1 who had
baseline disease characteristics and TA-TMA response rates consistent with the adult patients.

1.3.Benefit-Risk Assessment

Benefit-Risk Framework
Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
•

Analysis of
Condition

•

•

TA-TMA is a challenging complication of HSCT associated with
significant morbidity and mortality. The complex pathophysiology
and multiple contributing factors converge on a final pathway
involving endothelial cell injury and complement activation. TA-TMA
is characterized by microangiopathic hemolytic anemia,
thrombocytopenia, elevated serum lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)
and presence of schistocytes on peripheral blood smear. The gold
standard for diagnosis is based on characteristic histological tissue
findings; however, the high risk for procedural complications and
bleeding limits the ability to obtain a tissue diagnosis.
The incidence of TA-TMA varies among studies due to different
criteria used for diagnosis. There are several risk factors described
in the literature for the development of TA-TMA include older age,
female sex, advanced primary cancer disease, unrelated donor
transplants, high-dose conditioning regimen, human leukocyte
antigen (HLA) mismatch, nonmyeloablative transplants, total body
irradiation, calcineurin use, acute graft-versus host disease and
infection.
The clinical presentation ranges from mild, self-limited disease to
more serious disease involving multi-organ involvement (kidneys,
gastrointestinal tract, central nervous system, heart, lung, and
serosal surfaces), with severe disease leading to multi-organ failure
and death.

CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

Conclusions and Reasons
TA-TMA is a rare, serious and life-threatening
condition. It is a major cause of morbidity and
mortality post allogenic HSCT in both adult and
pediatric populations.

11

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
•

TA-TMA has a poor prognosis with mortality rates described in
literature in excess of 50% (Schoettler et al., 2023).

•

There are no FDA approved therapies for the treatment of TATMA. A range of treatments have been used in this condition
but none with established efficacy. Therapeutic interventions
include removal of the insulting agent, transfusion support,
and treating concurrent conditions such as GVHD and
infections. While often considered as an initial step in
management, modification and/or discontinuation of
calcineurin inhibitors and/or sirolimus may not be effective as
there is no clear evidence that discontinuation of these
agents provides a benefit to patients with TA-TMA. The role
of total plasma exchange in TA-TMA remains unclear and is
often not beneficial. Off-label therapies such as defibrotide
and rituximab have demonstrated variable response rates
and have an unclear impact on mortality. Other complement
inhibitors such as C5 inhibitors are currently being
investigated and frequently used off-label in treatment of TATMA.
The benefit of narsoplimab was assessed in a single-arm, externally
controlled, open-label study (Study TMA-001), which enrolled 28
patients with TA-TMA who received narsoplimab every week for 4
to 8 weeks. The primary endpoint was response rate, which was
defined as improvement in two laboratory TMA markers (LDH and
platelet counts) and either improvement in organ function or
independence from transfusions.
In Study TMA-001, 17 of 28 patients (61%; 95% CI: 40.6, 78.5)
achieved a TMA response. This was compared to an historic

Conclusions and Reasons

There are no approved therapies for pediatric or
adult patients with TA-TMA. There is an unmet
clinical need for an effective approved therapy
for patients with TA-TMA.

The submitted data demonstrate substantial
evidence of effectiveness on TA-TMA response.
The magnitude of response rate to treatment,
compared to literature based historic control,
demonstrates that narsoplimab is active in this
disease.

the underlying malignancy or chemotherapy.
The identified risks can be adequately addressed

1.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
TA-TMA is a rare, serious and life-threatening disease associated with a high mortality rate.
There are no approved treatments for this condition in adults or pediatric patients. Thus, TATMA represents an unmet medical need.
The benefit of narsoplimab was evaluated in a single-arm, externally controlled, open-label trial
(Study TMA-001) of 28 patients with high-risk TA-TMA who received narsoplimab for 4 to 8
weeks, supported by detailed patient-level data for 19 high-risk patients treated under the
narsoplimab EAP (EAP Subgroup 1). TMA response, defined as improvement in (i) both of two
laboratory TMA markers (LDH levels and platelet counts) and (ii) at least one of two clinical
findings, specifically improvement in organ function and freedom from transfusion, was
achieved in 17/28 (61%) patients in Study TMA-001 and 13/19 (68.4%) of the EAP Subgroup 1
patients. These results were compared to a threshold based on historical literature review.
The study protocol for Study TMA-001 specified study objectives, enrollment criteria, outcome
measures and an analysis plan to evaluate outcomes, which helped inform FDA’s determination
that the characteristics of an adequate and well-controlled study are present in Study TMA-001.
The primary evidence supporting the safety and efficacy assessment in this resubmission
derives from Study TMA-001 submitted with the original BLA along with the new information
provided by the Applicant with this submission. Study TMA-001 is a single-arm, multicenter trial
of narsoplimab in adult patients with TA-TMA. Study TMA-001 enrolled 28 patients with TATMA if they had diagnosis of TA-TMA that persisted at least 2 weeks following modification or
discontinuation of CNI treatment or at least 30 days after transplantation with platelet count <
150,000/µL, evidence of microangiopathic hemolysis (presence of schistocytes, serum LDH >
upper limit of normal (ULN), or haptoglobin < lower limit of normal) and renal dysfunction.
During this review, after consideration of the additional submitted data for the 5 patients for
which the review team disagreed with the Applicant’s assessment as responders during the
review of the initial cycle, the Agency verified the Applicant’s primary outcome measure
assessed as the proportion of patients with TA-TMA who responded to narsoplimab treatment
with a response rate of 61% (17/28) with a 95% CI of (40.6 to 78.5).
Although the Applicant did not identify any new literature sources in this resubmission, after
additional consideration, the review team concluded that calculating a response rate based on
all patients in the non-pharmacologically NPTP group, including those from studies with less
stringent response criteria than Study TMA-001, would likely overestimate an historic response
rate and therefore, could be considered as a benchmark for Study TMA-001 to be measured
against. The TMA-001 response rate of 61% is higher than the selected literature based historic
control response rate 23.3% (95% CI: 15.1, 34.2), with non-overlapping confidence intervals.

15

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
The Division concludes that there is a treatment effect of narsoplimab in patients with high-risk
TA-TMA based on response rate.
Confirmatory evidence is derived from data for 19 patients from the EAP for which there was
patient level data and was discussed in Castelli et al (Castelli et al., 2024). (EAP Subgroup 1).
Patients in EAP Subgroup 1 were thrombocytopenic, had evidence of microangiopathic
hemolytic anemia, and had at least one feature of high-risk TMA. The overall response rate in
EAP Subgroup 1 was 68% (13/19) with a 95% CI of (43.4, 87.4). When examined by age, 9 of 13
adult patients (69%; 95% CI: 38.6, 90.9) and 4 of 6 pediatric patients (67%; 95% CI: 22.3, 95.7)
were responders. Thus, the overall response rate and for adults and for the pediatric subsets of
EAP Subgroup 1 were consistent with the response rate seen in Study TMA-001.
The Sponsor also submitted analysis and data from a subset of patients enrolled on the Kyoto
Stem Cell Transplantation Group registry to serve as an external control for survival endpoints.
Due to significant limitations with comparability of the KSTG registry, results cannot be used as
comparator for survival data to provide primary evidence or confirmatory evidence for
regulatory decision making. See Section 5.3.6.2 for details.
The safety of narsoplimab was primarily assessed in the 28 patients from Study TMA-001. The
221 patients enrolled in the EAP provided supportive safety data. As expected, based on the
mechanism of action (MOA) of the drug, narsoplimab has risk of serious infections. In addition,
based on the MOA and literature review, there may be a risk of increased susceptibility to
tuberculosis. While no patient in clinical trials with narsoplimab were reported to have a
positive mycobacterium culture, one patient developed granulomatous orchitis. No
information regarding tuberculosis nor granulomatous orchitis will be included in the label
because the patient was found to be culture negative (including for mycobacteria) and the
infection resolved without treatment. The long-term risk of serious infections will be further
assessed in a PMR.
Pediatric and adult TA-TMA share similar underlying pathophysiology with similar
characteristics such as endothelial damage and complement activation after HSCT with similar
pattern of organ involvement, microangiopathic anemia. In the 6 pediatric patients with subject
level data available, the baseline disease characteristics and TMA response rates were
consistent with what was observed in the adult population. Safety data is derived from a total
of 97 pediatric patients age less than 17 from the EAP. No new safety signals were observed.
Given the similar pathophysiology between adults and pediatric patients reasonable to consider
partial extrapolation of efficacy from the adult population to support the pediatric indication in
pediatric patients age > 2 with TA-TMA.
The proposed dosing regimen allows for an escalation of dosing frequency. Study TMA-001
studied a once weekly regimen, and the EAP employed a twice weekly regimen of narsoplimab.
During the review, the Applicant clarified that once weekly administration is appropriate as the
starting regimen, however, given inter-patient variability in disease severity and level of lectin
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

16

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
pathway hyperactivation, increasing the frequency to twice weekly administration may be
clinically justified in patients who do not respond adequately to narsoplimab treatment.
Although direct evidence showing improvement in patient response escalating from once
weekly to twice weekly regimen is not available, from a pharmacodynamic (PD) perspective, a
more frequent (e.g., twice weekly) dosing regimen is expected to maintain higher lectin
pathway inhibition throughout the inter-dosing interval compared to once weekly regimen. It is
also reassuring that the safety profile of a twice weekly regimen employed in the EAP is
acceptable ensuring no additional safety risk to patients escalating to a more frequent regimen.
Although the typical duration of treatment for TA-TMA is relatively shorter (<10 weeks), as
patients are frequently monitored, it is still practically feasible to escalate the dosing frequency
based on inadequate treatment response.
Narsoplimab addresses an unmet medical need, as this will be the first FDA-approved drug to
treat patients with TA-TMA. The benefit-risk profile is favorable for narsoplimab for the
treatment of adult and pediatric (age 2 and greater) patients for the treatment of high-risk
TMA.
This application resubmission was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. The Applicant
has adequately addressed all deficiencies in the CRL. Safety review issues were resolved in the
original review of the application, see finalized review in DARRTS on October 15, 2021. Each
discipline recommends approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application
should be approved. The overall benefit-risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk
Framework above.

2. Therapeutic Context
2.1. Analysis of Condition
TA-TMA occurs when endothelial injury and a complex underlying pathophysiology (Meri S et
al., 2022), in the context of an allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation, causes
microangiopathic anemia and platelet consumption leading to microvascular thrombosis and
fibrin deposition. TA-TMA may present with new onset of increasing proteinuria, hypertension,
elevated serum creatinine and an increase in transfusion requirements with evidence of
intravascular hemolysis. TA-TMA survival rates vary in the reported literature due to different
diagnostic criteria, heterogenous populations in terms of severity, index dates (e.g. date of
HSCT, date of diagnosis of TMA) and other factors. Some patients will have a self-remitting
course but more than one-half of patients will progress to multi-organ failure with mortality
rates of greater than 50% at 1 year post-HSCT (Schoettler, 2022) with some literature citing
mortality rates for severe TA-TMA exceeding 80% (Jodele et al., 2024, Acosta-Medina et al.,
2025, Cho et al., 2008) and reported non-relapse mortality at 42% for patients identified with
severe TA-TMA (Vasu et al., 2025).
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
17
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug

2.2. Available Therapy
There are no FDA approved products for the treatment of TA-TMA. The first line of treatment is
generally limited to supportive therapy. Initial first line therapy has included withdrawal or
minimization of CNIs (i.e. sirolimus, tacrolimus, or both) as these agents have been implicated
as risk factors although there is ongoing debate as to whether withdrawal is beneficial.
Additional measures include treatment of infections, GVHD and aggressive hypertension
management. Off-label medications such as rituximab1, defibrotide2 and complement inhibitors
such as C5 inhibitors (ravulizumab3, ecluzimab4) have emerged as potential therapeutic agents
in TA-TMA.
1Rituximab is CD20 directed cytolytic antibody indicated for the treatment of adult patients with Non-Hodgkins

Lymphoma, adult patients with chronic lymphocytic leukemia, rheumatoid arthritis in combination with
methotrexate in adult patients, granulomatosis with polyangiitis and microscopic polyangiitis in adult and pediatric
patients 2 years of age and older, moderate to severe pemphigus vulgaris in adult patients.
2 Defibrotide is indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with hepatic veno-occlusive disease
(VOD).
3 Ravulizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients one month of
age and older with paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) and atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome.
4 Eculizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of patients with PNH, treatment of patients
with aHUS, treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis in adult patients, treatment of neuromyelitis optica
spectrum disorder in adult patients.

4. Approach to Review of the Resubmission for Efficacy
In this resubmission, the Applicant included the original efficacy data from Study TMA-001
along with efficacy data (response and survival) for 19 patients treated on the EAP for whom
there were patient-level data (EAP Subgroup 1), efficacy data (survival) for 221 patients treated
on the EAP (EAP Subgroup 2), and data from 121 patients with TA-TMA who did not receive
narsoplimab from the Kyoto Stem Cell Transplantation Group (KSCTG) registry intended to
provide a comparator group for the survival endpoints. In addition, the Applicant provided
additional data for the 5 responders from the original submission who were assessed by the
Agency as non-responders in the original review.
The approach to the review consisted of the following:
1. Evaluate evidence of benefit based on primary endpoint of response rate:
a. Reviewed and assess Sponsor’s rationale for the 5 disputed responders in Study
TMA-001 and re-adjudicate the Divisions assessment.
b. Reviewed the literature based historic response rate.
c. Analyzed data for EAP Subgroup 1 for response rate.
d. Integrated efficacy analysis data from TMA-001 and EAP Subgroup1 for the primary
response endpoint.
e. Provided overall summary and conclusions for the primary endpoint.
2. Evaluate evidence of benefit based on survival data:
a. Analyzed survival data for TMA-001, EAP Subgroup 1, and Pooled (TMA-001 + EAP
Subgroup 1), and EAP Subgroup 2.

5. Evidence of Benefit
5.1.Sources of Data
5.1.1. Study TMA-001
Study TMA-001 is a single-arm, open-label study in which 28 patients with TA-TMA received
narsoplimab once weekly for a median of 8 weeks (range: 2-16). In total, 24 patients received
narsoplimab 4 mg/kg intravenously once weekly and 4 patients received narsoplimab 370 mg
intravenously once weekly. Key inclusion criteria were a diagnosis of TA-TMA that persisted at
least 2 weeks following modification or discontinuation of treatment with calcineurin inhibitor
(CNI) or at least 30 days after transplant with platelet count <150,000/µL, evidence of
microangiopathic hemolysis (presence of schistocytes, serum LDH > upper limit of normal
(ULN), or haptoglobin < lower limit of normal), and renal dysfunction.
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of TA-TMA patients who responded to
narsoplimab treatment. There were several secondary endpoints to include 100day survival
from date of TMA diagnosis and overall survival from the date of TMA diagnosis. For a full list
of all the secondary endpoints, refer to the initial review date October 15, 2021 in DARRTS.
Efficacy data from TMA-001 was analyzed for the response (primary) and survival endpoints.
5.1.2. Compassionate Use Expanded Access Program (EAP)
The single patient expanded access treatment plan (EAP; also known as a compassionate use
plan) provides patients access to the investigational product narsoplimab. The purpose of the
EAP is to provide access to patients with TA-TMA who are not able to participate in a study and
who may benefit from treatment based on the medical judgment of their respective physicians.
This is an uncontrolled, case-by-case patient EAP. Dosing for patients ≥50 kg is narsoplimab 370
mg initially twice weekly and for at least 8 weeks. Dosing for patients ≤50 kg is 4 mg/kg initially
twice weekly and for at least 8 weeks. Change in dose frequency is determined by treatment
response.
The treatment course included the following parameters and measurements:
• Terminate treatment after 8 weeks if the patient has not demonstrated improvement
in at least platelet count or LDH defined for each as return to pre-TMA baseline level.

CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

20

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
• Reduce treatment frequency to once weekly when the patient achieves all of
following:
o Platelet count return to pre-TMA level
o LDH return to pre-TMA level
o Reduction of platelet and red blood cell transfusions to their respective preTMA frequencies
o Urine protein/creatinine ratio (uPCR) < 2.0
o Resolution of reversible TMA-related neurological abnormalities (e.g.,
headache, seizure, hallucination, or confusion) or stabilization of irreversible
neurological abnormalities (e.g., stroke deficits)
o Resolution of TMA-related gastrointestinal signs and symptoms
o Age-appropriate control of blood pressure on ≤ 2 anti-hypertensive
medications.
• Discontinue treatment when the patient maintains all of the above criteria for 2 weeks
while receiving narsoplimab once weekly.
• The patient will be retreated according to the original treatment regimen if they
experience a relapse of TMA that includes organ dysfunction.
Patient Eligibility
Main Inclusion Criteria
1. Patient (adult or pediatric) must have undergone allogeneic or autologous
hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT).
2. Patient, parent or legal guardian must be capable of understanding and complying with
the treatment protocol.
3. Patient must be capable of understanding an informed consent form (ICF) or the parent
or legal guardian of the patient is able to understand and sign a written ICF prior to the
initiation of any treatment procedures.
4. Patient, parent or legal guardian must voluntarily provide informed consent in
accordance with local regulations and governing ethics committee requirements prior to
any procedures or evaluations performed specifically for the sole purpose of the
program
5. If sexually active and of childbearing potential, the patient must agree to practice a
highly effective method of birth control throughout treatment with narsoplimab and for
at least 12 weeks after the last dose of the drug, such birth-control method defined as
one that results in a low failure rate (i.e., less than 1% per year) when used consistently
and correctly, such as implants, injectables, combined oral contraceptives, some
intrauterine devices, sexual abstinence (abstinence is acceptable when it is in line with
the patient’s medical condition or preferred and usual lifestyle and is defined as
complete abstinence of sexual intercourse, not periodic abstinence or withdrawal), or
vasectomized partner.
6. Patient must have TA-TMA defined as having thrombocytopenia and evidence of
microangiopathic hemolytic anemia.

5.1.2.1. EAP Subgroup 1 (Castelli et. al.)
The review team identified an article by the Castelli group (Castelli et al., 2024), which describes
a retrospective study that took place between January 2018 and August 2023 in Italy. This
retrospective study included 20 patients diagnosed with high-risk TA-TMA who received
narsoplimab under the EAP compassionate use program and for whom patient level data was
available. One patient was included in both Study TMA-001 and this retrospective study. For
analysis of the BLA, this patient was analyzed with Study TMA-001 only. This group of patients,
referred to as EAP Subgroup 1, comprises 19 patients (13 adult and 6 were pediatric).
The retrospective Castelli study (EAP Subgroup 1) defined high-risk as “the presence of at least
one of the following poor prognostic features: (1) rUPCR (urine protein creatinine ratio) ≥ 1
mg/mg; (2) the presence of organ dysfunction; (3) LDH levels exceeding two times the ULN; (4)
concurrent grade II–IV acute graft-versus-host disease (GvHD); (5) concurrent infections
(bacterial or viral)”. This is slightly different than the definition in study TMA-001, in which highrisk was defined as having at least one of the following (1) LDH ≥ 2x upper limit of normal (ULN),
(2) presence of infection, (3) acute GvHD Grade II-IV, and (4) evidence of organ dysfunction.
However, a patient with the presence of any one of these features can be considered as having
high-risk TA-TMA per Schoettler et al.
For the EAP Subgroup 1, the Applicant provided TMA response results based on two criteria: 1)
TMA response using the TMA-001 study response criteria and 2) TMA response as determined
by Castelli investigators’ opinion regarding whether the patient's TMA signs/symptoms
improved/resolved following narsoplimab treatment. The response assessments were identical
for the two criteria. For this review, we report the results from analysis based on the criteria
used in TMA-001.

EAP Subgroup 2

The Applicant also submitted high level data for all 221 patients treated in the EAP as of March

5.1.3. Kyoto Stem Cell Transplantation Group (KSCTG) registry
With the resubmission, the Sponsor submitted new clinical information which included data
from the Kyoto Stem Cell Transplantation Group (KSCTG) registry intended to serve as an
external control dataset for Study TMA-001 for survival endpoints. The KSCTG is a multicenter
collaboration of transplantation centers across Japan which collects detailed clinical
information on patients undergoing allogeneic and autologous hematopoietic stem cell
transplantation (HSCT). The registry includes patient demographics, disease characteristics,
treatment details, and key outcomes such as graft-versus-host disease, relapse, and survival.
The KSCTG registry draws its foundational data from Japan's national Transplant Registry
Unified Management Program (TRUMP), which systematically collects standardized HSCT data
across Japan and captures over 1,000 discrete data elements related to clinical outcomes.
The external control dataset of patients with transplantation-associated thrombotic
microangiopathy (TA-TMA) was derived through the following systematic process. KSCTG
investigators accessed TRUMP data for 2,915 adult patients who received allogeneic HSCT
between January 2000 and September 2016 at 17 KSCTG-affiliated institutions. After applying
predefined exclusion criteria, the cohort was refined to 2,425 patients, of whom 134 were
flagged by treating physicians as having TA-TMA. Through a secondary survey process
involving standardized questionnaires sent to treating physicians and subsequent medical
record verification, 121 patients were confirmed to have documented evidence of TA-TMA
diagnosis in their medical records and met the predefined diagnostic criteria established by
KSCTG investigators, forming the final external control cohort for the comparative analysis.
The data flow for the efficacy dataset is shown in

5.1.4. Applicant’s Summary Report of Literature to Provide External
Control for Response for Study TMA-001
In the initial BLA submission, the Applicant proposed a benchmark response rate of 15% in
patients with TA-TMA who did not receive narsoplimab to be used as an external control for the
primary endpoint. This was based on the Applicant’s discussions with clinical experts on TATMA. The Applicant’s rationale was not acceptable, and FDA requested additional data to justify
this 15% response rate to serve as a reference rate for Study TMA-001.
The Applicant submitted their response, which included a summary report1 based on a
literature search. The retrospective study population included on HSCT-TMA patients age ≥18
who had identifiable patient-level data and had data including (i) a response in at least one of
the primary response variables used in the TMA-001 study (platelet count, lactate
dehydrogenase [LDH], organ function or transfusions) or (ii) report of response AND at least 1
risk factor present in the narsoplimab pivotal trial patients (i.e., GVHD, neurological
dysfunction, infection, pulmonary dysfunction, renal dysfunction, gastrointestinal dysfunction,
relapse or persistence of underlying malignancy). Excluded from the study were patients who
had received eculizumab and/or defibrotide therapy. The report included a description of the
demographic and baseline reported risk factors e.g., GVHD, renal dysfunction, neurological
dysfunction, along with analysis of the literature-reported frequency of TA-TMA improvement/
resolution. This was provided for all patients who met the eligibility criteria ((AP group) and for
a subset of patients who had not received any pharmacological treatment (nonpharmacologically treated patients or NPTP group). The report included 35 studies, 25 of which
included patients (N=149) in the NPTP group.
Because the literature reports a variety of response criteria, which are not as rigorous as the
response endpoint used in Study TMA-001, the Applicant analyzed the criteria according to a
definition of stringency i.e., less stringent or equally stringent compared to the TMA-001
criteria, or incomplete. A meta-analysis was performed using a random-effect logistic
regression model with the selected studies as the random effect to estimate the overall
response rate and the corresponding 95% CI.
The review team identified several significant limitations to the Applicant’s literature-based
review, including unknown validity and accuracy of data, potential measurement errors
associated with endpoints (response rates), propensity score analysis performed on limited
confounding variables, timing of treatment visits not standardized across different publications,
study conducted using convenience samples and likely selection bias affecting generalizability
of the results. Therefore, during the first submission, the review team determined the
literature-based review could not be considered sufficient to estimate response rate in an
untreated group.
1 Report on Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplant-associated Thrombotic Microangiopathy Literature review: Data

Extraction, Analysis, and Interpretation; Omeros Corporation, BLA 761152, April 23, 2021.

For the resubmission, the Applicant conducted a new literature review covering publications
from 2000-2020 to identify patients with TA-TMA with individual patient-level data and
response outcomes, along with an updated search extending from January 2021 to August
2025. However, this new search did not identify any additional studies with necessary patientlevel data on TMA response.
For review of this resubmission, the Division re-evaluated the data from the literature and a
reasonable historic external control was identified for use as a comparator for response
endpoint for Study TMA-001 and EAP Subgroup 1. This is discussed in Section 4.2.1.

5.2. Analysis of Primary Efficacy Endpoint: TMA Response
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of TA-TMA patients who responded to
narsoplimab treatment. Treatment response was defined as both an improvement in TMAassociated laboratory markers (Requirement 1): platelet count and LDH, as well as
improvement in clinical status (Requirement 2): either improvement in organ function (renal,
pulmonary, GI or neurological) at any time point post-baseline during the study or freedom of
transfusion defined as no transfusions (RBC, platelet) for at least 4 weeks from the last
transfusion except for patients who died within 4 weeks of the last transfusion. Improvement in
organ function was determined by a clinical review of the data by the local site investigators.
See the original clinical review in DARRTS dated October 15, 2021, for a detailed description of
the study design and statistical analysis plan.
5.2.1.

Literature Review for External Control for Response Rate

To provide an external historic control, the Applicant performed a literature-based study, is
described in Section 5.1.4. As previously noted, the Applicant performed analysis on all patients
(AP) group and for a subgroup of non-pharmacologically treated patients (NTPT), and within
each of these groups an analysis based on comparability to the TMA-001 response criteria (i.e.,
all criteria, less stringent, equally stringent). These results are provided in Table 19 of the
original integrated review for BLA 761152. To remove potential effect of other pharmacological
treatments, the Division considered the NPTP group to most closely match the enrolled
population in TMA-001.
Using a liberal response definition that identified patients as responders if they met any of the
TMA-001 response criteria (rather than requiring improvement in both laboratory markers plus
clinical benefit), the response rate in the non-pharmacologically treated patient (NPTP) group
was 23.3% (95% CI: 15.1, 34.2). Although an analysis was performed for the subgroup of
studies that used a response criterion that was equally stringent as that used in the TMA-001
study, the calculated response rate was higher than in the less stringent group, which would
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

26

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
not be expected. This is most likely due to chance as this subgroup (NPTP-equally stringent)
included only 6 studies with a total of 56 patients. The review team chose to use the response
rate calculated for the NPTP-all studies, which includes 25 studies and 149 patients, for the
comparison historic external control response rate, noting this definition likely overestimates
the response rate compared to what would be obtained using the rigorous study criteria.
5.2.2. Study TMA-001
During the initial review of the application, there were 5 patients whom the Applicant
considered responders but whom the review team adjudicated as non-responders. The review
team re-analyzed the data for these 5 patients, incorporating additional information for each
patient provided by the Applicant in the resubmission. Our original assessment and reassessment for each of these patients is detailed in Appendix 16.6. The re-assessment
incorporated key concepts from a proposed consensus diagnostic criteria and prognostic
criteria for TA-TMA published since the initial review (Schoettler et al., 2023). The article
defines a group of high-risk TA-TMA patients based on the presence of several poor prognostic
factors. All patients (N=28) in Study TMA-001 met at least one of the high-risk criteria for TATMA per the proposed consensus diagnostic criteria. See Appendix 14.6 for summary of
proposed definition for high-risk TA-TMA.
After careful review, the review team amended its original assessment of response for each of
these patients to “responder”. Reasons include:
• Response criteria 1 requirement of demonstration of improvement for both
platelets and LDH excluded patients who had not had platelet engraftment as
potential responders. For patients who did not have patient engraftment, only LDH
response was required to satisfy response criteria 1.
• The medical complexity of these patients, including use of agents that potentially
confounded evidence of response, necessitates closer review of exposure timelines
for narsoplimab and concurrent agents. In the re-assessment, the review team
considered for each case, the likelihood of the concurrent drug contributing to the
observed response rather than applying an assumption of contribution.
The Division therefore concludes that response was achieved in 17/28 in Study TMA-001
yielding a response rate of 61% with a 95% CI of (40.6, 78.5), the same results the Applicant
reported.
5.2.3.

EAP Subgroup 1

TMA Response was assessed in the EAP 1 subset using the same definition as used in Study
TMA-001: Improvement in both of two laboratory TMA markers (LDH and platelet counts) and
either improvement in organ function or independence from transfusions.
Improvement in platelet count was defined as:
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

27

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
• For baseline platelet count ≤ 20,000/μL: (i) ≥ 3-fold increase in platelet count, (ii) postbaseline platelet count > 30,000/μL, and (iii) receipt of no platelet transfusions within 2
days prior to the platelet count assessment.
• For baseline platelet count > 20,000/μL: (i) ≥ 50% increase in platelet count, (ii) platelet
count > 75,000/μL, and (iii) receipt of no platelet transfusions within 2 days prior to the
platelet count assessment.
Improvement in LDH was defined as LDH < 1.5x ULN.
Results for the primary response endpoint overall and by component for the EAP Subgroup 1
are summarized in Table 2. The overall response rate in the EAP Subgroup 1 was 68% (13/19)
with a 95% CI of (43.4, 87.4). The point estimates were similar in adults (N=13) with a 69% response
rate (95% CI: 38.6, 90.9) and in pediatric (< 17 years of age) patients (N=6) with a 67% response rate
(95% CI: 22.3, 95.7).

5.2.4.2. Response Analysis
In Study TMA-001, 17 of 28 patients (61%; 95% CI: 40.6, 78.5) achieved TMA response. The
response in EAP Subgroup 1 was 68% (13/19), with 95% CI (43.4, 87.4). When combining adult
patients from both studies, 26 of 41 adult patients (63%; 95% CI: 46.9, 77.9) had a response,
and the overall combined analysis of all 47 patients showed a TMA response rate of 64% (95%
CI: 48.5, 77.3). Detailed results for each population and the pooled total are presented in Table
5.

The response rates from Study TMA-001 (TA-TMA Study) and the response rates for the adult
patients in the EAP subgroup 1 are consistent. Due to differences in the dosing of narsoplimab,
difference in use of concomitant therapies such as CNIs, between the two groups, no pooled
data columns are included in the label.
The Applicant conducted sensitivity analyses using Bayesian methods in response to the
Agency's information request (source: Applicant's response dated August 21, 2025).
Their statistical analyses yielded posterior mean estimates for response rates with
corresponding 95% credible intervals (CrI) as follows:
1) TMA-001 + EAP subgroup 1 (N=47) showed a 64% response rate (95% CrI: 50% to 77%);
2) TMA-001 + adult EAP subset (N=41) showed a 63% response rate (95% CrI: 47% to 77%).
In Study TMA-001 and in EAP Subgroup 1, the point estimate and the entire 95% CrI exceeded
the conservative literature-based threshold, with posterior probabilities greater than 0.9999. In
the EAP Subgroup 1, this was true for both the adult only population and the population
overall.
5.2.5.

Conclusions Efficacy: Response Endpoint

During this review, after re-adjudication of the 5 patients whom the review team disagreed
with the Applicant’s assessment as responders during the review of the initial cycle, the Agency
verified the Applicant’s primary endpoint response rate of 61% (17/28) with a 95% CI of (40.6 to
78.5) from Study TMA-001.
Although the Applicant did not identify any new literature sources in this resubmission, after
additional consideration, the review team concluded that calculating a response rate based on
all patients in the NPTP group, including those from studies with less stringent response criteria
than Study TMA-001, would likely overestimate an historic response rate, and therefore could
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

31

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
be considered as a benchmark for study TMA-001 to be measured against. The TMA-001
response rate of 61% is higher than the selected literature based historic control response rate
23.3% (15.1, 34.2) with non-overlapping confidence intervals. The Division concludes that there
is a treatment effect of narsoplimab in patients with TA-TMA based on response rate.
The response rate in the EAP Subgroup 1 (68% [95% CI: 43.4, 87.4]) is consistent with that
observed in Study TMA-001 and is internally consistent for pediatric and adult subgroups.

5.3. Survival Endpoints
Overall survival (OS) was defined as the time from first dose of narsoplimab to death. Patients
who were alive at the time of analysis were censored at the date of last follow-up. KaplanMeier (KM) estimates were used to summarize OS for the overall population, as well as for
adult and pediatric subgroups. Survival probabilities at 100 days and 1 year were estimated
with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) using the log-log transformation.
5.3.1. Statistical Methods for Evaluating Survival, Study TMA-001 and
EAP Subgroups
Overall survival was defined as the time from a specified index date to death, with multiple
index dates considered, including time of first dose of narsoplimab, time of TMA diagnosis, and
time of HSCT. Survival rates at day 100 and 1 year were estimated using the Kaplan–Meier
method and the corresponding 95% confidence intervals were calculated using the log–log
transformation. Results are presented for all considered index dates.
5.3.2. Study TMA-001
For the full analysis population in Study TMA-001 (N=28), the estimated survival probability
was 73.4% (95% CI: 52.2, 86.4) at day 100 and 41.0% (95% CI: 21.9, 59.2) at 1 year from the
date of TMA diagnosis.
Time to event endpoints are difficult to interpret in single-arm trials as lack of a concurrent
control group introduces bias, making it hard to isolate the survival effect. Therefore, the
survival results from Study TMA-001 are descriptive and were not used to make regulatory
decision regarding the treatment effect of narsoplimab on survival.
5.3.3. EAP Subgroup 1
•

Overall population (N=19): The estimated survival probability was 73.7% (95% CI: 47.9,
88.1) at 100 days and 73.7% (95% CI: 47.9, 88.1) at 1 year.

Several limitations should be considered when interpreting the survival results in the EAP
datasets: the EAP was an uncontrolled observational study with voluntary data reporting,
leading to substantial missing data (e.g., risk factors for TA-TMA), while TMA-001 followed
rigorous standards; the EAP included heterogeneous populations (both allogeneic and
autologous transplants, including patients with prior treatment failures) in different countries
with variable clinical practices, whereas Study TMA-001 enrolled only patients with allogeneic
HSCT; treatment administration was flexible in the EAP with physician-guided dosing compared
to TMA-001's standardized protocol; and Study TMA-001 had enrollment period 2015-2019,

5.3.6. External Control for Survival Endpoints, Kyoto Stem Cell
Transplantation Group (KSCTG) registry
For an overview of the KSCTG registry and identification of patients for inclusion in the external
control population, see Section 4.3.6
5.3.6.1.

Survival Analysis Plan

The statistical analysis for external control comparison focused on the following:
• comparisons between patients from the pooled dataset of TMA-001 and EAP subset
(N=47) to KSTCG confirmed TMA patients (N=121)
• comparisons between patients from TMA-001 (N=28) and KSTCG confirmed TMA
patients (N=121)
Propensity score–based methods were used to adjust for potential imbalances between
cohorts. The propensity score was estimated using logistic regression incorporating the
following covariates:
1) Age (continuous variable)
2) Sex (binary: 0 = female, 1 = male)
3) Acute GVHD Grade 2-4 (binary: 0 = absent, 1 = present)
4) Infection (binary: 0 = absent, 1 = present)
5) Neurological dysfunction (binary: 0 = absent, 1 = present)
6) Pulmonary dysfunction (binary: 0 = absent, 1 = present)
7) Renal dysfunction (binary: 0 = absent, 1 = present)
8) LDH ≥ 2 times ULN (binary: 0 = no, 1 = yes)
9) Donor mismatch (binary: 0 = matched, 1 = mismatched)
10) Donor relationship (binary: 0 = unrelated, 1 = related)
11) Time from HSCT to TMA diagnosis (discrete variable, days)
• In the Applicant’s analysis, the propensity score model only contained the first eight
covariates listed above, where covariates 3 – 8 are the risk factors for high-risk TMA
(Schoettler et al., 2023). In the Agency’s analysis, three additional covariates were
added in the propensity score model. Donor mismatch and donor relationship were
included given their potential association with TA-TMA development. For time from
HSCT to TA-TMA diagnosis, substantial differences were observed between cohorts,
with treated patients having considerably later onset (TMA-001: mean 120.8 ± 108.8
days; EAP Subgroup 1: mean 141.6 ± 120.0 days) compared to KSCTG registry controls
(mean 51.5 ± 58.7 days). These differences may indicate major differences such as
disease patterns, severity, or underlying mechanisms. Therefore, time to TA-TMA
diagnosis was included as a covariate to address this imbalance.

CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

35

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
The Applicant utilized inverse probability of treatment weighting (IPTW) as the primary
analytical approach. During the review, extreme propensity score values (approaching 0 and 1)
were observed, which can lead to unstable weight estimates and increased variance. To address
this concern and evaluate the robustness of treatment effect estimates, several sensitivity
analyses were conducted:
• Propensity score matching (PSM): Both 1:1 and 1:2 matching strategies were
implemented, with and without caliper restrictions.
• IPTW with truncation: Weights were truncated at the 1st and 99th percentiles (1%
truncation) and at the 5th and 95th percentiles (5% truncation) to reduce the influence
of extreme weights.
• Stabilized IPTW: Stabilized weights were calculated to improve the precision of effect
estimates while maintaining the benefits of weighting.
These complementary approaches were implemented to evaluate the robustness of the
treatment effect estimates and to mitigate bias due to residual covariate imbalance. Survival
outcomes were analyzed using Cox proportional hazards regression on the matched dataset for
PSM, and weighted Cox regression incorporating the IPW weights for the weighting analyses.
Hazard ratios with corresponding 95% confidence intervals and p-values were reported, and
Kaplan–Meier plots were generated to visually display survival distributions between groups.
Overall survival was measured in days from the index date; patients alive at the time of analysis
were censored at the last follow-up. For patients from study TMA-001 and EAP Subgroup 1
(Castelli et al.), the index date was defined as the first day of narsoplimab treatment. For
patients in the KSCTG registry with TA-TMA, a date of first dose of narsoplimab was not
applicable. The index date was therefore imputed as the date of TMA diagnosis plus the median
time from TMA diagnosis to first dose of narsoplimab observed in the study TMA-001.
Additional imputation methods were also applied to ensure the robustness of the final results.
5.3.6.2. Overall Survival Comparison TMA-001 and KTSCG
Results
This section presents the comparison of overall survival between patients from TMA-001
(N=28) and KSTCG confirmed TMA patients (N=121). The distributions of propensity scores are
shown in Figure 2Error! Reference source not found.. There was low-to-moderate overlap
overall, and a lack of common support was observed in parts of the propensity score
distribution. Additionally, some propensity scores were at the extremes, which may indicate a
potential violation of the positivity assumption. Similar findings were observed in a propensity
analysis performed comparing OS between patients in the pooled TMA-001 and EAP Subgroup
1 dataset and KTSCG and can be found in Appendix, Section .

Source: FDA analysis

Standardized mean differences (SMDs) were used to assess covariate balance between
groups. The SMD quantifies differences in means or proportions between the treated and
external control groups relative to the pooled standard deviation. An absolute SMD less than
0.1 is generally considered to indicate adequate balance. Covariate balance plot for the IPTWbased methods is shown below in
Figure 3. While weighting improved balance for many covariates, a number of covariates still
had absolute SMDs above the commonly used threshold of 0.1, such as time from HSCT to TMA
diagnosis, neurological dysfunction, LDH ≥ 2 times ULN and infection, indicating some residual
imbalance. These remaining imbalances should be considered when interpreting the adjusted
results.

Registry)

and can result in exclusion of some patients from the treatment group, which results in fewer
matched pairs available for analysis.

Comparability Issues
There are several limitations with the registry identified by the clinical team, statistical team
and the Division of Epidemiology 1 (DEP1) that limit the internal and external validity of the
comparative analysis and limits the use of the KSTG registry. Key points from the DEPI-1
consult and the clinical and statistical team are summarized below. A detailed review from
DEPI-1 can be found in DARRTS, dated December 15, 2025.
Lack of Patient Comparability
One of the major concerns for the external control study (KSTG registry) is the lack of
comparability between the single-arm trial and external control populations, which introduces
confounding, selection, and survival bias that may compromise the internal validity of the
results.
Temporal Disparities: A significant temporal gap exists between the treatment and external
control groups, with the KSCTG control data spanning 2000–2016 while the narsoplimabtreated patients were enrolled from 2015 onward. This temporal disparity is clinically significant
because transplant outcomes have improved substantially over this period due to advances in
supportive care and infection management. This secular trend is evident within the Applicant’s
external control data, which demonstrated that the proportion of patients surviving beyond
one year increased from 15% (7/48) of KSCTG registry patients in the 2000–2009 period to 25%
(18/73) in the 2010–2016 period. This temporal improvement represents a major uncontrolled
confounder that biases the results in favor of the narsoplimab group.
• Geographic Differences: The narsoplimab-treated cohorts comprise patients from Study
TMA-001, a global study and the EAP Subgroups 1 are derived from patients primarily in
Italy (Castelli et al.), while the OS control group consists solely of Japanese registry patients.
These geographical variations may introduce potential confounding due to differences in
intrinsic or extrinsic factors (ICH E17), e.g. patient demographics, genetic backgrounds,

CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

41

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
clinical practices, standard of care, and healthcare systems, thereby limiting the applicability
of findings to the US patient population.
• Small Sample Size: The limited number of patients from Study TMA-001 with only 28
patients could limit the generalizability of the findings to a broader TA-TMA population .
• Retrospective Analysis: Results of study TMA-001 were already available before the
consideration of using the KSCTG registry data as an external control.
• Selection Bias and Survival Bias: The single-arm trial’s eligibility criteria created a lefttruncation effect by requiring patients to survive at least 21-30 days post-transplant to meet
diagnostic criteria. This requirement introduced survival bias by systematically excluding
patients with early-onset, rapidly progressive disease who would typically be included in a
real-world registry. Furthermore, the trial-excluded patients with a life expectancy of less
than three months or with certain cardiovascular or hepatic dysfunctions, thereby selecting
for a healthier patient population compared to the unselected registry cohort. These
selection criteria collectively bias the effect estimate away from the null hypothesis and in
favor of the narsoplimab group.
• Time from Transplant to TMA Diagnosis: Earlier onset of TMA, which is associated with
worse prognosis (Van Benschoten V et al., 2022), was more common in the KSCTG registry.
The trial patients excluded those who developed TMA within three weeks following HSCT,
while the KSCTG registry include such patients. In the KSCTG registry, 26% (32/121)
patients had TMA onset within three weeks, with 6% (2/32) surviving beyond one year,
compared to 26% (23/89) among patients with TMA onset beyond three weeks.
• Missing Information: Some patient-level data are missing from the registry, such as highrisk factors for TA-TMA, and components to determine TMA response.
• Substantial Unmeasured and Residual Confounding: The Applicant utilized a propensity
score model to account for potential confounding. However, several important prognostic
factors that showed baseline imbalance between the narsoplimab-treated patients and
KSCTG controls were excluded from the model. This exclusion led to significant unmeasured
confounding that was likely to bias the results in favor of narsoplimab.
• Propensity Score (PS) Considerations: The propensity scores demonstrated only low to
moderate overlap between the narsoplimab-treated and external control cohorts which
could compromise the validity of the comparative analysis. Areas of limited common
support and the presence of extreme propensity score values (near 0 or 1) also suggest
potential challenges in achieving adequate matching and indicate that some patients may
lack comparable counterparts in the opposite group,
• Unmeasured Confounding: Registry data may lack detailed information on important
prognostic factors (e.g., patients’ baseline status, such as LDH and transfusion requirement)
that are systematically collected in clinical studies. Additionally, missing covariate
information may introduce unmeasured confounding effect. Although the Applicant utilized
the E-values to demonstrate that the impact of unmeasurable confounding is not a concern,
given the other concerns, such as single registry and selection bias, the exact impact of
unmeasurable confounding may not be ruled out.
• HLA Mismatch: The proportion of HLA mismatched patients was substantially higher in the
KSCTG registry (77%, 93/121) as compared to the trial patients (29%, 8/28). Our analysis of
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

42

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
the KSCTG data revealed that a much lower proportion of HLA-mismatched patients
survived beyond one year (17%, 16/93) compared to matched patients (32%, 9/28).
• Stem Cell Source: The use of umbilical cord blood (UCB), which is associated with poorer
outcomes (Lim YJ et al., 2022), was far more common in the KSCTG registry (39%, 47/121)
than in the narsoplimab groups (2-7%).
• Performance Status: In the KSCTG registry, 24% (29/121) of patients had a poor
performance score (≥2) with 0% surviving beyond one year, compared to a 29% (25/85) of
those with better performance status surviving beyond one year.
Additional Methodological and Analytical Concerns
Several technical aspects of the analysis further impact the study’s validity and may introduce
additional uncertainty.
• Multiple Imputation of Index Date: The study applied simple random sampling approach to
impute the index date for KSCTG patients based on the distribution of time interval
between HSCT and TMA onset in narsoplimab-treated patients. This indeed assumes that
the “latent treatment day” for KSCTG registry patients are “missing” completely at random.
This assumption is unlikely to hold given the systematic differences in disease severity,
clinical presentation, and healthcare settings between the trial, the EAP, and the registry,
where differences in disease severity, patient demographics, healthcare access, clinical
presentation may impact treatment timing.
• Propensity Score Methodology: In the original submission, the Applicant failed to provide
adequate documentation of the propensity score model diagnostics, including assessments
of model fit, weight distributions, and covariate overlap. The effectiveness of weight
truncation in such a small sample size is also uncertain.
• Integration of Heterogeneous Treatment Data: Analysis 2 and 3 of the supplemental study
integrated narsoplimab treatment data from all 28 patients in the pivotal trial and all 49
patients in the EAP. However, pooling data from these significantly different sources - a
clinical trial versus a real-world setting - without first evaluating for heterogeneity is
methodologically inappropriate. This approach risks obscuring the distinct characteristics
and outcomes of each patient population.
Although nominal statistical significances were observed in various analyses via different
methods, the results should be interpreted with caution considering the aforementioned
limitations and comparability issues.
5.3.7. Conclusion on Efficacy Overall Survival
In the absence of a reliable control arm, there is limited interpretability of the of the time-toevents survival endpoints from Study TMA-001. This remains true even with additional data
from EAP Subgroups. Any analysis of EAP subgroup 2 is limited by lack of patient level data,
missing data, heterogenous population, and temporal disparity rendering the survival data
uninterpretable.
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

43

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Based on description of the limitations described in the section above, the selection of KSCTG
as an external control is problematic and the results are inconclusive. In addition, the registrybased comparator for survival benchmark may introduce the potential for significant potential
bias. Therefore, the KSTG registry as a comparator and survival data from the EAP and TMA-001
was not used for primary evidence for regulatory decision making.
The Day 100 survival (from first dose of narsoplimab) for patients from the EAP Subgroup 1 with
patient level data and the Day 100 survival for the overall EAP group indicated directional
consistency with the efficacy results from Study TMA-001 (Day 100 survival from first dose of
narsoplimab). However, due to limitations with interpretation of single-arm survival data, the
results can be considered only descriptive and exploratory. Further, as stated in the guideline
from American Society for Transplantation and Cellular Therapy, published this year, “Overall
survival from TA-TMA diagnosis alone is an insufficient endpoint to understand the impact of
TA-TMA directed therapies as patients with TA-TMA may die of multiple etiologies including
complications of GVHD, infections, or relapsed disease even after TA-TMA has resolved.”
(Schoettler et al., 2025).

5.4. Pediatric Data
The sources of pediatric data for the use of narsoplimab is derived from the 6 patients (age
range 5-15) described in EAP 1 group (Castelli et al). In terms of safety, FDA assessed 97 (6 from
EAP 1 and 91 from EAP Subgroup 2) pediatric patients (defined as < 17 years of age) from the
EAP.
In EAP 1 (Castelli et al) the baseline patient characteristics for the pediatric patients
demonstrate that 100% of pediatric patients had an elevated LDH compared to 54% of adults.
Both adult and pediatric patients had similar Grade II-IV acute GvHD. The percentage of renal,
pulmonary and neurological dysfunction was similar in pediatric and adult patients. The rate of
infection in pediatric patients was 100%, and 62% in adults. See Table 3 which presents
demographics and baseline disease from 19 patients from EAP 1. The 6 pediatric patients had
one criterion that met the high-risk TMA definition as defined by Schoettler et al.
In the EAP 1, pediatric patients had a longer exposure to narsoplimab and, therefore, likely a
higher number of doses which was by investigator discretion (see Table 9. Duration of
Exposure, Narsoplimab EAP). The Applicant confirms that the longer pediatric treatment
duration was due to investigator-directed continuation of therapy after TA-TMA resolution,
influenced by relapse prevention considerations.
Efficacy
TMA Response: In the EAP 1 subset (Castelli et al., 2023), 4 of 6 pediatric patients (66.7%, 95%
CI: 22.3, 95.7) responded to narsoplimab treatment (see 5. Primary Endpoint, Response Rate,
Pooled Analysis). The lower bound of 22.3% reflects the limitation of small sample size (N=6).
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

44

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
The response rate is consistent with the response rate observed in adults, in a high -risk
population.
Survival rate at 100 days from first dose of narsoplimab was 69.9% (95% CI: 58.7, 78.7) and at 1
year, 51% (95% CI: 36.6, 63.6) for pediatric patients (N=97) in the EAP2 and the 6 pediatric
subjects from EAP 1 (See Table 6. The mortality is high in patients with TA-TMA and 6-month
overall survival rate is estimated to be approximately 18% in pediatric population (Jodele et al.,
2024). In the pediatric population, Jodele et al. has reported that in high-risk pediatric patients
with TA-TMA that did not receive complement inhibitors, OS at 1 year was 16.7% (Jodele et al.,
2024). For pediatric patients, 1 year OS in TA-TMA after diagnosis is reported to be 1% in
patients treated with therapeutic plasma exchange (Yang et al., 2023). The descriptive survival
findings in the pediatric subgroup of EAP 2 and EAP 1 suggest a potential treatment effect,
however no definitive conclusions regarding survival can be derived given limitations with
survival data from the EAP datasets.
Safety
In the EAP, 97 pediatric patients (defined as < 16 years of age) were exposed to narsoplimab, in
the EAP Subgroup 2 and EAP Subgroup 1. The youngest patient exposed to narsoplimab was
nine months old. Pediatric patients with TA-TMA being treated with narsoplimab, had 35 (42.2
%) SAEs (see Table 13 in the Appendix). One pediatric patient died. Overall, the safety profile of
narsoplimab in pediatric patients with TA-TMA was consistent with the adult patients with TATMA exposed to narsoplimab. Labeling should include Warnings and Precautions for serious
infections. As with adults, the majority of SAEs were infectious in pediatric patients. The longterm safety of narsoplimab in pediatric patients with TA-TMA will continue to be reviewed
through a PMR.
Conclusion
Pediatric and adult TA-TMA share similar underlying pathophysiology with similar
characteristics such as endothelial damage and complement activation after HSCT with similar
pattern of organ involvement, microangiopathic anemia.
There are differences in pediatric and adult patients in terms of the underlying diseases that
necessitated HSCT, where adults usually undergo transplantation for malignant hematologic
diseases and pediatric patients more frequently undergo transplantation for non-malignant
diseases. Adults are often more likely to have comorbid conditions whereas pediatric patients
often do not have baseline comorbidities, however the approach to treatment is similar for
pediatric and adult patients with TA-TMA. The course of the disease is similar although there
can be differences in baseline comorbidities, underlying disease and concomitant complications
such as GVHD which can also impact outcomes.
In the 6 pediatric patients with subject level data available, the baseline disease characteristics
and TMA response rates were consistent with what was observed in the adult population. Of
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

45

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
the 97 pediatric patients in the EAP, 89 were 2 years and older and there were no new safety
signals were observed. Given the lack of available therapy for this rare disease with high
mortality rate and the similar pathophysiology between adults and pediatric patients
reasonable to consider partial extrapolation of efficacy and safety from the adult population to
support the pediatric indication in pediatric patients age ≥ 2 with TA-TMA.

5.5.Overall Conclusions on Benefit
In Study TMA-001, there is a benefit of estimated TMA response observed in 17 of 28 patients
(61%; 95% CI: 40.6, 78.5). No benefit on survival could be confirmed.

6. Safety Update
A thorough evaluation of safety of narsoplimab in patients with TA-TMA based on data from
TMA-001 was performed as part of the review of the original BLA (see finalized review in
DARRTS, loaded October 15, 2021). Serious and life-threatening infections have occurred in
patients treated with narsoplimab and were reported in 36% (10/28) patients with TA-TMA in
clinical trials in patients receiving narsoplimab. These included sepsis, viral infections,
pneumonia, bacteremia, fungal infections, gastroenteritis, respiratory tract infections and
urosepsis. The most common adverse reactions (incidence >20%) are viral infections, sepsis,
hemorrhage, diarrhea, vomiting, nausea, neutropenia, pyrexia, fatigue and hypokalemia.
Additional safety data from the EAP program was submitted with this resubmission. Analysis of
this data did not identify any new or more serious safety signal not already identified the
analysis of safety based on the data from TMA-001. Table 9 provides exposure information for
the EAP population.

Patients with TA-TMA in the EAP received narsoplimab for an average of 2.5 months. The
duration of treatment in the safety database appears adequate to support approval in the
context of this rare, serious disease. TA-TMA is not a chronic process and narsoplimab is
discontinued when a patient achieves all of the response criteria and no longer requires
therapy for TA-TMA. It is noted that the pediatric patients had a longer exposure to
narsoplimab and, therefore likely a higher number of doses which was by investigator
discretion. The Applicant confirms that the longer pediatric treatment duration was due to
investigator-directed continuation of therapy after TA-TMA resolution, influenced by relapse
prevention considerations.
In the EAP, the rate of serious adverse events (SAEs) was 46.2%. Serious adverse reactions
included respiratory failure, multiple organ dysfunction syndrome, and polyserositis. Six (5.9%)
deaths are listed as SAEs.
The most common serious adverse reactions were in the system organ class of ‘Infections and
Infestations’, which is again similar to what was observed the TMA-001 study. Therefore, if the
product were to be approved, serious infections should be included in the ‘Warnings and
Precautions’ section of the USPI. Serious infections should also be further monitored through a
postmarketing requirement.
See Table 12 and Table 13, in the Appendix for the total serious adverse events for the entire
EAP group, and serious adverse events for the pediatric patients from the EAP, respectively.

7. Clinical Pharmacology
Although no new clinical pharmacology data was included in the BLA resubmission, this section
includes a summary of assessment on dose and dosing frequency in adult and pediatric patients
based on the additional clinical experience from the EAP and the need for supplemental dose
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

47

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
following plasma exchange. For clinical pharmacology assessment of the original BLA
submission, refer to the Integrated Review in DARRTS (10/15/2021).
Dose and Dosing Frequency
The proposed narsoplimab dose and dosing regimen is 4 mg/kg, given as a 30-minute IV
infusion (up to 370 mg per infusion) once weekly. This was the dose and dosing regimen
studied in Study OMS721-TMA-001. As noted in the Integrated Review associated with the
original BLA submission (DARRTS date: 10/15/2021), the Applicant selected a dose of 4 mg/kg
based on the pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) results from Study OMS721NHV-002 in healthy patients. This dose was expected to maintain C4d inhibition (marker for
lectin pathway inhibition) greater than 80% for about 168 hours providing the basis for
administering once weekly.
The efficacy and safety of narsoplimab 4 mg/kg administered once weekly has been
demonstrated in OMS721-TMA-001. In the expanded access program (EAP), the Applicant
evaluated a more frequent regimen of 4 mg/kg administered twice weekly due to the high-risk
nature of patients typically enrolled under expanded access and further supported by the
established safety profile from OMS721-TMA-001. Duration of dosing in OMS721-TMA-001 was
4 to 8 weeks. The duration and frequency of treatment administered to patients under the EAP
varied and were determined by the treating physician based on the individual’s clinical
response. Median duration of dosing in the EAP was 5.7 weeks, and median number of doses
was 9. The twice weekly regimen is supported by the efficacy and safety data published in EAP
Subgroup 1 (Castelli et al), reviewed and confirmed by the Agency.
As OMS721-TMA-001 studied a once weekly regimen and the EAP employed a twice weekly
regimen of narsoplimab, an information request was sent to understand the Applicant’s
approach to dosing and administration in the product’s prescribing information. The Applicant
clarified that once weekly administration is appropriate as the starting regimen, however, given
inter-patient variability in disease severity and level of lectin pathway hyperactivation,
increasing the frequency to twice weekly administration may be clinically justified in patients
who do not respond adequately to narsoplimab treatment. Direct evidence showing
improvement in patient response escalating from once weekly to twice weekly regimen is not
available. However, from a PD perspective, a more frequent (e.g., twice weekly) dosing regimen
is expected to maintain higher lectin pathway inhibition throughout the inter-dosing interval
compared to once weekly regimen. It is also reassuring that the safety profile of a twice weekly
regimen employed in the EAP is acceptable ensuring no additional safety risk to patients
escalating to a more frequent regimen. Although the typical duration of treatment for TA-TMA
is relatively shorter (<10 weeks), as patients are frequently monitored, it is still practically
feasible to escalate the dosing frequency based on inadequate treatment response.

CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

48

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Therefore, based on the totality of data, narsoplimab can be initiated once weekly in patients
with TA-TMA. The dosing frequency can be increased to twice weekly if there is no
improvement in TA-TMA signs and symptoms.
While the proposed dose is narsoplimab 4 mg/kg (not to exceed 370 mg), patients in Study
TMA-001 following amendment #10 received a flat dose of 370 mg once weekly if they weighed
≥50 kg. Only patients weighing <50 kg received a body weight adjusted dose of 4 mg/kg once
weekly. Similarly, in the EAP, patients weighing ≥50 kg received 370 mg twice weekly and
patients weighing <50 kg received 4 mg/kg twice weekly. As seen from Figure 11 in the
Integrated Review associated with the original BLA submission, a flat dose of 370 mg results in
higher exposure compared to the body weight adjusted dose of 4 mg/kg. As the safety of 370
mg in patients weighing ≥50 kg is demonstrated, the Agency recommends that the Applicant
proposed dosing instructions i.e., 4 mg/kg (up to a maximum of 370 mg), be revised to
recommend a flat dose of 370 mg in patients weighing ≥50 kg and 4 mg/kg in patients weighing
<50 kg.

No pediatric patients were enrolled in Study OMS721-TMA-001. Clinical experience in pediatric
patients comes from the EAP. Eighty-six pediatric patients with TA-TMA were treated in the EAP
and their age at the time of treatment is shown in Table 10 below.

Out of 97 pediatric patients (<17 years old) treated in EAP Subgroup 2, efficacy and safety data
