Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
No PK or PD data was available from pediatric patients. In response to an information request,
the Applicant simulated narsoplimab exposures in pediatric subjects using the adult population
PK model. Of note, the adult population PK model including a body weight effect on clearance
(exponent factor of 0.726) was reviewed by the Agency with the original BLA submission and
found to be acceptable to characterize the observed adult PK data. The simulated PK data in
pediatric subjects suggested comparable area under the curve (AUC) at steady state for
subjects ≥2 years of age with either once weekly or twice weekly regimen of 4 mg/kg. Based on
the general understanding of monoclonal antibody pharmacokinetics in pediatric population,
the clearance on a per-kilogram basis is usually higher in pediatric patients compared to adults.
As per expectation, the resulting narsoplimab exposures when dosed at the same ‘mg/kg’ dose
in pediatric patients are not higher than what was observed in adults. The adult population PK
model did not include allometric scaling on other PK parameters (e.g., volume of distribution),
therefore the predicted concentration, particularly Cmax, could be under predicted in young
pediatric patients with low body weight and adds to the uncertainty in projecting exposures
without any pediatric PK data in patients <2 years of age. A post-marketing commitment is
(b)
issued to collect PK and PD data in pediatric patients, including in patients < (4)years of age.
In summary, from a safety perspective the dose of 4 mg/kg seems appropriate in pediatric
patients ≥2 years of age. With regard to efficacy, the appropriateness of the dose relies
primarily on the efficacy evaluation from the EAP. As stated earlier, although the starting
regimen is 4 mg/kg once weekly, patients including pediatric patients have the option to
increase the frequency to twice weekly if there is no improvement in TA-TMA signs and
symptoms.
Need for supplemental narsoplimab dose following plasma exchange (PE)
During the review of the original BLA submission, the clinical pharmacology review team
(b) (4)
evaluated
a supplemental narsoplimab dose of 2 mg/kg to be
administered up to 3 times weekly for patients who undergo plasma exchange (PE). Although 5
patients received supplemental doses of narsoplimab because of PE, the effect of PE on
narsoplimab clearance (CL) and its exposure was not investigated in a dedicated clinical study
or did the Applicant provide adequate justification for the selected supplemental dose of
narsoplimab post-PE. Therefore, an information request was sent to the Applicant during the
original BLA review to specifically assess the effect of PE as a time-varying, non-constant
variable on CL via population PK analyses to accurately capture the transient nature of the
intervention. In response, the Applicant conducted a comprehensive reanalysis by
implementing a modified population PK model incorporating PE as a time-varying covariate
across 36 documented PE events with a median duration of 1.45 hours. The Applicant’s analysis
estimated a 37% increase in narsoplimab clearance during PE events, representing a statistically
significant improvement in model fit compared to the original model. The Applicant also
showed that because of the transient nature of the intervention, the effect of PE on
narsoplimab exposure was negligible and concluded that a supplemental dose following PE was
not required. The Agency’s review of the Applicant’s analysis showed that the impact of PE on
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

50

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
narsoplimab CL is on an average 47% but with a wide 95% CI ranging from 24% to 124%. To
improve the precision on the CL estimate, the Agency included a comment in the Complete
Response Letter (CRL) to conduct a dedicated clinical study to methodically assess the effect of
PE on narsoplimab clearance by collecting rich PK samples pre- and post-PE.
In the current BLA resubmission, no additional data to understand the effect of PE on
narsoplimab CL/exposure was submitted. The EAP protocol also did not specify administration
of supplemental doses following PE. Although any new data was not generated, the clinical
pharmacology review team concludes that there may not be a need for a supplemental PE dose
because (i) the duration of PE is short, (ii) despite limitations the transient nature of the
intervention had negligible effect on narsoplimab exposures, (iii) supplemental doses were not
administered in the EAP, and (iv) the current dosage and administration instructions in the label
provides the option to increase the dosing frequency to twice weekly making the utility of a
supplemental PE dose limited. Due to these considerations, a supplemental narsoplimab dose
following PE is not required.

8. Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC)
Key points from the CMC consult review are briefly summarized here, the detailed, finalized
primary review memo is in Panorama, dated 10/30/2025, and the quality executive summary
can be found in DARRTS, dated 11/26/2025.
The CMC reviewer has concluded that the supported preparation and administration conditions
are D5W saline administered with PVC contact materials (infusion bag and line) with a
polyethersulfone (PES) in-line filter. Further, for pediatric patient with less than or equal to 10
kg (the total dose is 40 mg, around 2 years old) should be diluted to 0.8 mg/mL D5W by using
polypropylene syringe and syringe pump since the dosing is small and maximal volume
can be used as 50mL. The use of syringe pumps is also supported based on the stability
data and dose preparation data (Table 6) in the IR response (SDN 103, received on
October 1, 2025). For pediatric patients with more than or equal to 10 kg can be diluted
between 0.8-8 mg/mL in D5W by regulator IV with PVC with in-line filter.
The suitability of preparation and administration conditions for narsoplimab will be further
addressed in PMCs (see Section 8 below).

9. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Pediatric patients were included in the expanded access program (EAP) for narsoplimab. The
indication statement will include pediatric patients down to 2 years old. The youngest patient in
the EAP was 4 months of age. The efficacy and safety profile of the drug appear similar
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

51

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
between adult and pediatric patients. The review team expects the weight-based dosing to
result in similar exposures and should not be higher than exposure in adults. In addition, a
reportable PMR will be issued to establish a registry to characterize the long-term safety of
narsoplimab in at least 50 adult and pediatric patients with TA-TMA.

10.

Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations

An advisory committee meeting was not convened to discuss this application. No issues were
identified that would have benefitted from a public discussion with external experts.

12.

Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)

Based on the available data, a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy is not necessary to ensure
the benefits of narsoplimab in the high-risk TA-TMA population outweigh the risks.
Narsoplimab is part of the complement inhibitor class but has a different mechanism of action
than other approved complement inhibitors. Certain approved complement inhibitors have a
REMS due to their direct involvement in the alternative complement pathway which protects
against encapsulated organism infections. Narsoplimab inhibits mannose binding proteinassociated serine protease 2 (MASP-2) which is an enzyme in the lectin pathway of the
complement system. The other two pathways of the complement system, classical and
alternative, are not affected; and these two pathways are fully functional.
Inhibition of MASP-2 increases the risk of serious infections, but it is not clear how it increases
the risk of infections from encapsulated bacteria. Infections with encapsulated organisms in
patients with MASP-2 deficiency have not been a common finding (Garcia-Laorden et al., 2020).
TA-TMA is an acute, potentially life-threatening complication that typically occurs early after
transplant. It is expected that patients will be treated in an inpatient setting and narsoplimab is
not intended as a chronic or long-term therapy. The standard of care for monitoring and
managing serious infections for post-transplant patients would be similar to the management
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

56

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
of patients who are post-transplant, in general. Post-transplant patients are managed closely by
a multidisciplinary team with hematology/oncology specialists who are knowledgeable about
the risk of infections and management. This patient population is immunocompromised and at
high risk of infection due to the recent transplant. The standard of care for post-transplant
patients includes close monitoring for infections. Treatment with narsoplimab may increase the
risk of serious infections; however, we do not anticipate a need for additional monitoring or
changes to the standard of care. Patients who are candidates for transplant typically receive
education on the risks and are expected to be knowledgeable about the infection risk and
management. We do not anticipate additional mitigation strategies would be necessary beyond
labeling to ensure prescribers are monitoring and managing serious infections
Based on our understanding of the disease state, duration of therapy, and anticipated
prescribers and patient population, our assessment is that a REMS is not necessary to ensure
the benefits of narsoplimab outweigh its risks.

13.

Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments

Postmarketing requirement (PMR)
Narsoplimab is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectinassociated serine protease 2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of
complement. The long-term safety of patients who receive treatment with narsoplimab is
unknown. The goal of a patient registry is to provide continued follow-up of patients to
characterize the long-term safety of narsoplimab in patients with TA-TMA. An important risk
associated with narsoplimab is serious infections. MASP-2 is an effector enzyme in the lectin
complement pathway, which plays an important role in innate immunity. In addition,
autoimmunity has rarely been associated with MASP-2 deficiency, and since narsoplimab
inhibits MASP-2, there is a theoretical risk of autoimmunity. This registry would provide safety
follow-up data and a summary of the major safety findings for this population, including all
serious infections and autoimmune disorders.
4938-1 PMR:
Establish a registry to characterize the long-term safety of narsoplimab-wuug in at least 50
adult and pediatric patients with transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA),
with at least 1 year of follow-up.
The final study report should include a summary of the major safety findings for all patients
including patient level data on narsoplimab-wuug dosing, serious infections, serious treatment
emergent adverse events and laboratory data (i.e., CBC with differential and renal function
tests), autoimmune disorders, concomitant medications, vital status, and cause of death for any
fatalities.

Postmarketing commitments (PMCs)
No PK or PD data is available in pediatric patients who have been exposed to narsoplimab in the
submitted clinical studies or compassionate use program. However, based on the general
understanding of monoclonal antibody pharmacokinetics in pediatric population, the clearance
on a per-kilogram basis is usually higher in pediatric patients compared to adults. Therefore,
narsoplimab, a monoclonal antibody, when dosed at the same mg/kg dose in pediatric patients,
the resulting exposures are not expected to be higher than what was observed in adults, at
least down to 2 years of age. Below 2 years of age, there may be greater uncertainty in
projecting exposures without any pediatric PK data due to maturation of clearance pathways.
Therefore, from a safety perspective, the dose of 4 mg/kg seems appropriate in pediatric
patients ≥2 years of age. However, evidence from a PK study in pediatric patients would further
support pediatric dosing, particularly in patients <2 years of age and provide data to lower the
indicated age to <2 years old, in the future.
4938-2 PMC:
Provide safety, pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) data in pediatric patients <17
years of age treated with the recommended dosing regimen of narsoplimab for transplantassociated thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA).
In-use compatibility studies were not conducted under worst-case conditions. Thus, there is
residual risk that there are real-world conditions which may adversely impact product quality
during routine preparation and administration in the clinic. To address this risk, we request the
Applicant’s commitment to conduct in-use compatibility studies under justified worst-case
conditions (i.e., agitation, light exposure, duration of storage, administration volumes) and
propose mitigations in the labeling, as necessary. The Applicant should investigate the root
cause of visible and sub-visible particle formation. Identified visible and sub-visible particles
should be adequately characterized to support conclusions that the preparation and
administration conditions are appropriate and risks from sub-visible and visible particles are
appropriate characterized. We also recommend that you evaluate additional preparation and
administration conditions to determine compatibility with narsoplimab to allow for further
flexibility in administration (i.e., product contact materials, narsoplimab dilutions, diluents, or
filters). All incompatibilities should be thoroughly investigated to inform labeling.

14.2
•

Summary of Re-adjudication by Patient

Patient
Original assessment:
The patient had a non-evaluable platelet response as the patient never engrafted platelets.
During the initial review, there was insufficient evidence that the patient is a responder
based on only two parameters of the response endpoint definition. In addition, cyclosporin
was stopped 10 days prior to starting narsoplimab, and the review team could not
completely exclude the possibility that the findings for the two other response parameters,
an improvement in LDH and in serum creatinine, were attributable to cessation of
cyclosporin.
(b) (6)

Re-assessment:
The reviewer acknowledges that failure to engraft platelets in the post-transplant setting is
well described as a serious complication and that initial review and adjudication of the
patient as a non-responder may have been overly stringent. If a patient has not engrafted
platelets and developed TA-TMA, a platelet count response would likely not be
demonstrated with TA-TMA directed therapy in setting of platelet engraftment. The patient
demonstrated a clear response in terms of a large magnitude of reduction in LDH (LDH
decreased from 712 U/L to < 1.5 x upper limit of normal) and serum creatinine (creatinine
decreased 1.68 mg/dL to 1.03mg/dL). While not part of the TMA response definition as
defined in the protocol, the haptoglobin increase from undetectable to the normal range is
evidence of a reduction of hemolysis, a key feature of TA-TMA.
With respect to the observed improvement in LDH and renal function potentially being due
to discontinuing the cyclosporin 10 days prior to narsoplimab, it is possible that cyclosporin
discontinuation improved renal function, as renal toxicity is well described with this drug.
However, given the patient met the criteria for high-risk TMA as defined in protocol, it is
unlikely that a substantial improvement in hemolytic markers such as LDH and haptoglobin
would be attributed solely to stopping the CNI. As stated by Schoettler et al, patients with
high-risk features should receive TA-TMA directed therapies (Schoettler et al., 2023).
Further, there is recent literature and retrospective studies suggesting that outcomes are
not improved with CNI withdrawal in patients with TA-TMA (Acosta-Medina et al, 2025).

Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review

Narsoplimab-wuug
The reviewer concludes there is sufficient evidence that this patient is a responder to
narsoplimab based on improvement in LDH and decrease in creatinine in setting of failure
to engraft platelets.
•

Patient
Original assessment:
The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria, and the baseline serum creatinine was
trending down prior to narsoplimab administration; therefore, the effect of narsoplimab on
the renal response could not be determined. The platelet response is non-evaluable as the
patient had not engrafted platelets, and thus, the patient only met one response criteria,
LDH.
(b) (6)

Re-assessment:
This is another case in which the strict TMA response definition was problematic in
determining response to narsoplimab, as the patient did not engraft platelets so this
component could not be assessed.
The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria at the time of study entry; however, it is
notable that the patient did meet renal inclusion criteria at the time of TA-TMA diagnosis. In
addition, the decline in renal function starts early, often with proteinuria, and the change in
eGFR may not appear until further into the progression of the TMA. The improvement in
renal function was likely due to withdrawal of sirolimus and tacrolimus (nephrotoxic
agents); however, normalization of renal function occurred following administration of
narsoplimab therapy. Further evidence of response to TA-TMA is supported by reductions in
hemolytic markers of LDH and haptoglobin.
In considering the totality of the clinical picture, it appears that this patient had a response
to narsoplimab based on improvement in renal function and reduction in LDH in setting of
failure to engraft platelets.
•

Patient
Original assessment:
The patient had a non-evaluable platelet response due to failure to engraft platelets. The
LDH response was transient, and tacrolimus was stopped the same day as narsoplimab
initiated so we cannot conclude that the LDH response or renal response was due to
narsoplimab.
(b) (6)

Re-assessment:
Consistent with similar line of reasoning as above, it is not unusual for this medically
complex population to have platelet engraftment failure and therefore the entire clinical
picture should be considered.

During a Type A meeting with the Sponsor on February 9, 2022 (DARRTS 3/16/2022), the
Applicant clarified that the patient did not discontinue tacrolimus as stated in the patient
narrative. The Applicant stated this was an error in the patient narrative and confirmed with
documentation in the case report form that tacrolimus continued to be administered.
Therefore, the intervention of administration of narsoplimab in setting of declining renal
function provides evidence that the renal response was likely due to narsoplimab.
Further, while there were increases in LDH (>1.5x ULN) during this time period, upon review
of the record, these lab values appear to be single values and not sustained. This patient
also had multiple infections during the course of transplant and while receiving
narsoplimab. When looking at the overall trend of LDH from time of administration of
narsoplimab, the trend was downward, and the LDH response criterion was met.
It is the clinical reviewer’s assessment based on the totality of data that the patient did
respond to narsoplimab given the observed improvements in LDH and renal function in
setting of failure to engraft platelets.
•

Patient
Original assessment:
The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria. The patient met platelet and LDH response
criteria, but there was no evidence of clinical improvement, and thus, the patient did not
meet response criteria. The patient also had a diagnosis of microangiopathic hemolytic
anemia (MAHA) and a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT) 80 days prior to finally meeting
inclusion criteria of a negative DAT, calling into question as to whether this patient truly had
DAT negative TA-TMA.
(b) (6)

Re-assessment:
The patient did not have an evaluable organ function component of TA-TMA to assess
response. However, response components of LDH and platelet count were met. As
supportive evidence, hemoglobin also increased by approximately 2g/dL. While hemoglobin
increase in the absence of transfusions was not a component of the response criteria it is
evidence of reduction in hemolysis.
Despite the positive DAT, it does appear that the patient had a diagnosis of TA-TMA. Recent
consensus diagnostic criteria for TA-TMA does not include a negative DAT, unlike some the
previously proposed diagnostic criteria such as Cho et al., 2010 and Li et al., 2019
(Schoettler et al., 2023).
It is the reviewer’s assessment that this patient responded to narsoplimab. While there was
no evaluable “clinical” component, the patient clearly had a substantial reduction in
hemolysis. In addition, the patient was receiving platelet transfusions at the time of TA-TMA
diagnosis and achieved platelet transfusion independence following narsoplimab, which
represents a clinical benefit.

Patient
Original assessment:
The patient’s neurological response could not be clearly attributed to a treatment effect of
narsoplimab. Additionally, we could not conclude that the 4-week transfusion free interval
was due to narsoplimab due to confounders. The patient did not meet three response
criteria.
(b) (6)

Re-assessment:
The patient met LDH and platelet count response criteria. Regarding the neurologic
response, it is this reviewer’s opinion that this is very difficult to interpret given the
supportive care measures that were implemented to treat posterior reversible
encephalopathy syndrome (PRES). However, the clinical reviewer does acknowledge that a
4-week transfusion free period did occur, and this was likely due to narsoplimab, as it
occurred in the setting of decreased hemolysis after initiation of narsoplimab therapy. The
other concern was this patient was not considered a responder by the Applicant until 103
days after narsoplimab treatment. However, the clinical reviewer acknowledges that
underlying comorbidities likely contributed to inability to assess response prior to this
timepoint.
It is the clinical reviewer’s assessment that the patient did have a response to narsoplimab.

extremes, suggesting a potential violation of the positivity assumption.

14.4 Summary of Key Issues with the Original Submission

A summary of the key issues with the original submission is described in the Appendix
1. There were concerns regarding the conduct and efficacy analysis of TMA-001. Although
TMA-001 had a protocol with inclusion and exclusion criteria and response criteria, the
Applicant repeatedly issued waivers in order to enroll individual patients who did not
meet the inclusion criteria and the lack of adherence to response criteria undermined
the credibility of trial results. The trial enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. The review
team discovered 11 patients who did not meet the entry criteria for whom waivers were
granted. The Applicant concluded that the response rate based on the enrolled
population was 17/28 (61%, 95% CI: 41, 79%). The review team independently reviewed
each case in detail and concluded that there were 5 patients judged as responders by
the Applicant who did not meet the response criteria. The FDA analysis of the response
rate was 12/28 (43%) (95% CI 24% to 63%).
2. The primary analysis was to determine whether the study TMA-001 has demonstrated
at least a 15% response rate of the study drug. The statistical assumptions in the
protocol listed response rate of 15% for a non-treated group. However, they did not
provide adequate justification for this assumed 15% response rate when the BLA was
submitted.
3. Lack of confirmatory evidence. The Applicant submitted a mechanism of action (MOA)
paper to provide further support regarding the mechanism of action of narsoplimab.
There is sufficient literature to document the presence of MASP-2 and lectin
components in TMA and HSCT-TMA. The Applicant also provided data based on in vitro
and in vivo models of TMA demonstrating that narsoplimab mitigated complement
deposition derived from the lectin/MASP2 pathway, reduced markers of endothelial cell
injury and apoptosis, and delayed though did not prevent the onset of thromboses
secondary to endothelial injury. Although there appears to be partial mitigation of Cactivation and delay in thrombotic onset by narsoplimab in animal and in vitro models,
the Applicant has not shown sufficient evidence that this is expected to translate into a
measurable clinical benefit, either directly or indirectly.
4. The clinical pharmacology review team’s assessment found no association between
narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough concentrations or the extent of complement
component 4d (C4d) inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in
patients with HSCT-TMA. Almost all patients who did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug
treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than patients who
responded to treatment. Therefore, although related to the mechanism of action of the
drug, the extent of C4d inhibition does not appear to be predictive of treatment
response in HSCT-TMA patients.

14.5 Summary of Regulatory History

The following are summaries of the key regulatory interactions with the Sponsor from after the
CRL to the submission of the response to clinical hold on XX date.
High level summaries from the meeting are described below, see finalized meeting minutes in
DARRTS for further details.
Type A Meeting, February 9, 2022: Discussion of deficiencies of CRL and path forward
The Division acknowledged that they did not object to the proposed efficacy analysis during
the IND stage of development, however, upon review of the application, significant efficacy
review issues (including high proportion of patients not meeting inclusion/exclusion criteria,
missing response data that introduced evaluation challenges, and significant issues with
response interpretability), as described in the CRL were identified. These issues were
determined to be too great, given the small number of patients evaluated.
The Division expressed concern regarding the robustness of the results of Study TMA-001 as the
success of the trial hinged on a few patients with varying responses dependent upon different
adjudications. The Agency reiterated concerns regarding the success threshold of 15% and
concerns with the literature review to serve as an external control. The Agency also reiterated
that the application lacked confirmatory evidence and the PD and PK data provided were not
adequate.
Formal Dispute Resolution Request (FDRR) July 15, 2022.
The Applicant submitted a formal dispute resolution request (FDRR) to the Office of New
Druga(OND) on July 15, 2022. The appeal concerned the October 15, 2021, CRL issued by the
Office of Cardiology, Hematology, Endocrinology, and Nephrology (OCHEN). On November 3,
2022, Peter Stein, MD, the deciding authority, issued a Formal Dispute Resolution Appeal
Denied letter. In the letter, several paths forward were outlined. One approach would be to
conduct a new trial—either randomized controlled or a single-arm trial using agreed upon
responder criteria and compared to a well-matched historical control group and/or a prespecified threshold based upon an independent literature assessment. Alternatively, the
Applicant could consider a resubmission to address the CR deficiencies including results
comparing response from Study TMA-001 to a threshold derived from an independent
literature analysis and/or evidence of increased survival among patients from Study TMA-001
compared to an appropriate historical control group.
Post FDRR Meetings
- A Type B meeting was held on May 11, 2023 to discuss the Applicant’s proposal for a
resubmission which would include results comparing the response from Study OMS721TMA-001 to a threshold derived from an independent literature analysis, as well as
evidence of increased survival among patients from Study TMA-001 compared to an
appropriate historical control group. In particular, the Applicant proposed to use data from
CDER Clinical Review Template (CRT)
Version date: March 8, 2019
Reference ID: 5718152

77

Clinical, Statistical, and Clinical Pharmacology Review
BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
an independently conducted retrospective cohort study from the Kyoto Stem Cell
Transplantation Group (KSCTG) for determination of survival estimates following TA-TMA
diagnosis against which survival in OMS721-TMA-001 can be compared. The Division
expressed concerns with this approach as highlighted in the Dispute Appeal Denial Letter,
which noted survival results in the series reported by Matsui et al. (H Matsui, Y Arai et al.
Blood Advances 4:2169-3179, 2020) with a <10% long-term survival in patients with TA-TMA
appeared to be an outlier compared to other literature reports.
- Agency issued Written Responses on January 4, 2024, to provide feedback on the
Applicant’s resubmission proposal to provide evidence of efficacy based on the KSCTG
registry to compared to survival data to Study TMA-001. In addition, the Applicant proposed
to submit details from the single patient IND expanded access program and additional
mechanistic information. The Agency expressed concerns with the Applicant’s proposal in
particular with comparability between the KSCTG registry patients and patients in Study
TMA-001.
- A Type B face-to-face meeting was held with the Applicant on September 5, 2024 to discuss
Omeros’ proposal for resubmission of their BLA using the KSCTG registry as an external
control group and using survival data from Omeros’ EAP for mechanistic data as
confirmatory evidence. The Division provided advice regarding the Applicant’s proposal,
stating that they were concerned the Applicants proposal for a resubmission may not be
adequate to address the deficiencies outlined in the CRL without individual patient data and
the limitations of using the real-world data for supporting the drug’s efficacy.
- An Applicant-requested meeting took place on April 30, 2025. The Applicant received the
acknowledgment letter for the resubmission of BLA 761152, which the Agency did not
consider a complete response to the October 15, 2021 action letter, on April 28, 2025, and
requested to meet with FDA to clarify the contents of the resubmission. The Applicant
detailed which data was available for the KSCTG registry. The Applicant provided slides
which stated: “We appreciate FDA’s request for raw patient-level data, algorithms, and
program codes that would permit the Agency to confirm which patients from the KSCTG
registry (N = 2425) met diagnostic criteria for HSCT-TMA. Data necessary to verify or
exclude a TA-TMA diagnosis are not collected or entered into the KSCTG registry. Omeros
does not view the overall registry as germane to the external control in the BLA
resubmission. The patients in the KSCTG registry who were diagnosed by their treating
physician as having TA-TMA (N = 134) are the relevant population from which to begin the
analysis of the external control.” The Applicant also stated that “Additional data for the 134
patients, responding to nearly all of FDA’s queries, have just been obtained and can be
submitted shortly.” The Agency stated that they would await the additional data and
review once they were submitted.

Narsoplimab-wuug

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On November 17, 2020, Omeros Inc. submitted an original new drug application (NDA) for narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of
patients with hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (HSCT-TMA) based on data from an openlabel, single arm phase 1/2 trial. Narsoplimab-wuug is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectinassociated serine protease-2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement. The proposed dosage of
(b) (4)
narsoplimab-wuug is 4 mg/kg administered as an intravenous infusion once a week
.

HSCT-TMA
HSCT-TMA occurs when endothelial injury, in the context of an allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation, causes
microangiopathic anemia and platelet consumption, leading to microvascular thrombosis and fibrin deposition. HSCT-TMA may
present with new onset or increasing proteinuria, hypertension, elevated serum creatinine and an increase in transfusion requirements
with evidence of intravascular hemolysis. There is no consensus on a standardized diagnostic criterion, but most definitions include a
combination of clinical and laboratory findings. The fatality rate of HSCT-TMA has been reported to be from 60-90% (George et al.
2004). HSCT-TMA is associated with increased morbidity from chronic kidney disease or other end organ dysfunction (i.e.,
pulmonary, gastrointestinal, neurological systems). The renal system is most frequently involved with acute kidney injury, chronic
kidney disease, proteinuria, and hypertension. (Laskin et al. 2011). The use of high dose chemotherapy, radiation, calcineurin inhibitor
(CNI) exposure, graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) and infections have all been reported as causative factors.

Current Treatment Options
There are no FDA approved products for the treatment of HSCT-TMA. The first line treatment of HSCT-TMA is generally limited to
supportive therapy. Initial first line management has included withdrawal or minimization of CNIs (i.e., sirolimus, tacrolimus or both)
as these agents have been implicated as risk factors although there is ongoing debate as to whether withdrawal is beneficial. Changing
the immunosuppressive regimen is another important initial management step in patients with HSCT-TMA, but may take 1-2 weeks
before an effect is observed. Additional measures include treatment of infections, GHVD and aggressive hypertension management.

4
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Off-label medications such as rituximab* 1, defibrotide* 2 and complement inhibitors such as C5 inhibitors (ravulizumab 3,
eculizumab 4)* have emerged as potential therapeutic agents in HSCT-TMA. There is no accepted pharmacodynamic marker to
diagnose HSCT-TMA, or to assess prognosis/follow response to treatment, although several are being evaluated and used in clinical
studies.

Study OMS-721-TMA-001 (Study TMA-001)
The Applicant conducted Study OMS721-TMA-001 (from here on referred to as Study TMA-001) in patients with HSCT-TMA.
Study TMA-001 was originally designed as a 3-stage, open-label single-arm, ascending-dose-escalation study of narsoplimab-wuug
evaluating patients with atypical hemolytic anemia (aHUS), TTP or HSCT-TMA. Stage 2 was a cohort expansion stage and Stage 3
was an optional extension of dosing for patients who completed stage 2. Enrollment of patients with TTP ended early as a treatment
effect over standard of care was not observed, and enrollment of patients with aHUS was stopped early when a dedicated Phase 3
study was initiated for patients with aHUS.
Stage 2 of Study TMA-001 enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. Stage 2 of the trial consisted of a 4-week dosing period with
optional extension of 4 additional weeks of dosing in Stage 3. Eligible patients were enrolled if they had a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA
that persisted at least 2 weeks following modification or discontinuation of CNI treatment or at least 30 days after transplant with

1
Rituximab is CD20-directed cytolytic antibody indicated for the treatment of adult patients with Non-Hodgkin’s Lymphoma, adult patients with
chronic lymphocytic leukemia, rheumatoid arthritis in combination with methotrexate in adult patients, granulomatosis with polyangiitis and
microscopic polyangiitis in adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older, moderate to severe pemphigus vulgaris in adult patients.

Defibrotide is indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with hepatic veno-occlusive disease (VOD). The mechanism of action has
not been fully elucidated.
2

Ravulizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients one month of age and older with paroxysmal
nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) and atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS).

3

Eculizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of patients with PNH, treatment of patients with aHUS, treatment of
generalized myasthenia gravis in adult patients, treatment of neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder in adult patients.

4

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
platelet count <150,000/uL, evidence of microangiopathic hemolysis (presence of schistocytes, serum LDH > upper limit of normal
(ULN), or haptoglobin < lower limit of normal), and renal dysfunction.
There was no internal control group.
Statistical Methods
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of HSCT-TMA patients who responded to narsoplimab-wuug treatment. Treatment
response was defined as both an improvement in TMA- associated laboratory markers (Requirement 1): platelet count and LDH, as
well as improvement in clinical status (Requirement 2): either improvement in organ function (renal, pulmonary, GI or neurological)
at any time point post-baseline during the study, or freedom of transfusion defined as no transfusions (RBC, platelet) for at least 4
weeks from the last transfusion except for patients who died within 4 weeks of the last transfusion. Improvement in organ function
was determined by a clinical review of data by the local site investigators.
The primary analysis was to determine whether the study TMA-001 has demonstrated at least a 15% response rate of the study drug.
The statistical assumptions in the protocol listed response rate of 15% for a non-treated group. However, they did not provide adequate
justification for this assumed 15% response rate when the BLA was submitted. As noted above, there was no control group in the
study; therefore, upon request, the Applicant submitted a literature-based review to justify the selected response rate from an external
control group (see below).
Trial Conduct and Analyses of Efficacy
Although TMA-001 had a protocol with inclusion and exclusion criteria and response criteria, the Applicant repeatedly issued waivers
in order to enroll individual patients who did not meet the inclusion criteria and the lack of adherence to response criteria undermined
the credibility of trial results. The trial enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. The review team discovered 11 patients who did not
meet the entry criteria for whom waivers were granted. The Applicant concluded that the response rate based on the enrolled
population was 17/28 (61%, 95% CI 41, 79%). The review team independently reviewed each case in detail and concluded that there
were 5 patients judged as responders by the Applicant who did not meet the response criteria. The FDA analysis of the response rate
was 12/28 (43%) (95% CI 24% to 63%).
Literature-Based Historical Data of Untreated HSCT-TMA
The Applicant conducted a literature review based on publications from 2000 to 2020 to provide relevant clinical data on HSCT-TMA
patients with the attempt to include similar risk factors and inclusion/exclusion criteria to those in pivotal Study TMA-001 to estimate
6
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
the response rate in patients not treated with narsoplimab, i.e., to provide justification for the 15% efficacy threshold for Study TMA001. The clinical and statistical review teams identified a number of limitations to the use of the proposed literature based historical
data to establish the response threshold. The limitations are outlined in the review. One hundred and seventy patients were identified
for the final analysis for a historical control response rate of 32% (95% CI 26%, 40%). Although limitations exist in this exercise, at a
minimum we can state that the chosen 15% appears too low and not supported by the literature review.
Mechanistic Evidence
The Applicant submitted a mechanism of action (MOA) paper to provide further support regarding the mechanism of action of
narsoplimab. There is sufficient literature to document the presence of MASP-2 and lectin components in TMA and HSCT-TMA. The
Applicant also provided data based on in vitro and in vivo models of TMA demonstrating that narsoplimab mitigated complement
deposition derived from the lectin/MASP2 pathway, reduced markers of endothelial cell injury and apoptosis, and delayed though did
not prevent the onset of thromboses secondary to endothelial injury. Although there appears to be partial mitigation of C-activation
and delay in thrombotic onset by narsoplimab in animal and in vitro models, the Applicant has not shown sufficient evidence that this
is expected to translate into a measurable clinical benefit, either directly or indirectly. It is possible that narsoplimab-wuug’s inhibition
of the lectin pathway is not sufficient to alter the outcome in HSCT-TMA in a sufficient magnitude to demonstrate clinical benefit.
Exposure-Response
The clinical pharmacology review team’s assessment found no association between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough
concentrations or the extent of complement component 4d (C4d) inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in
patients with HSCT-TMA. Almost all patients who did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug treatment had consistently comparable or
higher exposure than patients who responded to treatment. Therefore, although related to the mechanism of action of the drug, the
extent of C4d inhibition does not appear to be predictive of treatment response in HSCT-TMA patients.
Conclusions on Effectiveness
The FDA review team concludes that the data presented in BLA 761152 fail to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness for
narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of HSCT-TMA. This conclusion is primarily based on the lack of at least one “adequate and wellcontrolled” trial. In addition, there is insufficient mechanistic evidence that narsoplimab-wuug will have the purported effect It is also
notable that, although we have limited confidence in the estimated response rate of 32% from the literature review, this percentage
falls within the 95% confidence interval for the FDA’s calculated response rate of 43% (95% CI 24% to 63%) not supporting a
treatment effect.
7
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
To establish a drug’s effectiveness, it is essential to distinguish the effect of the drug “from other influences, such as spontaneous
change in the course of the disease, placebo effect, or biased observation” (21 CFR 314.126(a)). This is the basis for the statutory
requirement that approval be based on adequate and well-controlled investigations, as well as the basis for FDA’s regulations
describing the characteristics of such investigations (i.e., design elements that are generally intended to minimize bias and permit a
valid comparison with a control to provide a quantitative assessment of drug effect).
There are certain circumstances when one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation with compelling results, supported by
additional data from the natural history of the disease could support an application. However, Study TMA-001 is a single arm
uncontrolled study and the lack of a concurrent control prevents differentiation of the treatment effect from the confounding therapies,
competing risks, and durability of response in patients with HSCT related TMA. The Applicant has not provided sufficient evidence
for a reliable responder rate in an untreated group. The Applicant has not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness to support
approval.
Safety
The safety database is limited given rarity of disease and small sample size. Observed serious adverse events included infections
occurring in 36% of patients but given the lack of a control arm, the level of certainty for casual association between drug exposure
and risk difficult to determine. If a clinical benefit of the treatment can be established for narsoplimab for the proposed indication, the
risks, e.g., infections, can be adequately addressed in the product labeling.
Path Forward
Additional data will be needed to demonstrate efficacy of narsoplimab in HSCT-TMA. Possible options include:
If a reliable responder rate in a comparator group could be worked out, that might allow for retaining the current approach (threshold
analysis). The Applicant could explore alternative external data (e.g. registries) to establish a responder rate. The Applicant would
need to increase the total number of evaluable patients in TMA-001 so that the analysis isn’t entirely post hoc, for example, to 50 as
we stated in the minutes to the January 29, 2019 meeting (see last paragraph of response of FDA to Question 2).
Alternatively, the Applicant could conduct at least one randomized control trial to demonstrate the effectiveness of narsoplimab-wuug
for the treatment of HSCT-TMA.

8
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
The Applicant will also need to provide confirmatory evidence; what this evidence will consist of can be discussed in a post action
meeting. Of note, if the current trial that Omeros is now running in HUS were to be successful, that data might be used as
confirmatory evidence for HSCT TMA.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
HSCT-TMA is a rare disease that occurs post hematopoietic stem cell transplantation associated with high morbidity and mortality.
There are no FDA available therapies and usual treatment is supportive care, modification of calcineurin inhibitors and off-label use of
complement inhibitors. The nature of the benefit, size of treatment effect and clinical benefit of treatment effect cannot be determined
based on the data submitted in this application. The safety database is limited given rarity of disease and small sample size. The
therapeutic context in which this drug would be used is a condition for which there is high mortality with no available therapies.
Observed serious adverse events included infections occurring in 36% of patients but given the lack of a control arm, the level of

Narsoplimab-wuug
certainty for casual association between drug exposure and risk difficult to determine. If a clinical benefit of the treatment can be
established for narsoplimab for the proposed indication, the risks, e.g., infections, can be adequately addressed in the product labeling
to support informed individual benefit-risk assessments by patients and providers However, at this time no conclusion regarding
benefit-risk can be determined as efficacy cannot be established for this product.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Product Introduction
Narsoplimab-wuug (proprietary name, YARTEMLEA), a new molecular entity (NME), is a
monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2
(MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement.
The Applicant proposes the following indication: the treatment of patients with HSCT-TMA
The proposed dosing regimen is to administer narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg once weekly, given as
(b) (4)
a 30-minute intravenous (IV) infusion (up to a maximum of 370 mg per infusion)
(b) (4)

Disease Background
Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (HSCT-TMA) is
a serious multisystem complication of transplant that is associated with significant morbidity and
mortality. Milder forms of HSCT-TMA can result in an increased risk of long-term
complications such as chronic kidney disease, while whereas severe forms are associated with
mortality rates of 60-90% or even higher % or more (Khosla et al. 2018; Epperla et al. 2020).
HSCT-TMA is characterized by thrombocytopenia, Coombs-negative hemolysis (elevated
lactate dehydrogenase, decreased haptoglobin, anemia), presences of schistocytes, and often
renal injury or other organ involvement (Elsallabi et al. 2016). Extra- renal manifestations
include central nervous system (convulsions, encephalopathy, drowsiness), cardiovascular
complications (cardiomyopathy, myocarditis), and pulmonary and gastrointestinal involvement.
It remains unclear if the high mortality rate is due to HSCT-TMA or a reflection of the severity
of illness in patients with HSCT-TMA, unrelated to the etiology (Schoettler et al. 2020).
The true incidence of HSCT-TMA following allogeneic transplant is not known at as it is
difficult to calculate given the lack of consensus on diagnostic criteria; studies have estimated the
incidence anywhere from 0% to 64% (Khosla et al. 2018). One study cited an incidence of 8.2%
in a comprehensive literature review published in 2004 based on 5423 allotransplant patients
(George et al. 2004). A more recent review of nearly 2000 adult patients with HSCT identified
13% as having definite TMA (Postalcioglu et al. 2018).
Diagnosis is particular also challenging not only because of the complex and critically ill patient
population, with but due to lack of a universally accepted diagnostic criteria. The diagnosis is
difficult due to confounding complications such as graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), which can
closely resemble certain aspects of HSCT-TMA (Khosla et al. 2018). Biopsy with clinical
criteria can yield a definitive diagnosis, but many patients cannot undergo invasive procedures
13
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
without a high risk of bleeding or other complications (Khosla et al. 2018). Histologic features in
the kidney include thickened capillary walls, occluded vascular lumen and endothelial cell
separation (Elsallabi et al. 2016). Common features of HSCT-TMA include the presences of
schistocytes, elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), decreased haptoglobin, renal or neurologic
dysfunction, thrombocytopenia and anemia.
It is critical to differentiate HSCT-TMA from other TMA disorders such hemolytic uremic
syndrome (HUS) and thrombotic TTP, in addition to disseminated intravascular coagulation and
Coombs-positive hemolytic anemia. To aid in this, typically patients with a diagnosis HSCTTMA are found to have a disintegrin and metalloproteinase with a thrombospondin type 1 motif,
member 13 (ADAMTS13) activity level >10%, normal coagulation studies and a negative direct
antiglobulin test. Proteinuria and terminal complement activation were recently reported to be
markers of poor prognosis in children and young adults with HSCT-TMA (Jodele et al. 2014).
More recently it has been proposed to include elevated serum C5b-9 levels to the diagnostic
criteria, in addition to hypertension and proteinuria as early clinical markers of HSCT-TMA
(Khosla et al. 2018).
The pathophysiology of HSCT-TMA is complex and continues to evolve as it is investigated.
Fundamental to the pathophysiology of HSCT-TMA is endothelial dysfunction (Khosla et al.
2018). Inflammatory cytokines result in endothelial damage and the activation of platelets as
well as other coagulation factors, this in turn leads to thrombosis and fibrin deposition in the
microvasculature of organs with subsequent end organ damage (Kim et al. 2015). Complement
dysregulation has more recently been recognized as an important component of HSCT-TMA
pathophysiology (Kim et al. 2015). Neutrophil extracellular traps (NETs) have also been
implicated as a biomarker in HSCT-TMA (Arai et al. 2013).
Risk factors for HSCT-TMA include unrelated donor, human leukocyte antigen mismatch, highdose chemotherapy, radiation therapy, immunosuppression agents (calcineurin inhibitors and
newer mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) inhibitors such as sirolimus), advanced age,
female sex, GVHD and infections (Kim et al. 2015; Elsallabi et al. 2016).
The majority of cases present 20 to 100 days following transplant, although rare cases up to 2
years have been reported (Jodele et al. 2014)(Gavriilaki et al. 2017; Khosla et al. 2018).
Current Therapy Options
There are no FDA-approved therapies for HSCT-TMA. Response rates to supportive care and
off-label therapies are variable and range from 25% to 93%, with lower response rates attributed
to plasma therapy and higher response rates seen with complement therapies (Young et al. 2021).
The first-line management of HSCT-TMA is to provide supportive care which includes
minimizing the insult that is causing endothelial damage, this may be an effective treatment
strategy in patients with mild-moderate forms of HSCT-TMA (Rosenthal 2016). This approach
includes treating infections and/or GVHD and hypertension management (Jodele et al. 2015;
Khosla et al. 2018). In addition, reduction or withdrawal of calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs) may be
considered an initial step in HSCT-TMA management (Ho et al. 2005; Khosla et al. 2018).
Sometimes an effective approach is to change the immunosuppression from a CNI to an
14
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
alternative agent. Patients may not be responsive to withdrawal or modification of
immunosuppressants and there is the potential to increase the risk of developing or worsening
GVHD (Elsallabi et al. 2016; Li et al. 2019; Epperla et al. 2020).
Therapeutic plasma exchange (TPE), an established treatment for TTP, is not generally
considered to be as beneficial (Khosla et al. 2018) for patients with HSCT-TMA. TPE may be
more effective when initiated early or when there is factor H autoantibodies (Khosla et al. 2018;
Young et al. 2021). TPE is also associated with significant risks such as infection, hemorrhage,
thrombosis, hypoxia, hypotension, serum sickness and anaphylaxis (Khosla et al. 2018).
There are several unapproved therapies that are used for the treatment of HSCT-TMA with
varying response rates.
Response Rates to Unapproved Therapies
Response rates to supportive care and off-label therapies are variable and are reported to range
from 25% to 93%, with lower response rates attributed to plasma therapy and higher response
rates seen with complement therapies (Young et al. 2021) with the caveat that responses
described in literature are frequently based on retrospective studies, use different response
definitions and do not control for biases.
Rituximab, an anti-cluster of differentiation 20 (CD20) monoclonal antibody has also been used
as monotherapy or in combination with other therapies such as TPE or defibrotide (Jodele et al.
2015). Jodele et al demonstrated a 67% response rate with rituximab with or without plasma
exchange in 6 patients although this was single arm trial and prospective randomized trials
needed. Rituximab is associated with adverse reactions including; infusion related reactions,
peripheral edema and hypertension, as well as less common but serious adverse reactions such as
hepatitis B virus reactivation and progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (Kim et al. 2015).
Defibrotide, a polydeoxyribonucleotide salt, has also been used for HSCT-TMA, with response
rates ranging from 61-76% (monotherapy or in combinations with other therapies), although
prospective studies are still needed (Young et al. 2021).
Other complement inhibitors are being investigated as therapeutic options. More recently,
eculizumab, a C5 inhibitor, has been reported to be successful at resolving HSCT-TMA in 6792% of patients in case reports and small studies to include pediatric population (Khosla et al.
2018). Many of the dosing regiments with C5 inhibitors in HSCT_TMA is guided by measuring
CH50 and adjusting dosing schedule to maintain an adequately suppressed CH50 level. Larger,
multi-institutional, controlled studies are need to better evaluate the use ofC5 inhibitors in
patients with HSCT-TMA. Reported case series have indicated a possible role for daclizumab
and vincristine as well (Elsallabi et al. 2016).

Mechanism of action
Mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease-2 (MASP-2) is a key enzyme for the activation of the lectin pathway of
complement, which leads to cleavage of complement components C2 and C4, followed by C3 and C5 activation and
formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). OMS721 selectively blocks MASP-2 and inhibits the lectin pathway of
complement activation, which is postulated to reduce prothrombotic responses in thrombotic microangiopathy.
Active moieties
OMS721 is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody directed against MASP-2.

Not applicable since narsoplimab-wuug is a mAb

Body weight
The clearance of narsoplimab-wuug is affected by body weight; therefore, weight-based dosing of 4 mg/kg is recommended.
However, there is a dose cap of 370 mg. A weight-based dosing and dose cap will provide comparable steady-state exposure
across all body weight ranges.

Immunogenicity (if applicable)
Anti-Narsoplimab-wuug antibodies were measured by electrochemiluminescence immunoassay (ECLIA) via C4d on mannancoated beads.
Two of 6 (33%) healthy subjects who received IV narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg once weekly for 6 weeks developed antidrug
antibodies. Narsoplimab-wuug exposure (AUC0­last) was 50% lower (1810 vs. 2781 mcg.h/mL) in the 2 subjects with ADA. In
HSCT-TMA patients who received IV narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg or 370 mg once weekly for up to 8 weeks, 2 of 28 (7%)
developed antidrug antibodies. One of the 2 HSCT-TMA patients developed neutralizing antibodies.
Compared to HSCT-TMA patients without antidrug antibodies, total clearance (CLtotal) was greater in HSCT-TMA patients with
antidrug antibodies. Based upon popPK Vmax was 143% higher in patients with ADA, and is predicted to result in 22% lower
AUC,ss and 52% lower Cmin,ss. The patient who developed neutralizing antibody was a treatment responder.

5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Summary: Narsoplimab is specific for MASP-2 and selectively blocks lectin-initiated
complement activation. The lectin pathway contributes to complement (C) activation and
thrombus formation in defined in vitro and in vivo models of endothelial injury, which
presumably reflect key elements of TMAs. Narsoplimab inhibits this contribution, but other Cactivation pathways are also contributory; thus, vascular occlusion appears delayed, but
occlusion still occurred in the murine model of TMA.
Background: OMS721 (narsoplimab) binds MASP-2 with a KD of 62-73pM and selectively
inhibits the lectin pathway of complement (IC50 2.5nM). Specificity for the lectin pathway was
evaluated by demonstrating a dose-dependent inhibition of lectin-dependent membrane attack
complex (MAC) deposition with an IC50 of 1nM without significant inhibition of the alternativeor classical pathway-induced MAC deposition up to 100nM. In vivo studies in healthy animals
demonstrated that OMS721 has a pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic (PK-PD) relationship with
eventual saturation of lectin-dependent C4 and C3 activation.
Key pharmacodynamic effects of OMS721 were evaluated in an ex vivo model of aHUS and an
in vivo mouse model of TMA. In the ex vivo aHUS study, serum from patients with aHUS
(during the acute phase of the disease and at remission) was incubated with activated human
microvascular endothelial cells in vitro in the presence and absence of OMS721.
OMS721 partially reduced C5b-9 deposition (range 40% - 54%; Figure 1) and reduced platelet
adherence (range 57% - 79%; Figure 2) on the surface of microvascular endothelial cells at the
timepoint measured upon exposure to serum of patients with aHUS both in the acute phase and
in remission. Of note, Figure 2 indicates that the pan-complement inhibitor sCR1 abrogated C5b9 deposition whereas narsoplimab exhibited partial inhibition, demonstrating that complement
pathways other than lectin substantially contribute to C5b-9 deposition induced by aHUS serum.

Narsoplimab-wuug
In vivo efficacy of OMS721 was assessed in a mouse model of TMA in which localized
thrombosis was induced by photo-activation of fluorescein isothiocyanate-labeled dextran in the
microvasculature of the cremaster muscle of C57Bl/6 mice.
Administering OMS721 one hour before induction of thrombosis delayed the initiation of
thrombus formation and the time to complete vessel occlusion, demonstrating a contribution of
the lectin complement pathway to the thrombotic response to injury (Figure 3). However, over
time, thrombus formation still occurred in a similar percentage of mice regardless of treatment,
suggesting that pathways in addition to lectin C activation contribute to and eventually result in
microvascular thrombosis.

(bottom panel) in the Mouse Model of FITC-Dextran-Induced TMA

Narsoplimab-wuug

6. Assessment of Effectiveness
6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
6.1.1. Study TMA-001
The proposed narsoplimab-wuug dose and dosing regimen is 4 mg/kg, given as a 30-minute IV
infusion (up to 370 mg per infusion) once weekly. The clinical studies to support the proposed
dose and dosing regimen include a multiple, dose-escalation study (OMS721-NHV-002) in
healthy subjects and a small safety and efficacy study (TMA-001) in HSCT-TMA patients.
The Applicant conducted a multiple dose escalation study (OMS721-NHV-002) in healthy male
and female subjects to assess the PK, PD, and safety of narsoplimab-wuug IV and SC. The study
included assessment of C4d inhibition, a pharmacodynamic marker of the lectin pathway that the
Applicant hypothesized would be representative of clinical efficacy. After the first IV infusion of
narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg, maximum inhibition of C4d was approximately 95% at 6 hours after
the start of infusion and was sustained above 80% from 48 to 312 h after the last dose. With
narsoplimab-wuug 2 mg/kg, C4d inhibition above 80% was maintained from 2 to 48 h after the
first dose. The ex-vivo assay of C4d inhibition was used to justify the selection of a maximum
dose of 4 mg/kg IV dosing in the Phase 2 studies as it was expected to maintain mean lectin
pathway inhibition >80% for about 168 hours, the proposed dosing interval.
Evaluation of inhibition by narsoplimab-wuug of the lectin-pathway of complement activation,
and specifically the lectin-mediated complement component 4d (C4d) formation, is not an
unreasonable approach to dose selection; however, it is important to note that there are no
available biomarkers known to predict treatment response in HSCT-TMA.
The Applicant conducted Study TMA-001, which was initially a proof-of-concept, dose-finding
study in patients with HSCT-TMA, but then was submitted as the ‘pivotal’ safety and efficacy
study for the proposed indication. In stage 1 of Study TMA-001, narsoplimab-wuug was
administered to patients in 3 cohorts (Cohorts 1, 2, and 3), with dose escalation by cohort
(0.67 mg/kg, 2 mg/kg, and 4 mg/kg, respectively) in order to identify the optimal dosing
regimen. After enrollment and dosing at each dose level, a safety review by the clinical study
monitoring team was conducted to determine whether dose escalation should proceed. In Stage 2
of the Core Study (refers to data collected under protocol versions prior to Amendment 11.1), the
dose selected in the first stage (4 mg/kg) was administered to patients by disease etiology (aHUS
alone in Cohort 4; TTP or HSCT-TMA in Cohort 5). Patients enrolled in Stages 2 and 3 prior to
Amendment 10 (Appendix III.15) received narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg. As of Amendment 10 of
the protocol, newly enrolled patients received a fixed narsoplimab-wuug dose of 370 mg.
Preliminary PK evaluation suggested that clearance (CL) was not affected by body weight. In the
Core Study, patients in Stage 2 could proceed to Stage 3 based on the patients’ response to
therapy, safety, and tolerability.
The review team’s assessment found no association between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or
trough concentrations or the extent of C4d inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug
treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA. Almost all non-responders to narsoplimab-wuug
treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than patients who responded to
28
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
treatment, with trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations consistently above IC80 for C4d
inhibition in majority of non-responders (Figure 34, Section 14.3.4). Conversely, amongst the
treatment responders, many patients had trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations just above
IC50 for C4d inhibition.
Therefore, although related to the mechanism of action of the drug, the extent of C4d inhibition
does not appear to be predictive of treatment response in HSCT-TMA patients at the studied
dosage.

6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate
Efficacy
6.2.1. Study OMS-721-TMA-001 (TMA-001)
Overview and Objectives
To support the proposed indication, the Applicant conducted Study TMA-001, titled “A Phase 2,
Uncontrolled, Three-Stage, Dose-Escalation Cohort Study to Evaluate the Safety,
Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics, Immunogenicity, and Clinical Activity of OMS721 in
Adults with Thrombotic Microangiopathies.”
Study TMA-001 was originally designed to provide preliminary information on the safety and
efficacy of narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of TMAs (aHUS, TTP, or HSCT-TMA). Based
on preliminary results, the study was modified to support the indication for treatment of HSCTTMA with narsoplimab-wuug.
Primary Objective (HSCT-TMA):
The coprimary objectives of this study were to:
•

Assess the safety and tolerability of multiple-dose administration of narsoplimab-wuug in
patients with TMA, including patients with HSCT-TMA, aHUS, and TTP

•

Evaluate the efficacy of narsoplimab-wuug in patients with HSCT-TMA by response.
Response is defined as meeting both of the following criteria:
— Improvement in TMA laboratory markers of platelet count and LDH
— Improvement in clinical status (explained in detail below)

Secondary Objectives (HSCT-TMA):
•

100-day survival

•

Overall survival

•

Platelet count change from baseline

•

Lactate Dehydrogenase (LDH) change from baseline

•

Haptoglobin change from baseline
29

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
•

Hemoglobin change from baseline

•

Duration of response (defined as the time between the first date a response is achieved and
the first date the platelet count decreases by more than 50% from the maximum and LDH
increases to >150% of the upper limit of normal).

•

Creatinine change from baseline in patients who had no reason other than HSCT-TMA for
creatinine elevation

•

Freedom from platelet transfusion in patients who received at least 1 platelet transfusion
within 2 weeks of the first narsoplimab-wuug dose

•

Freedom from red blood cell (RBC) transfusion in patients who received at least 1 RBC
transfusion within 2 weeks of the first narsoplimab-wuug dose

•

The PK of multiple-dose administration of narsoplimab-wuug (this will be assessed in
patients with HSCT-TMA as well as patients with aHUS and TTP)

•

The PD of multiple-dose administration of narsoplimab-wuug (this will be assessed in
patients with HSCT-TMA as well as patients with aHUS and TTP)

•

The immunogenicity of multiple-dose administration of narsoplimab-wuug (this will be
assessed in patients with HSCT-TMA as well as patients with aHUS and TTP)

Study Design
Study TMA-001) is a Phase 2, open-label, uncontrolled, 3-stage ascending-dose-escalation study
in patients with TMA, specifically aHUS, TTP, and HSCT-TMA. The study design described
below will focus on HSCT-TMA patients which is used to support the HSCT-TMA indication
for narsoplimab-wuug.
In Stage 1 of the study, narsoplimab-wuug was administered to 3 cohorts (Cohort 1, Cohort 2,
and Cohort 3), with dose escalation by cohorts. Each patient in stage 1 received 4 weekly doses
of narsoplimab-wuug. In Stage 2 of the study, the dose selected in the first stage (4 mg/kg IV)
was administered to expanded cohorts. Prior to Amendment 10, patients in Stage 2 with HSCTTMA received 4 weekly doses (See Table 115 for details on protocol amendments). Patients in
Stage 2 who demonstrated improvement in TMA signs during their initial treatment period were
eligible for Stage 3, which provided an additional 4 weekly doses in patients with HSCT-TMA.
Dosing and administration of narsoplimab-wuug is described in Table 6. Design schema of Stage
1, 2, and 3 (HSCT-TMA patients only) are shown in Figures 4, 5, and 6 below. Standard
treatments were continued on study.

The protocol for Study TMA-001 underwent 12 amendments;, description of all amendments is

changed the dose of narsoplimab-wuug from 4 mg/kg IV to a fixed dose of 370 mg IV for
patients with HSCT-TMA. Enrollment was closed for aHUS patients because a Phase 3 study
was opened for aHUS patients (Study OMS721-HUS-002). Enrollment was closed for TTP
patients because there was no apparent benefit of narsoplimab-wuug over standard of care.

HSCT-TMA, including changes to the primary and secondary objectives to focus on HSCTTMA, and defined criteria by which to determine clinical response to treatment. As of
