7.6. Safety Findings and Concerns Based on
Review of Clinical Safety Database
7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Study TMA-001
It is important to note that because the safety database is composed of only uncontrolled data,
causality assessment of adverse reactions is very challenging. Therefore, all adverse events are
assumed to be drug related adverse reactions for the purposes of this review. In Study TMA-001,
infections, hemorrhage, diarrhea, neutropenia, pyrexia and vomiting were the most common
adverse reactions. Infections were also the most common serious adverse reaction. In total, four
(14%) of patients died during the AE evaluation period, including two (7%) due to adverse
reactions of sepsis. One patient discontinued narsoplimab-wuug due to underlying disease
progression which is likely unrelated to narsoplimab-wuug. The described safety profile is not
unexpected in a complex, immunosuppressed, and ill patient population with multiple
comorbidities, cytopenias, and who are receiving multiple concomitant medications.
A 90-day safety update was provided additional safety data on two ongoing clinical trials; HUS002 and IGA-001, in addition to safety data from compassionate use patients with HSCT-TMA.
Only SAEs, deaths, and discontinuations were reported. There were no events that changed the
safety profile of narsoplimab-wuug.
7.6.1.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event Summary,
Study TMA-001
Grading of AEs was based on the National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for
Adverse Events v.4.0. An overview of TEAEs that occurred in patients with HSCT-TMA during
the AE evaluation period in Study TMA-001 is presented in Table 32. The AE evaluation period
was defined from the time of informed consent to 37 days after the last narsoplimab-wuug
administration. All but one patient experienced at least one TEAE. Most patients experienced a
TEAE that was moderate or severe. In addition, the majority of patients had a SAE. In total, 4
patients had SAEs with fatal outcomes. There were low rates of AEs leading to discontinuation

Narsoplimab-wuug
or interruption of narsoplimab-wuug. See an overview of adverse events in the supportive safety

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Study TMA-001
In total, 16 (57.1%) of 28 patients died during Study TMA-001. Table 33 provides an overview
of the deaths. An additional 2 patients died after withdrawing consent; the cause of death was
unknown. Of the 11 (39.3%) patients who experienced a treatment-emergent death, the most
common TEAE leading to death were infections: neutropenic sepsis, septic shock,
gastrointestinal candidiasis, Escherichia sepsis, pneumonia, and pseudomonal sepsis. There were
4 (14.3%) patient deaths that occurred during the AE evaluation period as presented in Table 33,
narratives are presented below.

7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
The most common (>5%) SAEs reported by preferred terms in Study TMA-001 for HSCT-TMA
patients included graft-versus-host-disease (GVHD) of the gastrointestinal tract, acute kidney
injury, confusional state, acute respiratory failure, neutropenic sepsis, pulmonary edema,
vomiting, and septic shock (Table 34). The most common (>10%) SAEs reported by FDA
MedDRA Queries included infections in particular sepsis, GVHD, acute kidney injury,
neutropenia, and confusional state (Table 35).

Clinical reviewer comment
SAEs occurred in approximately 61% of patients with HSCT-TMA treated with narsoplimabwuug. This is not unexpected given patients were acutely ill at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis
and with multiple comorbidities. Infections were most the most common treatment-emergent SAE
and discussed more in Section 7.7.1 (key review issues). While GVHD was the next most common
TEAE, this was unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug, as this commonly occurs in the posttransplant setting. The clinical review team did not consider GVHD or malignancy to be an
adverse reaction. However, given the lack of an adequate control, cannot fully determine
attribution.
7.6.1.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events, Study TMA-001
(b) (6)

One patient had an AE leading to discontinuation of study drug (Table 36). Patient
received two doses of narsoplimab-wuug and then discontinued due to progressive general
deterioration from failed HSCT, although the ADAE dataset reports the drug was discontinued
due to worsening acute myeloid leukemia (AML). The patient died shortly after discontinuing
narsoplimab-wuug. This TEAE was unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug. See narrative in
Section 17 Deaths.

In the supportive safety database, there were 6 discontinuations due to TEAEs. Table 127 lists
the TEAEs leading to narsoplimab-wuug discontinuation. The Applicant highlighted three
patients’ discontinuations due to TEAEs as adverse events of special interest (AESI). The patient
narratives describing TEAEs that led to premature narsoplimab-wuug discontinuation are
described in the Section 17, this included a TEAE of supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) in a
healthy volunteer, a TEAE of granulomatous orchitis in a patient with aHUS, and a TEAE of
liver insufficiency in a patient with aHUS.
Clinical reviewer comment
There were low rates of drug discontinuations in the HSCT-TMA population despite consisting
of an acutely ill population with multiple comorbidities. One patient in Study TMA-001
discontinued narsoplimab-wuug, this was likely related to the patient’s underlying disease
diagnosis of AML. In the supportive safety database one healthy volunteer discontinued
narsoplimab-wuug due to an event of SVT. One other patient in the safety database developed
SVT, this was a patient with HSCT-TMA, the patient did not discontinue therapy due to this
event. It does not appear that SVT is an adverse reaction given low rates in the safety
population. The TEAE of granulomatous orchitis and liver insufficiency are discussed in key
review issues for safety.

7.6.1.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
The most common TEAEs (≥30%) in patients with HSCT-TMA in Study TMA-001 were
infections, viral infections, hemorrhage, diarrhea, vomiting, neutropenia, pyrexia, fatigue, and
GVHD. Table 38 provides TEAEs occurring in >10% of patients by MedDRA System Organ
Class (SOC). Infections were the most common TEAE, occurring in 21/28 (75%) of patients.
Further analysis of infections can be found in Section 17 (AESI Infections). Hemorrhage was the
second most common TEAE, this was a combined term. Further analysis of hemorrhage can be
found in key review issues relevant to safety. TEAEs by system organ class are included in Table
128.

7.6.1.7. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Study TMA-001
Infections
The Applicant identified infections as an adverse event of special interest. In patients with
HSCT-TMA, 21 out of 28 (75%) of patients had a TEAE of infection (Tables 40 and 41). The
most common types of infections included viral infections, sepsis, pneumonia and bacterial
infections. Viral infections occurred in 36% of patients, the most common type of viral infection
was cytomegalovirus. Serious TEAE infections occurred in 10 out of 28 (36%) of patients in the
HSCT TMA population (Table 3 and Table 42). The most common serious TEAEs included
sepsis, bacterial infections, viral infections and pneumonia. Safety analysis for infections are
described below. Safety analysis for infections in the entire TMA population is described in
Tables 129 and 130.

7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
7.7.1. Infections Were the Most Common TEAE Reported
in Patients With HSCT-TMA
Issue
Infections were the most common TEAE reported in patients with HSCT-TMA. The Applicant
has proposed to include infections in the Warnings and Precaution section of the USPI.
Background
Infections were identified as an adverse event of special interest by the Applicant. Narsoplimabwuug is a human mAB that binds to and inhibits MASP-2. MASP-2 is an effector enzyme in the
lectin complement pathway, which plays an important role in innate immunity (Fujita et al.
2004). Therefore, there could be a risk of increased infections in patients who receive
narsoplimab-wuug based on its mechanism of action. While there are no approved drugs that
specifically inhibit the lectin pathway, other drugs that inhibit different pathways of the
complement (i.e., eculizumab and ravulizumab) are associated with an increased risk of
infections, especially encapsulated organisms. In the literature, there are several reports of
inherited MASP-2 deficiencies, a very rare primary immunodeficiency disorder. MASP-2
deficiency was first reported in 2003, which described a patient suffering from recurrent
pneumococcal infections, as well as autoimmune and inflammatory conditions (StengaardPedersen et al. 2003). MASP-2 deficiency is considered a primary immunodeficiency, associated
with pyogenic infections, inflammatory lung disease, and autoimmunity (Garcia-Laorden et al.
2020). Since the first description, there have been several (although rare) individual case reports
describing patients with MASP-2 deficiency. The clinical manifestations appear heterogenous
ranging from recurrent pneumonia, cystic fibrosis, skin abscesses, colorectal cancer, pulmonary
tuberculosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). Recently, there were
two reported cases of herpes simplex virus encephalitis reported in patients with MASP-2
deficiency (Bibert et al. 2019). MASP-2 deficiency has also been associated with an increased
risk of fever and neutropenia in pediatric patients receiving chemotherapy (Brodszki et al. 2020).
The p.D120G mutation, in a homozygous state is the primary cause of MASP-2 deficiency
(Sokolowska et al. 2015). Yet, there has also been a significant number healthy individuals
identified with MASP-2 deficiency with no overt symptoms, including patients with the
p.D120G mutation (Thiel et al. 2007; Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). For example, homozygosity
for pD120G may occur in 7-15 out of every 10,000 individuals of European origin (GarciaLaorden et al. 2020). It has been proposed that MASP-2 deficiency is a disease modifier rather
than causing the disease (Thiel et al. 2007; Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). In addition, the lectin
pathway is largely redundant in human defenses and MASP-2 deficiency classification as a
primary immunodeficiency disorder has been questioned recently (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020).
Therefore, the clinical impact, including risk of infections of MASP-2 deficiency remains
unclear.

102
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Assessment
Infections were the most common TEAE, occurring in 21/28 (75%) of patients in Study TMA001. The most common type of infection was viral, occurring in 10/28 (35.7%) of patients and
the most frequently reported type of viral infection reported was CMV. Sepsis, pneumonia,
bacterial infections, bacteremia, lower respiratory tract infections, upper respiratory tract
infections and fungal infections occurred in >10% of patients in HSCT-TMA (Table 129). No
patients experienced an encapsulated organism infection of Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus
influenzae type b (Hib), or Streptococcus pneumoniae during the AE evaluation period. Of note,
one patient had pneumococcal pneumonia, but this occurred in the post AE evaluation period.
Serious infections occurred in 10/28 (35.7%) of patients. Neutropenic sepsis and septic shock
occurred in two patients with HSCT-TMA. The following was reported in one patient;
bacteremia, Escherichia sepsis, fungal infection, gastroenteritis, infection, viral pneumonia, viral
respiratory tract infection, urosepsis, and pneumonia. In total, 7% of HSCT-TMA patients died
due to infections.
Infections were also assessed in the integrated summary of safety (ISS) population which
confirmed the safety findings from the primary safety population.
There was no comparator arm in Study TMA-001 so it is difficult to determine if infection rates
are increased with narsoplimab-wuug compared to a control population. The Applicant provided
data in response to an IR from two hospital centers that were sites in the trial (Table 44 and
Table 45). The infection rates at the site for patients undergoing HSCT appear to be similar to
what was reported in Study TMA-001, although interpretation is very difficult given this data
were reported from only two centers and further data were not provided besides infection
incidence post-allogeneic transplant (i.e., demographics, baseline characteristics, conditioning
regimen, human leukocyte antigen mismatch, source of cells, immunosuppressants,
comorbidities and severity of infections). In addition, these infection rates were not limited to
patients with HSCT-TMA.

Following Transplant

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug

Abbreviations: ASST, Azienda Socio Sanitaria Territoriale; HSCT, hematopoietic stem cell transplant

Patients who undergo a HSCT are at increased risk for developing infections. This remains a
common cause of morbidity and mortality in the post-transplant setting, due to a variety of
factors including, but not limited to, the following: immunosuppressant therapies, multiple
comorbidities, immune system dysfunction, neutropenia, and presence of central line. Therefore,
patients treated with narsoplimab-wuug for HSCT-TMA are already at risk for serious infections.
Based on the mechanism of action of narsoplimab-wuug, as a lectin complement inhibitor that
inhibits MASP-2, it is possible that narsoplimab-wuug further increases the risk of infections as
the lectin pathway is a component of the innate immune response and patients with inherited
MASP-2 deficiency may be at increased risk of infections.
Conclusion
In conclusion, after review of the literature including reports of inherited MASP-2 deficiency, in
addition to a significant number of patients experiencing serious or fatal infections in Study
TMA-001, and the lack of comparative safety, an increased risk of serious infection with
narsoplimab-wuug cannot be ruled out. Therefore, if the product were to be approved, serious
infections should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. Serious
infections should also be further monitored through a postmarketing requirement.

7.7.2. Based on the Mechanism of Action of
Narsoplimab-wuug of Inhibition of MASP-2,
Narsoplimab-wuug May Increase Risk of
Tuberculosis Infections
Issue
In clinical trials, one patient with aHUS developed a TEAE of granulomatous orchitis. Based on
the mechanism of action of narsoplimab-wuug of inhibition of MASP-2, narsoplimab-wuug may
increase risk of tuberculosis infections.
Background
One patient with aHUS developed granulomatous orchitis (biopsy proven) following three doses
of narsoplimab-wuug. All cultures, including mycobacterium, were negative. The event resolved
approximately 2 months after discontinuation of narsoplimab-wuug. Of note, this patient had a
104
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
previous mycobacterium infection several years prior to receiving narsoplimab-wuug. The full
narrative can be found in Section 17.
Assessment
A review of the ADAE dataset did not reveal any additional TEAEs of orchitis, granuloma, or
tuberculosis.
The Applicant and the clinical reviewer conducted a literature review to further understand
whether inhibition of MASP-2 could increase the risk of tuberculosis. Of the 440 patients tested,
two patients had pulmonary tuberculosis with MASP-2 deficiency, as did one healthy volunteer
out of 276 healthy controls (Sokolowska et al. 2015). All three subjects had low MASP-2
concentrations and complex activities. A polymorphism of the MASP-2 gene was associated
with nonsmoker’s susceptibility to tuberculosis (Chen et al. 2015). Therefore, it is possible that
narsoplimab-wuug, by blocking MASP-2, may have contributed to the development of
granulomatous orchitis.
Given the TEAE of granulomatous orchitis in a patient treated with narsoplimab-wuug and
literature suggesting that MASP-2 deficiency may increase risk of tuberculosis infection, there
may be an increased risk of tuberculosis following treatment with narsoplimab-wuug. Therefore,
the USPI should include in the Warnings and Precautions that a patient in clinical trials
developed granulomatous orchitis and recommend that patients be assessed for risk factors of
tuberculosis when considering the risk/benefit profile of narsoplimab-wuug.
Conclusion
In conclusion, one patient in clinical trials developed granulomatous orchitis. While no patients
were reported to have a positive mycobacterium culture in Study MTA-001, the literature
suggests that MASP-2 deficiency may increase susceptibility to tuberculosis. Therefore, one
cannot rule out the possibility of contributing to the TEAE of granulomatous orchitis. Providers
will be informed on this adverse reaction in the Warnings and Precautions of the USPI which
will recommend assessing patients for risk factors. As only a small number of patients with
HSCT-TMA have been treated with narsoplimab-wuug, we would also recommend that this risk
be further assessed in a postmarketing requirement if the product were to be approved.

105
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug

7.7.3. Additional Analysis Conducted to Try to Determine
Whether Narsoplimab-wuug Caused Drug Induced
Liver Injury
Issue
In clinical trials, one patient developed grade 4 liver insufficiency. 5 A clinical reviewer
conducted an additional analysis to try to determine whether narsoplimab-wuug caused druginduced liver injury.
Background
(b) (6)

Patient
was a 28-year-old female with a history of aHUS and end-stage renal
disease requiring hemodialysis, who initiated narsoplimab-wuug per Study HUS-002. While
receiving the narsoplimab-wuug the patient developed a TEAE of grade 4 hepatic failure. The
hepatic failure occurred after eating a meal consisting of fish and while experiencing fever,
hypotension, and hypoxia. In the safety narrative it was noted the patient received hydralazine
from an unknown source, which was characterized as “overdosing on hydralazine.” The patient
did not continue hydralazine during her hospitalization. Viral studies were negative and the
patient did not have a history of consuming alcohol, acetaminophen, herbal supplements, or other
over-the-counter medications. Narsoplimab-wuug was discontinued. The patient recovered from
the liver insufficiency. A more detailed narrative can be found in Section 17.
The Agency sent an IR asking for additional information regarding the described TEAE. The
investigator for the site was not the treating physician so limited details were provided. The
interpretation that the event could have been related to hydralazine was based on the patient
receiving hydralazine from an unknown source for hypertension and the liver insufficiency
resolved in the hospital when the patient was not receiving hydralazine. The investigator also
considered ischemic hepatitis to be a possible cause of the liver insufficiency. The differential for
the etiology of ischemic hepatitis was the patient’s concurrent infection, hypoxia, and/or
hypotension.
Assessment
The clinical reviewer reviewed the narrative to assess for causality. Temporally, narsoplimabwuug could have contributed to the event of liver insufficiency as the patient was actively
receiving narsoplimab-wuug when the event of grade 4 hepatic failure occurred and the TEAE
Grade 4+, Severe: Raised serum aminotransferase or alkaline phosphatase levels and serum
bilirubin ≥2.5 mg/dL and at least one of the following:
5

Prolonged jaundice and symptoms beyond 3 months, or
Signs of hepatic decompensation (INR ≥1.5, ascites, encephalopathy), or
Other organ failure believed to be related to drug induced liver injury

Reference ID: 4873032

Narsoplimab-wuug
resolved with discontinuation of narsoplimab-wuug. At the same time, the patient did receive an
unknown quantity of hydralazine. In the hydralazine USPI, hepatitis is listed as a rare adverse
reaction. Hydralazine is described as a less concern for drug induced liver injury in the drug
induced liver injury rank dataset. While rare, several case reports of hepatotoxicity associated
with hydralazine described in the literature (Stumpf 1991; Harati et al. 2016; Sharma et al. 2018).
The patient also had hypotension at the time of presentation of liver insufficiency, hepatic
ischemia could have also contributed to the TEAE.
In clinical trials with narsoplimab-wuug, there were no other events of grade 4 hepatic failure or

safety signal. Therefore, the clinical reviewer does not recommend that hepatotoxicity be
included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI should the product be approved;
however, the clinical reviewer does recommend that hepatobiliary injury be further assessed in in
future post-marketing assessments.

7.7.4. Second Most Common TEAE (FMQ) Was
Hemorrhage
Issue
The second most common TEAE (FMQ) was hemorrhage.
Background
Given that hemorrhage was the second most common TEAE observed in Study TMA-001, the
Agency asked the Applicant if they had data regarding the frequency of hemorrhage/bleeding
events in the post-transplant setting as a comparison. The Applicant did not have access to this
data. The Agency reviewed the hemorrhage TEAEs in the single arm trial of Study TMA-001
and the ISS dataset.
Assessment
During the FMQ analysis the second most common TEAE in Study TMA-001 was hemorrhage.
Hemorrhage occurred in 12/28 (42.9%) of patients with HSCT-TMA (Table 47). Most TEAEs
were grade 1 or 2. Serious TEAEs of hemorrhage occurred in 2/28 (7.1%) of patients with
HSCT-TMA, this included intracranial hemorrhage and pulmonary alveolar hemorrhage.
In the ISS safety population, 8 out of 36 (22.2%) patients with either aHUS or TTP experienced
a TEAE of hemorrhage (FMQ). Four out of 29 (13.8%) patients with IgA nephropathy (from
study GNP-001) had a TEAE of hemorrhage, 3 of those patients were receiving narsoplimabwuug and 1 was receiving placebo. In total, 3 healthy volunteers had TEAEs of hemorrhage, two
patients were receiving narsoplimab-wuug had had a contusion and one was receiving placebo
and had a vessel site puncture site hematoma.
In the ISS safety population, there were two patients with serious TEAEs of hemorrhage. This
included one patient with aHUS treated on HUS-002 with a fatal event of cerebral hematoma,
this patient had a recent history of subdural hemorrhage and thrombocytopenia prior to the event,
108
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
and thrombocytopenia was ongoing at the time of the cerebral hemorrhage. The other patient
also had aHUS and was treated on TMA-001 and had a grade 4 TEAE of postprocedural
hemorrhage following a kidney biopsy.
In reviewing the literature, hemorrhage does not seem to be described as a component of the
phenotype of patients with inherited MASP-2 deficiency.

Conclusion
Hemorrhage is known to occur in patients in the post-transplant setting due to a variety of

HSCT-TMA. Causality is difficult to assess, given that Study TMA-001 was a single arm trial
and all patients had thrombocytopenia. In addition, there may be deficiencies in the coagulation
cascade and fibrinogen levels in this seriously ill population. In reviewing the ISS safety
database there does not appear to be an increased risk of hemorrhage due to narsoplimab-wuug,
but this database is small and patients with other forms of TMA had thrombocytopenia.
Hemorrhage will be included in the Adverse Reactions section of the USPI given the high
frequency of bleeding events.

7.7.5. Autoimmunity Has Rarely Been Associated With
MASP-2 Deficiency; Given That Narsoplimab-wuug
Inhibits MASP-2, There Is a Theoretical Risk of
Autoimmunity
Issue
Autoimmunity has rarely been associated with MASP-2 deficiency. Given that narsoplimabwuug inhibits MASP-2, there is a theoretical risk of autoimmunity.

109
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Background
Complement has an important role in autoimmune disease, mostly associated with components
of the classical complement pathway. It is not clear if there is an association between
deficiencies in components of the lectin pathway and autoimmune disease development. MASP2 deficiency has been described as a primary immunodeficiency disorder characterized by
infections, inflammatory lung disease and autoimmunity (Bousfiha et al. 2018; Garcia-Laorden
et al. 2020). In the novel case of MASP-2 deficiency the patient had autoimmune manifestations
but was also noted to have low levels of C1q, C3, and C4 (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). A case
report describing a patient with Crohn’s disease and MASP-2 deficiency was reported (Hejazi
and Hasosah 2019).
Assessment
The entire ISS dataset was assessed for autoimmune diseases. There were no apparent
autoimmune diseases that occurred during time period reviewed for the study. Table 48 describes
preferred terms captured under immune system disorders (captured all TEAEs in the AE and post
AE evaluation period). Most TEAEs were related to GVHD, which is not unexpected in a posttransplant patient population. Hypersensitivity reactions occurred in three patients and all were
reported as unrelated to narsoplimab-wuug. Two patients had an allergic reaction to plasma
therapy and one had an allergic reaction to Tazocin.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
A population PK model was used to assess the effect of covariates on narsoplimab-wuug
exposure. The covariates that affect narsoplimab-wuug exposure included patients, body weight,
serum albumin, and presence of ADAs. Compared to healthy subjects, patients had a 20% lower
maximum elimination rate (Vmax). Albumin, particularly at high levels, can result in lower
narsoplimab-wuug clearance and greater exposure. Presence of ADA increased Vmax by 143%,
decreased AUCss by 22%, and decreased Cmin,ss by 52% (Section 14.3.2). The effect of weight is
discussed in the section below and in the Pharmacometrics review (Section 14.3).
The covariates that did not affect narsoplimab-wuug exposure included age, race, and sex.
Body Weight
The Applicant’s population PK model showed that body weight was a significant covariate for
narsoplimab-wuug clearance and, therefore, proposed, dosing of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug, up
to a maximum of 370 mg, administered by intravenous infusion once weekly. The Office of
Clinical Pharmacology assessment concluded that the proposed dosing of 4 mg/kg of
narsoplimab-wuug capped to 370 mg is appropriate, as it provides comparable steady-state
exposure (area under the concentration-time curve over the dosing interval: AUCss, maximum
concentration: Cmax, and trough concentration: Cmin) across all body weight ranges (Figure 27,
Section 14.3.2).

111
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Figure 11. Predicted Narsoplimab-wuug AUCss, Cmax,ss, and Cmin,ss for 4 mg/kg and 370 mg in
Patients

Source: FDA Reviewer

The reviewer’s PK simulations demonstrated that capping narsoplimab-wuug dosing to 370 mg,
particularly in heavier subjects, provides consistent and comparable narsoplimab-wuug exposure
(mainly Cmax,ss and AUC,ss) to subjects with body weight below 92.5 kg and receiving 4 mg/kg
of narsoplimab-wuug. Conversely, the administration of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug dose in
subjects with body weight above 100 kg or administration of a fixed dose of 370 mg in subjects
with body weight below 70 kg is expected to result in relatively higher exposure compared to the
studied and current proposed dosing.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Plasma Therapy/Plasma Exchange
The objective of plasma therapy (PLT), specifically plasma exchange (PE), in HSCT-TMA
patients is to remove thrombogenic and antifibrinolytic molecules and to replenish
anticoagulants and profibrinolytics with new plasma. The process removes large molecules such
(b) (4)
as proteins and monoclonal antibodies from the systemic circulation.
(b) (4)
that an additional dose of 2 mg/kg
narsoplimab-wuug be administered up to 3 times weekly. However, the Applicant did not
provide a justification for the selected supplemental narsoplimab-wuug dose of 2 mg/kg. The
effect of PLT on narsoplimab-wuug clearance (CL) and its overall exposure was not investigated
in a dedicated clinical study, and the Applicant did not attempt to use a population PK modeling
approach to assess the effect of PLT on narsoplimab-wuug exposure or justify the need for the
selected supplemental dose of narsoplimab-wuug post-PE.

112
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Five patients received PE during narsoplimab-wuug treatment, and were used to evaluate the
effect of PE on narsoplimab-wuug PK. The median duration of PE in these 5 patients was 1.8 h
(range: 1.4 h to 2.9 h). Following an information request (see Section 14.3.5), the Applicant’s
post hoc analysis estimated that PLT increased narsoplimab-wuug CL by 37.1% based upon their
population PK modeling and concluded that due to the modest effect size and short duration of
PLT, no clinically meaningful effects of PLT on narsoplimab-wuug exposure could be identified
(b) (4)
. The Office of Clinical
Pharmacology’s review of the population PK analysis found that PE increased narsoplimabwuug CL by an average of 47%. However, the estimated effect on narsoplimab-wuug CL could
not be precisely estimated, with a 95%CI of the mean effect ranging from 24% to 124%. The
difficulty in reliably estimating the effect of PE on CL using the population PK model is most
likely related to the lack of informative PK data. There was no PK sampling performed
concurrently immediately pre- and post-PE amongst the 5 patients who received PE during
narsoplimab-wuug treatment.
The reviewer’s PK simulations showed that PE does not seem to have a relevant effect on
narsoplimab-wuug PK (see Section 14.3 Pharmacometrics review for more details). However,
the population PK modelling was not considered reliable for predicting the effect of plasma
therapy on drug exposure. Therefore, the need as well as the strength and frequency of
supplemental dosing post-PE could not be reliably evaluated.
The Applicant and the Clinical Pharmacology review team have shown that PE increases
narsoplimab-wuug clearance; therefore, supplementation of narsoplimab-wuug at 2 mg/kg
following each PE appears to be reasonable. The supplemental dose of 2 mg/kg is supported by
safety established in the clinical trials. However, it is unclear if the supplemental dose and dosing
regimen is optimal. To identify a precise dose and dosing regimen for narsoplimab-wuug
supplementation following PE, the Office of Clinical Pharmacology review team recommends
that Applicant conduct a dedicated clinical study to methodically assess the effect of PE on
narsoplimab-wuug clearance and to identify a dose and dosing regimen that can replace
narsoplimab-wuug that is removed through PE.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The initial application for approval of narsoplimab-wuug is limited to adults. Pediatric patients
were not included in the Phase 2 Study TMA-001. Considering multiple interactions between the
complement system and the immune system and the role of complement in some forms of bone
remodeling, a juvenile toxicity study in mice was conducted to support future pediatric
development. Mice were administered narsoplimab IV twice a week at doses of 0, 50, 150, or
300 mg/kg for 8 injections during the preweaning period (postnatal day 4-28) followed by
subcutaneous (SC) once a week for 9 injections during the postweaning period (postnatal day 3591).
Substantial (>70%) and similar inhibition of the complement lectin pathway was observed at all
dose levels and was sustained beyond 28 days after the last dose, verifying that all dose levels
tested were pharmacodynamically active.

113
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
No treatment-related effects were observed on physical development parameters (vaginal
opening and preputial separation), behavioral performance, or bone dimensions. Treatmentrelated findings included non-adverse increases in neutrophil counts, eosinophilic infiltration in
the bone marrow, injection site, and inguinal skin, and moderately elevated IgE plasma levels.
These changes showed reversibility and did not affect antibody response to antigen in a T-cell
dependent antibody response assessment. The exposure multiple at the NOAEL is 2X MRHD
based on estimated AUC(0­168h).

III. Appendices
12. Summary of Regulatory History
The following provides a regulatory overview for biologics license application (BLA) 761152,
product name OMS721 (also referred to as narsoplimab-wuug). OMS721 is a monoclonal
antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2, the
effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement, inhibiting the inflammatory and
prothrombotic responses to endothelial injury. The proposed indication is hematopoietic stem
cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (HSCT-TMA). OMS721 is stored as a
single dose yellow/brown solution in a vial to be administered as an intravenous injection.
Currently, no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved products are indicated for the
treatment of HSCT-TMA.
On January 23, 2014, the Agency received the submission for an investigational new drug (IND)
application under section 505(i) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act for IND 120524.
Prior to the submission a Type B pre-IND meeting was conducted with Omeros Corporation on
January 23, 2014, for OMS721 under PIND 120524. The Applicant obtained Agency advice on
the development plans for the investigation of OMS721 for inhibition of complement mediated
thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) under Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act. On
April 2, 2014, Omeros Corporation was advised that the 30-day, safety review of the application
had concluded and that the study may proceed with their proposed clinical investigation for the
treatment of chemotherapy induced neutropenia with nonhold comments. On September 8, 2014,
an institutional review board waiver was granted under 21 CFR Part 56 for the use of OMS721
for the conduct of Study TMA-001 in Germany.
A type B, end-of-phase 2 meeting was held in person on April 24, 2018, to discuss a Phase 3
program design and the clinical studies to support submission of a BLA for OMS721 in the
treatment of HSCT-TMA. The Division stated that the Applicant should provide an explanation
for the following:
•

Why OMS721 demonstrated little efficacy in patients with thrombotic thrombocytopenia
purpura (TTP) or aHUS.

•

Why the biweekly or three times a week dosing of OMS721 was given to the
compassionate-use patient compared to the weekly dosing used in Study TMA-001.

•

Why the volume of distribution of OMS721 (0.071 L/kg) is similar to that of blood
volume (~0.08 L/kg). The Division requested the Applicant address if changes in volume
status (such as hypervolemia due to therapy interventions) may have an impact on the
pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) and/or efficacy of OMS721 in patients with
HSCT-TMA.

On April 26, 2018, breakthrough therapy designation was granted for OMS721 for the treatment
of patients with HSCT-TMA. Breakthrough therapy designation was followed by a July 31,

117
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
2018, Type B – breakthrough therapy meeting. Discussions from this meeting included the
following:
•

The Division stated that multiple hurdles would need to be overcome using a chart-based
historical control and recommended that the Applicant use TMA response rate as primary
endpoint in a single-arm trial with duration of response as a secondary endpoint. The
Applicant noted the challenges with a TMA response rate endpoint due to confounding
effects of transplantation and other concomitant medical conditions.

•

The Applicant discussed their plan for conducting a historical control using chart review.
The Agency expressed several concerns, to include: ensuring matching populations,
contemporaneous review of charts and study patients, data integrity, time involved,
endpoint definition, and various aspects of the propensity score analysis.

•

The Agency stated that they could not agree with the Applicant’s proposal to use a chart
based historical control until further review and internal discussion. The Division
recommended that the Applicant submit a detailed proposal of the chart based historical
control for the Agency to review and provide feedback.

On January 29, 2019, a Type B meeting was held. Discussion at this meeting included the
following:
•

The Division stated that the decision on whether to accept a proposed surrogate endpoint
is based on the scientific support for that endpoint and that confirmatory trials are usually
ongoing at the time of an application submission.

•

The Division recommended that the Applicant assemble the data to define a responder
endpoint definition for both surrogate and clinical benefit endpoints.

•

The Division recommended that the Applicant include the scientific rationale for
endpoints selected. Additionally, the Applicant should prespecify the endpoints to reduce
or minimize potential bias and include discussions of why some response outcomes are
excluded from responder endpoint definition.

On March 2, 2019, a Type A meeting was held to discuss response-based clinical endpoints for a
registrational trial. Discussion point(s) for this meeting included:
•

The Agency expressed concerns that the magnitude of change in platelet count based on
their current proposal may not be meaningful.

•

The Agency recommended that the Applicant prespecify an endpoint definition for
platelets based on proportion of patients who achieve a prespecified threshold.

•

The Agency also recommended that the Applicant review data with their drug to
determine appropriate threshold for a proposal.

On March 12, 2019, preliminary comments were issued for a type B, chemistry, manufacturing,
and controls (CMC) meeting. The Meeting request was then withdrawn by the Applicant on
March 15, 2019, citing acknowledgment and understanding of The Division’s preliminary
comments.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional
Information and Assessment
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted
Under the IND
13.1.1. Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
•

Narsoplimab-wuug is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding
lectin-associated serine protease-2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway
of complement, see Figure 12.

119
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
•

MASP-2 also reportedly contributes to cleavage of prothrombin to thrombin independent
of downstream complement activation (Krarup et al. 2007); see orange arrows/boxes,
Figure 12. Narsoplimab was not tested against this reported MASP-2 activity.

•

In vitro binding studies using plasmon resonance and enzyme-linked immunosorbent
assay (ELISA) showed high affinity binding of OMS721 with human MASP-2 (6273pM). Intact bivalent OMS721 demonstrated 100-fold greater binding affinity compared
to a monovalent Fab indicating that functional bivalency is an important contributor to
high-affinity binding, see Table 51.

•

OMS721 exhibited a selectivity ratio of >8,000-fold for MASP-2 relative to other serine
proteases of the complement system (C1r, C1s, MASP-1, and MASP-3).

•

In vitro functional assays demonstrated that OMS721 inhibited human lectin pathwaydependent C3, C4 (IC50 2.5nM), and membrane attack complex (MAC) (IC50 1nM)
activation without affecting the classical and alternative pathway-dependent MAC
activation at concentrations up to 100nM.

Figure 12. Proposed Mechanisms of Action of OMS721

Source: Excerpted from the Applicant’s submission

120
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Table 51. Bivalent OMS721 mAb Displays Higher Affinity Than OMS721-Related Fab

Source: See below

Abbreviations: ELISA, enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay; Fab, antigen-binding fragment; ka, association rate constant; kd,
dissociation rate constant; KD, dissociation equilibrium constant; MASP-2, mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2

•

OMS721 displayed functional activity in mouse, rabbit, and cynomolgus monkey, with
potencies 3.8, 2.7 and 10-fold less than human. No functional activity was observed in
rats. Functional potency was determined from ELISA and flow cytometric measurement
of C4 and C3 activation in serum from each species tested against mannan immobilized
on a substrate.

•

An ex-vivo and an in vivo pharmacodynamic study showed that narsoplimab reduced
platelet/endothelial adherence, and delayed the initiation of thrombus formation and time
to complete vessel occlusion providing evidence of its mode of action in conditions with a
microvascular thrombotic component, see Section 5.1. Inhibition of the lectin pathway by
narsoplimab is evident from the reduction in C5b-9 deposition on endothelial cells;
however, other complement and coagulation pathways remain active which ultimately led
to similar levels of microvascular occlusion in the murine TMA study regardless of
treatment with narsoplimab.

•

The minimum pharmacodynamically active dose of OMS721 in mice and monkeys was
5 mg/kg and 0.5 mg/kg, intravenously (IV), respectively. There was a consistent
correlation in both species between OMS721 serum concentrations and percent inhibition
of lectin pathway activity. Based on PK-PD response modelling, an in vivo EC50 value for
lectin pathway inhibition in cynomolgus monkey was estimated to be approximately
7 µg/mL.

Secondary Pharmacology
•

A mouse tail bleeding assay was conducted to evaluate whether MASP-2 inhibition
affects hemostasis. A recombinant mouse monoclonal antibody, AbD04211, which binds
to mouse MASP-2 and blocks the lectin pathway of complement in mice, was used in this
study. MASP-2 blockade did not significantly affect the bleeding time in mice assessed
after a standard cut applied to the tail. This result suggests that any contribution of MASP2 to cleavage of prothrombin, as reported in the literature, is marginal and insufficient to
alter the hemostatic or thrombotic responses by this mechanism.

Safety Pharmacology
•

Cardiovascular and respiratory safety pharmacology assessment in monkeys and CNS
safety assessments in mice were incorporated into 28-day repeat dose toxicology studies.
121

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
•

OMS712 showed no effect on cardiovascular and respiratory parameters in cynomolgus
monkeys or on neurobehavioral parameters in mice.

13.1.2. Toxicology
Study Number/ Title: 1235-013: A 28-Day Subcutaneous and Intravenous Toxicity Study in
Mice With a 56-Day Recovery Period
10, 50, 150 mg/kg/week subcutaneous (SC), 10 mg/kg/week IV
Exposure margins
0.8, 2 and 4-times (SC) and 1-times (IV) the clinical dose of 4 mg/kg/week IV, based on AUC
Key study findings
•

Injection site reactions consisting of minimal to mild subacute/chronic inflammation were
observed in all groups, including control, but were of greater severity in the dosed groups.

•

The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) with IV route is 10 mg/kg given once
weekly for 4 weeks.

•

The NOAEL with SC route is conservatively 50 mg/kg given once weekly for 4 weeks,
based on potential adverse hepatic effects:
— Increases in AST (2-fold) and ALT (2.6-fold) in SC treated females (at 150 mg/kg)
relative to controls at the end of treatment period (largely a result of changes observed
in female animal no. 4509)
— Liver histopathology (necrosis) in animals receiving 150 mg/kg SC (although liver
necrosis was also observed in 2 control females).

Liver
Minimal liver necrosis was observed in 2 control females and with

TK was evaluated on Days 1 and 22 with blood samples collected

compared to SC administration.

ratios ranged from 1.26 to 2.03).

concentration of 682 ng/mL on Day 22 (72 hour), which did not
affect interpretation of the results.
Terminal half-lives after SC and IV administration ranged from 216
to 321 hours.

fourth dose). Of the 532 samples collected, 140 were screened for
the presence of antidrug antibodies (ADA). 63 samples screened
positive, of which 26 samples were confirmed to be positive for
ADA (including 3 samples from the control group).
Samples were collected from designated animals on Days 1 (0, 2,
and 24 hours post-dose), 22 (2 and 24 hours post-dose), 49 (648
hours) and 84 (1488 hours after the fourth dose). The percent
MASP-2 inhibition was generally low (if detectable) in control
animals, and in 10 mg/kg SC animals, on Day 1. By Day 22 most
of the samples from animals treated with 150 mg/kg SC and
10 mg/kg IV exhibited MASP-2 inhibition on the order of ≥90%.
The MASP-2 inhibition was considerably decreased in the
recovery samples, but the effect was not entirely absent.

Source: Study 1235-013
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; HD, high dose; IV, intravenous(ly); MASP-2,
mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2; SC, subcutaneous; TK, toxicokinetic

Study Number/ Title: 1235-012: A 28-Day Subcutaneous and Intravenous Toxicity Study in
Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 56-Day Recovery Period
10, 50, 150 SC mg/kg/week SC, 10 mg/kg/week IV
Exposure margins
4, 12 and 18-times (SC) and 7-times (IV) the clinical dose of 4 mg/kg/week IV, based on AUC
Key study findings
•

Test article-related findings were seen at the injection sites at all SC doses in females in a
nondose-dependent manner. One female showed mild edema and minimal adventitial
necrosis of the vessel at the IV injection site.

•

Minimal increases in ALT and AST levels at 150 mg/kg SC without microscopic
correlates in the liver.

•

The NOAEL in monkeys is 10 mg/kg given IV and 150 mg/kg given SC once weekly for
4 weeks.

Toxicokinetics

Major Findings
TK was evaluated on Days 1 and 22 with samples collected at pre
dose, 0.083, 0.5, 2, 6, 24, 72, and 168 hours after dosing, and on
Days 35, 49, 63, and 84.
Exposures to OMS721 (Cmax and AUC0­168 hour) were similar in
males and females on Days 1 and 22.
The increases in exposures to SC OMS721 (Cmax and AUC0­168hr)
were slightly less than dose proportional to increases in dose.
Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC0­168hr) exhibited minimal
accumulation following repeated SC (mean accumulation ratios
ranged from 1.54 to 1.96)
At 10 mg/kg, systemic exposure was greater following IV slow
bolus as compared to SC administration.
Mean bioavailability was ~42.6 and 54.4% on days 1 and 22,
respectively.
Mean t1/2 calculated for recovery animals following SC and for all
animals following IV bolus of OMS721 ranged from 120 to 202
hours.
All control group sample results were below the lower limit of
quantification (LLOQ, <391 ng/mL).

Pharmacodynamic activity

In the IV dose group (10 mg/kg/week), ADAs were detected in 3 of
10 monkeys, but when present corresponded with decreased
serum OMS721 concentration. In the SC groups, there was no
clear effect of ADA status on serum OMS721 concentration.

pathway activity (surrogate for MASP-2 activity) was correlated to
OMS721 serum concentrations. Maximal inhibition was evident in
samples taken from animals given 150 mg/kg SC and 10 mg/kg
IV.

Study Number/ Title: 8299929/A Repeat-Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in
Cynomolgus Monkeys Administered OMS721 by Subcutaneous and/or Intravenous Injection
Once Weekly for 26 Weeks with a 10-Week Recovery
50, 150 and 300 mg/kg/week SC, 150 mg/kg/week IV
Exposure margins
18, 26 and 38-times (SC) and 43-times (IV) the clinical dose of 4 mg/kg/week IV, based on AUC
Key study findings
•

One female in 150 mg/kg/week IV group showed pronounced decreases in total protein
and albumin at the end of the treatment period which became more severe during the
recovery period. This animal presented clinical pathology changes consistent with
inflammation (mildly increased white blood cell and absolute neutrophil counts) at the end
of the recovery period.

•

The NOAEL was considered to be 300 mg/kg/week SC due to the reversibility of serum
protein changes and injection site inflammatory changes at this dose.

Toxicokinetics

Major Findings
A nondose dependent minimal decrease was observed in total
protein (-2.7 to -9.5% compared with control) and albumin (-4.4 to
-11% compared with control) on Days 91 and 183 of the dosing
phase in females at >50 mg/kg IV or SC.
1 female (I03200) in 150 mg/kg IV group showed the most
pronounced decreases in total protein (-7 to -9% compared to
predose) and albumin (-16 to -28%) on Days 91 and 183 of the
dosing phase which became more severe during the recovery
phase (-28% total protein and -53% albumin). This female also
showed increased WBC and absolute neutrophil counts (3-fold of
predose values) at the end of the recovery phase. However,
microscopic evidence of inflammation was not observed in the
tissues.
All other animals showed reversibility in serum protein changes at
the end of the recovery phase.
No effect
No effect
No effect
OMS721-related minimal to slight mononuclear cell infiltrates were
observed at SC injection sites of most animals at all doses.
Injection sites reaction showed no dose-response relationship to
incidence or severity and showed reversibility at the end of
recovery period.
No OMS721-related microscopic findings were seen in animals
given 150 mg/kg IV.
TK was evaluated on Days 1, 85 and 176 with samples collected
at pre dose, 0.083, 0.5, 2, 6, 24, 72, and 168 hours post-dose.
Additionally, samples were collected from all dose groups 10
minutes predose and 72 hours post-dose on Days 29, 57, 120 and
148 and during the recovery phase on Days 28, 56 and 71.
No remarkable sex differences in OMD721 exposures were
observed. OMS721 exposure increased less than dose
proportionally with SC route. Accumulation with SC route ranged
from 1.2 to 4.85. Accumulation was minimal with IV route (0.91.8). Mean half-life values ranged from 191-367 hours with SC
and 208-300 hours with IV route on Day 176. Mean bioavailability
was ~40, 90 and 60% on days 1, 85 and 176, respectively.

Study Number/Title: 20050868 / NARSOPLIMAB: A Fertility and General Reproduction
Toxicity Study by Subcutaneous and Intravenous Injection in Mice
GLP compliance: Yes
Key study findings:
•

Body weight gain was 71% lower compared to controls during the overall premating
period in 300 mg/kg (IV) females.

•

Significant increase in percent abnormal, detached head and no-head sperm parameters
occurred in 300 mg/kg (SC/IV) males at 5.4-times the exposure at the MRHD with no
direct effects on fertility. MASP-2 is not known to be expressed in testicular tissue
(Atlas).

effect on fertility.

•

Inhibition of the lectin pathway occurred at all doses through the end of the study.

Table 62. Observations and Results, Fertility and Early Embryonic Development (FEED) Study in
Mice
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
No narsoplimab–related mortality occurred in the study.
Clinical signs
Piloerection was observed in single occasions in 3/25 females receiving 300 mg/kg
IV on SD 15; one of those female mice also presented ptosis (dropping eyelid) on
SD 1.
Body weights
Males: Mean body weight gains for Days 1 through 57 compared to control males
were +13, +13, +23, and -6% in the LD, MD, and HD (SC) and in the HD (IV),
respectively.

Sperm evaluations
(motility, density,
and morphology)

Females:
Days 1 to 15, premating period: Mean body weight gains during the overall
premating period interval of SD 1 through 15 compared to control females were 29, +43, +57, and -71% in the LD, MD, and HD (SC) and in the HD (IV),
respectively.
GD 0 through GD 13: Mean maternal body weight gains during GD 0 through 13
compared to controls were +2, -4, -1, and +4% in the LD, MD, and HD (SC) and in
the HD (IV), respectively.
The mean numbers of normal sperm were reduced or significantly reduced (↓1.9%,
↓4.6%) and the mean percentages of abnormal sperm (↑47%, ↑77%) and mean
numbers of sperm with detached heads (↑57%, ↑84%) and no heads (↑47%, ↑76%)
were increased or significantly increased (p≤0.05 or p≤0.01) compared to controls.
Except for the mean number of sperm with no heads at 300 mg/kg SC, these
values were also outside the range of historical control data for the Testing Facility.

Embryo Fetal Development
Study Number/Title: 20050865 / Narsoplimab: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study by
Subcutaneous and Intravenous Injection in Pregnant Mice
GLP compliance: Yes
Reviewer’s Comment
Subcutaneous administration provided the more relevant data as it utilized the clinical route of
administration and achieved a modest ~3-fold multiple of clinical AUC exposure. The
intravenous route led to maternal and fetal toxicity associated with a very high Cmax and AUC
relative to clinical exposure and to mice from SC dosing.
Key study findings:
Subcutaneous administration:
•

There were no clear drug-related adverse findings in dams or fetuses with subcutaneous
administration.

•

The maternal and fetal NOAEL at 300 mg/kg SC provided ~3-times the exposure at the
MRHD, narsoplimab based on extrapolated AUC0­168h.

133
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Intravenous administration
•

Lower gestational weight gain and a single early decedent (gestation day (GD) 15) found
with a litter of 13 dead fetuses suggests maternal toxicity was present at the 300 mg/kg IV
dose (~22-times AUC and ~150-times Cmax relative to MRHD)

•

Embryofetal loss is indicated by findings of 13 dead fetuses from the early decedent
female, a ~2-fold higher percent postimplantation loss, and a lower number of live fetuses.

•

Surviving fetuses had lower body weight (-5.1% males, -3.8% females) and an increased
number with cleft palate which exceeded the historical control data.

Table 68. Observations and Results, Embryo-Fetal Developmental (EFD) Study in Mice
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
A 50 mg/kg SC female delivered on GD 18 and it was
subsequently euthanized. This female received all 4 doses of
narsoplimab, gained weight during the study, did not present
clinical signs prior to parturition neither abnormalities at necropsy
examination. The litter consisted of 15 live fetuses and 1 early
resorption in utero. One fetus presented a supernumerary rib, a
common variation.

Clinical signs
Body weights

Necropsy findings
Cesarean section data
Laparohysterectomy Evaluations

A 300 mg/kg IV female was found dead after dose administration
on GD 15. This dam received all 4 doses of narsoplimab, gained
weight during the study, did not present clinical signs prior to death
neither abnormalities at necropsy examination. The litter consisted
of 13 dead fetuses and one late resorption in utero. The viability of
fetuses at the time of early maternal death could not be
determined.
No narsoplimab–related clinical signs occurred on study.
Mean maternal body weight gains during the interval of GD 6
through 18 were +5%, +7%, +12%, and -5% compared to controls
in the 50, 150, and 300 mg/kg SC and the 300 mg/kg IV dose
group, respectively. Transient decreased maternal body weight
gains were observed in 300 mg/kg IV individual dams at 5.8-times
the exposure at the MRHD.
No narsoplimab–related maternal gross findings.
There were no differences on the number and litter averages for
corpora lutea and number of implantations; however, pregnant
mice receiving narsoplimab at 300 mg/kg IV delivered a lower
number of live fetuses and the mean percent postimplantation loss
was increased ~2-fold.

Parameters
Major Findings
Toxicokinetics
Systemic exposure to narsoplimab, as measured by Cmax and AUC(0­72), generally increased with
increasing dose from 50 to 300 mg/kg SC but the increases were less than dose-proportional and
exposure values decreased on GD 15 compared to GD 6. Narsoplimab exposure after 300 mg/kg IV
administration was at least 7-fold increase compared to the SC administration. There were no signs of
accumulation after four doses.
Table 72. Summary of OMS721 Toxicokinetic Parameters in Pregnant Female Crl:CD1(ICR) Mice
(Excerpted From Submission)

Antidrug Antibody Assessments
No ADA positive samples were found
Pharmacodynamic Evaluation – Inhibition of the Lectin Pathway
Narsoplimab–related inhibition (range 49.3% to 97.8%) of the complement lectin pathway activation
occurred at all SC doses and the IV dose. The inhibition was observed at all timepoints tested and lasted
until the end of the study (72 hours post last injection).

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

Narsoplimab-wuug
Study Number 20050867 / NARSOPLIMAB: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study by
Subcutaneous and Intravenous Injection in Pregnant Rabbits
GLP compliance: Yes
Reviewer’s Comment
A staggered dosing design was employed to accommodate a robust antidrug antibody (ADA)
response identified in preliminary studies. Lectin inhibition by narsoplimab was confirmed in the
course of the definitive study. Subcutaneous administration provided the more relevant data as it
utilized the clinical route of administration and achieved an ~8-fold multiple of clinical AUC
exposure. The intravenous route did not result in clear maternal toxicity but was potentially
associated with an increase in postimplantation loss associated with a very high Cmax and AUC
relative to clinical exposure.
Key study findings:
Subcutaneous administration:
•

There were no clear drug-related adverse findings in dams or fetuses with subcutaneous
administration. Transient reductions in maternal weight gain were not considered as
indicative of maternal toxicity by the reviewer.

•

The maternal and fetal NOAEL at 150 mg/kg SC provided ~8-times the exposure at the
MRHD, narsoplimab based on extrapolated AUC0­168h.

Intravenous administration:
•

Increased postimplantation loss occurred on GD 7/10 (+88%) and GD 11/14 (+41%) but
not on GD 15/18 at ~91- times the exposure at the MRHD (AUC). This finding is
consistent with a drug action during early gestation.

Table 75. Observations and Results, Embryo-Fetal Developmental (EFD) Study in Rabbits
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
Cohort 1 Dosed GD 7 and 10
A 50 mg/kg/dose SC aborted on GD 23 and then euthanized.
Litter of 14 conceptuses (14 empty implantation sites were
observed due to abortion). The Applicant considered this
abortion not narsoplimab–related since it occurred at the low
dose, was not dose-dependent, and the single event was within
historical data for the Testing Facility.
Cohort 2 Dosed GD 11 and 14
No abortions or mortality occurred.

Clinical signs

Cohort 3 Dosed GD 15 and 18
A 150 mg/kg SC dam aborted on GD 28 and then euthanized.
Litter of 9 live and 1 dead fetuses. All fetuses appeared normal
at gross external examination. The dead fetus had a small right
eye, a dilated aortic arch, a ventricular septal defect proximal to
the semilunar valves, and a narrow pulmonary trunk at soft
tissue examination. It also had an enlarged frontal suture, fused
4th and 5th sternebra, and a misshapen 1st sternebra. One of the
live fetuses had unossified pubis at skeletal examination. All
other fetuses appeared normal at soft tissue and skeletal
examination. The Applicant considered this abortion as not
narsoplimab–related since the occurrence was not dosedependent and the single event was within historical data for
the Testing Facility.
Cohort 1 Dosed GD 7 and 10
The 50 mg/kg SC rabbit that aborted on GD 23 presented
ungroomed fur (GD 17 through 23), absent feces (GD 18
through 20), decreased fecal output (GD 21 through 23), and
aborted red tissue and red liquid in the cage pan (GD 23).
Narsoplimab–related clinical signs occurred at 150 mg/kg SC
and at 300 mg/kg IV and included soft feces (MD 4/20; HD
3/20), liquid feces (HD 2/20), reduced fecal size (MD & HD
1/20), decreased fecal output (LD 1/20; MD 7/20; HD 2/20), and
absent feces (LD & HD single dams). These clinical signs
occurred after dose administration and were transient (up to 4
days). Decreased fecal output and absent feces were also
observed in one 50 mg/kg SC dam.
Cohort 2 Dosed GD 11 and 14
No narsoplimab–related clinical signs occurred.
Cohort 3 Dosed GD 15 and 18
No adverse clinical signs were observed in the 150 mg/kg SC
rabbit that aborted on GD 28. No narsoplimab-related clinical
signs occurred in other rabbits.

Major Findings
Cohort 1 Dosed GD 7 and 10
A single 150 mg/kg SC dam presented both kidneys with clear,
fluid-filled cysts that were too numerous to count ranging in size
from pinpoint to 0.4 cm x 0.5 cm x 0.2 cm. The cysts extended
into the renal parenchyma. This gross lesion did not affect
pregnancy (9 live fetuses) and was not considered related to
OMS721 based on a single incidence in the absence of doserelationship.
Cohort 2 Dosed GD 11 and 14
A single incidence of reduced to a ligament of the right uterine
horn in a 50 mg/kg SC dam was not considered narsoplimab–
related based on the single incidence and lack of doserelationship.

Laparohysterectomy evaluations

Cohort 3 Dosed GD 15 and 18
No maternal gross observations occurred in cohort 3.
Cohort 1 Dosed GD 7 and 10
The number and percentage of dams with any resorptions was
increased at the HD (9/47 vs. Control 4/20) but it did not affect
the mean number of live fetuses in narsoplimab–treated
rabbits.
The mean percent implantation loss was increased at the
50 mg/kg SC (+124%) and 150 mg/kg IV (+88%) but it was
lower than control at the 150 mg/kg SC (-33%); the relation of
implantation loss to narsoplimab administration is unclear
based on the lack of dose-response for SC administration but
the increased postimplantation loss at 150 mg/kg IV was
considered related to narsoplimab.
Cohort 2 Dosed GD 11 and 14
Narsoplimab–treated rabbits presented a better profile
compared to control except for an increased mean percent
postimplantation loss (+41%) at 150 mg/kg IV that was
considered narsoplimab–related.

Necropsy findings
Offspring

Cohort 3 Dosed GD 15 and 18
Narsoplimab–treated rabbits presented a better profile
compared to control except for lower number of females
pregnant at 150 mg/kg SC (18/20) and a lower number of live
fetuses at the 50 mg/kg SC (153, -20.7%) and 150 mg/kg SC
(167, -13.5%) compared to control that corresponded with
lower number of corpora lutea (177 and 180 vs. 216) and
implantations (162 and 172 vs. 208) although values were
within the historical control data range.
Fetal Malformations/ Variations (external, visceral, skeletal)
No narsoplimab–related gross external, soft tissue, or skeletal
abnormalities occurred at any dose in all Cohorts in rabbits.
All ossification site averages were similar across all dose
groups, including controls.

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Parameters
Major Findings
Toxicokinetics
Increases in exposure were less than dose-proportional after the first dose and close to dose
proportional after the second dose. The mean exposures were comparable among the 3 TK cohorts at
both SC dose levels. OMS721 exposure after 150 mg/kg IV administration was ≥5-fold increases
compared to the SC administration. Exposure after the second dose increased only in TK cohort 2.
Narsoplimab half-life of 57.7 hours was estimated for the SC route from the first TK cohort and between
14.5 and 22.4 hours for the IV route from all three TK cohorts. Bioavailability (%F) based on AUC(0­72)
ranged from 6.77 to 9.27% following the first 150 mg/kg SC dose and ranged from 8.59 to 18.7%
following the second SC dose.

From Submission)

Narsoplimab-wuug
Parameters
Antidrug antibody assessments

Major Findings
Summary of ADA response
• Across all TK Cohorts, 9 of 26 pregnant narsoplimab-treated
animals were ADA positive at the last sample evaluated (72
or 96 hours after the last dose).
• Across all main cohorts, 12 of 173 pregnant narsoplimab-–
treated animals were ADA positive at the prefirst dose time
point.
• None of 173 pregnant narsoplimab–treated animals were
ADA positive at 24-hour post second dose
• On DG 29, 11 to 19 days after the last dose (depending on
cohort), 167 of 173 pregnant narsoplimab–treated animals
were ADA positive.
• Confirmed positive ADA responses detected at 72 hours
after the second dose were generally associated with a
decreased serum narsoplimab concentrations and samples
were excluded from TK analysis.
• Pregnant females positive for ADA at 72 hours presented
decreased in pharmacodynamic activity.
• Maternal ADA response did not appear to affect fetal
narsoplimab serum concentrations in both SC and IV bolus
groups.

Prenatal and Postnatal Development (PPND)
Study Number/Title: 20113153 / NARSOPLIMAB: A Developmental and Perinatal/Postnatal

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Reviewer Comment
No clear adverse effects were observed in dams or in F1 offspring after administering
narsoplimab to dams during gestation and lactation by either the IV or SC routes. Signals of
embryofetal toxicity (e.g., cleft palate, fewer live fetuses/litter) observed at 300 mg/kg IV in the
definitive embryo-fetal development study were not observed at the same IV dose in this study,
for uncertain reasons.
Key study findings:
•

Narsoplimab–related effects in F0 generation mice were limited to transient and
nonadverse reductions in maternal body weight and gestational weight gain at 300 mg/kg
IV and 150 mg/kg SC.

•

There was no effect on litter parameters including body weight and viability indices, and
no necropsy findings in preweaning F1 offspring.

•

No narsoplimab–related effects on growth, sexual maturation, or neurobehavioral or
reproductive function in the F1 generation at any dose level evaluated.

•

Narsoplimab was present in serum of F1 offspring, presumably via transfer during the
lactation period. Levels of narsoplimab in maternal milk were not measured.

•

A NOAEL was defined as 300 mg/kg IV and 150 mg/kg SC.

Juvenile Animal Studies
Study Number/Title: 9000601: A 13-Week Subcutaneous Injection Toxicity Study of in the
Juvenile Mouse with a 16-Week Recovery
Key study findings:
•

Narsoplimab–related nonadverse increases in neutrophil counts at ≥50 mg/kg
corresponded with nonadverse microscopic findings of eosinophilic infiltration in the
bone marrow, injection site, and inguinal skin. All changes were resolved at the end of
recovery except for lower incidence of findings at the injection site.

•

Elevated IgE plasma levels occurred at all dose levels with partial reversibility at the end
of the recovery period indicative of an immunological sensitization of juvenile mice to
narsoplimab. T-cell dependent antibody response assessment shows juvenile mice
successfully mounted an immune response to keyhole limpet hemocyanin.

•

Substantial (>70%) and similar inhibition of the complement lectin pathway was observed
at all narsoplimab dose levels. The inhibition was sustained beyond 28 days after the last
dose. Results indicate narsoplimab inhibition was achieved at the lowest dose tested.

•

Antidrug antibody response onset occurred after the last dose and did not interfere with
toxicokinetic assessments of narsoplimab in juvenile mice.

•

NOAEL was 300 mg/kg/dose SC at 2-times the exposure at the MRHD (AUC,
conservatively corrected for a 4-fold potency difference).

Major Findings
No ADA was detected predose on PNDs 4, 28, or 91, or following

following the last dose, for which all their corresponding TK
samples had nonquantifiable concentrations of serum
narsoplimab.

Pharmacodynamic assessment –
inhibition of the lectin pathway

Therefore, all ADA confirmed positive results had no impact on the
overall TK interpretation and no serum narsoplimab
concentrations were excluded from TK analysis.
In general, narsoplimab inhibition was similar in male and female
juvenile mice.
Inhibition of >70% was frequently observed in all groups after
dose administration on PND 91 and was sustained up to 28 days
(672 hours) into the recovery period.
Inhibitory activity decreased overtime, but it was ≥50% at the end
of the 16-week recovery period (2688 hours).
Narsoplimab inhibition of the complement lectin pathway was
similar at the LD of 50 mg/kg/dose compared to the MD and HD of
150 and 300 mg/kg/dose.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Narsoplimab IV and SC Single Ascending Dose (OMS721-NHV-001)
Study Design
Study OMS721-NHV-001 was a Phase 1, sequential, single dose, escalation study to evaluate the
pharmacokinetics (PK), pharmacodynamics (PD), and ADA response of narsoplimab-wuug or
placebo given via IV and SC administration in healthy male subjects.
There were 7 cohorts with weight-based dosing of narsoplimab-wuug administered via IV
infusion (30 min) or SC injection. The doses were 0.025, 0.675, and 2 mg/kg for IV
administration and 0.075, 0.225, 0.675, and 2 mg/kg for SC administration.

Conclusions
For the IV route, there was approximately dose proportional increases in Cmax and AUC0­inf.
Clearance (CL) was the same for the three IV doses evaluated. For the SC route of
administration, there was greater than dose proportional increase for AUC0­inf and dose
proportional for Cmax.
For highest single dose 2 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug IV, the median (range) Tmax was
approximately 0.5 (0.5-8.0) h and mean (range) half-life was 79 (44-104) h. At the 2 mg/kg dose,
IV administration resulted in approximately 3.1-fold higher exposure (AUC0­inf) and
approximately 10-fold higher maximum concentration (Cmax), compared to SC administration.
Narsoplimab-wuug
Inhibition of the lectin pathway as assessed by C4d inhibition was considered high (80% from
baseline) for 0.675 and 2 mg/kg for up to 72 h postdose and highest (~100%) 0.5 to 4 h postdose.
The inhibition following lowest IV dose of 0.25 mg/kg was moderate (50-80% inhibition) on
Day 1 and declined to baseline afterwards. SC administration resulted in a lower C4 inhibition,
compared to IV dosing.
Narsoplimab-wuug IV and SC Multiple Ascending Dose (OMS721-NHV-002)
Study Design
Study OMS721-NHV-002 was a Phase 1, placebo-control, sequential, multiple dose escalation
study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics (PK), pharmacodynamics (PD), and ADA response of
narsoplimab-wuug or placebo given via IV and SC administration in about 90 healthy male and
female subjects. The objectives of the study were to characterize multiple dose
pharmacokinetics, assess safety, pharmacodynamic markers of lectin pathway inhibition, and
development of antidrug antibody.
The purpose of including both daily and weekly administration regimens was to assess the
potential of providing trough serum narsoplimab-wuug concentrations that can adequately inhibit
the lectin pathway. The Applicant anticipated that once daily administration would result in
higher trough and lower peak serum narsoplimab-wuug concentrations and a decrease in
intersubject variability, compared to weekly administration.
There were 10 cohorts, two dosing schemes (weight-based dosing or fixed dosing), and two
routes of administration (IV or SC).
Cohorts 1-6 received IV or SC once weekly administration of narsoplimab-wuug or placebo for
6 weeks:
•

Cohort 1: IV once weekly doses of 2 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug or placebo

•

Cohort 2: IV once weekly doses of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug or placebo

•

Cohort 3: SC once weekly doses of 2 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug or placebo

•

Cohort 4: SC once weekly doses of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug or placebo

•

Cohort 6: SC once weekly doses of 8 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug or placebo

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Conclusions
Steady-state serum narsoplimab-wuug concentrations were reached by the third (Day 15) IV
once weekly injection of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug. Mean (range) accumulation ratio for
4 mg/mL IV once weekly was 1.3 (1.0-1.8) based upon Day 36/Day1 serum concentrations.
Narsoplimab-wuug 2 and 4 mg/kg administered intravenously once weekly (represented as blue
and green triangles in Figure 20) resulted in the greatest serum concentration over the 6 weeks of
treatment. Subcutaneous dosing with fixed doses or weight-based dosing resulted in lower
systemic exposure.
The Applicant postulated that inhibition of C4d is representative of lectin pathway inhibition.
With 2 mg/kg administered once weekly by intravenous infusion, C4d inhibition of
approximately 90% was achieved after the first dose and later in the treatment (~Day 36);
however, it was not maintained (>80%) over each dosing period during the 6-week treatment
period.
With 4 mg/kg administered once weekly by intravenous infusion, C4d inhibition of
approximately 95% was achieved and a high level was maintained (>80%) over each dosing
period during the 6-week treatment period. Compared to 2 mg/kg IV, the higher 4 mg/kg once
weekly IV dose exhibited greater C4d inhibition and was within the target to anticipate clinical
benefit as per the Applicant.
Subcutaneous dosing regimens up to 8 mg/kg, 370 mg, 740 mg once weekly and 50 mg and
150 mg once daily were not able to maintain the same degree of persistent C4d inhibition as
4 mg/mL IV once weekly.

14.3. Pharmacometrics Review
In general, the Applicant’s population PK-PD analysis is considered acceptable in describing the
observed narsoplimab-wuug concentrations in healthy subjects and patients. The Applicant’s
analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified.
More specifically, the developed model was used to support the current submission as outlined
below:

• The PK modelling was not considered
reliable for predicting the effect of

remove monoclonal antibodies from

recommendation of a supplemental

exchange. The supplemental dose of
2 mg/kg was administered in clinical
trials and the safety is established.
Nevertheless, a dedicated PK study to
evaluate the impact of plasma
exchange on narsoplimab-wuug
clearance should be considered as a
postmarketing requirement to
accurately assess the need and the
amount of supplemental dose required
post-plasma exchange.

14.3.1. Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
The population PK (popPK) analysis was based on PK data from 4 studies (Table 102): Two
Phase 1 studies in healthy subjects (OMS721-NHV-001 and OMS721-NHV-002) and two Phase
2 studies in different patients’ populations (OMS721-GNP-001 and TMA-001) with thrombotic
microangiopathies (TMAs) or glomerulopathies (GNPs). Narsoplimab-wuug was administered
by either an SC injection or 30-minute IV infusion in healthy subjects. In Phase 2 studies,
Narsoplimab-wuug was administered as 30-minute IV infusion. Only narsoplimab-wuug serum
concentration data from the IV arms were used for the popPK analysis.
The analysis dataset comprised of 3330 PK observations from 110 subjects, including 159 below
quantification limit concentrations. The planned timing of blood samples for narsoplimab-wuug,
ADA, and inhibition of ex-vivo lectin-mediated complement pathway activation (inhibition of
complement component 4d - C4d - formation) measurements are presented in Table 103.
