Narsoplimab-wuug
Base PK Model
The pharmacokinetics of narsoplimab-wuug following IV administration was described by a 2compartment distribution model with parallel linear, and nonlinear Michaelis Menten elimination
(Figure 23). The PK model was parameterized in terms of CL, central and peripheral volumes of
distribution (V1 and V2 respectively), intercompartmental clearance (Q), maximum saturable
elimination rate (Vmax) and the Michaelis-Menten constant (Km: Narsoplimab-wuug
concentration that achieves half Vmax). Between subject variability (BSV) was estimated on all
parameters. The residual error model consisted of a combined additive and proportional error
model.
Figure 23. Schematic of Structural PK Model for Narsoplimab-wuug

Source: Applicant’s Population PK Report, Figure 8, page 37.

Covariates Analysis
A stepwise covariate modeling procedure was used in order to build the covariate model, with a
significance level of 0.05 for the forward inclusion and 0.001 for the backward deletion step.
The following covariates were included in the final model:
•

Baseline Body Weight on CL using a power model, centered at 77.2 kg.

•

Albumin as a time varying covariate on CL using a proportional linear model, centered at
38 g/L.

•

Baseline albumin on V1 using a power model, centered at 36 g/L.

•

Dose on V1 using a power model, centered at 260 mg.

•

Antidrug antibody positive status as a time varying covariate on Vmax (using linear
proportional model).

•

Albumin as a time varying covariate on Km using a power model, centered at 38 g/L.

•

Health status on Km and Vmax (using proportional linear model).

Body weight was not found to be a statistically significant covariate on the volumes of
distribution. A total of 7 patients from Study TMA-001 (n=58) received plasma therapy (PLT)
on the day of narsoplimab-wuug treatment or later during narsoplimab-wuug treatment, with 6 of
58 patients receiving plasma exchange and 1 patient receiving plasma infusion. The frequency of
174
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
plasma therapy varied between patients (Table 104). The effect of plasma therapy on
narsoplimab-wuug CL was evaluated in the PK model and deemed not clinically significant.
Final Model
The parameter estimates from the final popPK model for narsoplimab-wuug are listed in Table
105. For an average 77.2 kg patient with a baseline albumin level of 36 g/L, an average albumin
over time of 38 g/L, and no ADA, treated with 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug, the population
estimates for narsoplimab-wuug CL, V1, V2, Q, Km and Vmax are 0.12 L/h (2.9 L/day); 10.9 L,
4.64 L, 0.021 L/h (0.5 L/day), 5705 ng/mL, and 0.346 mg/h (8.31 mg/day). The interindividual
random effect (ETA) shrinkage from the final popPK model was 10.3%, 7.5%, 22.8%, 32.8%,
32.6%, and 40.9% for CL, V1, Q, V2, Km and Vmax.
Table 105. Parameter Estimates From Final Population PK Model

Source: Applicant’s Population PK Report, Table 14, page 43.

Albumin was identified as a major covariate explaining parts of the between subject variability
on CL, V1, and Km. Further covariate effects were body weight on CL, dose on V1, ADA status
on Vmax, and health status on Km and Vmax.
175
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Most of patients who received plasma therapy on the day of treatment start or later (median
plasma therapy duration of 1:75 h, ranging from 1 h to 2.87 h) received a supplemental
narsoplimab-wuug dose of about 2 mg/kg after plasma therapy. The effect of plasma therapy on
narsoplimab-wuug CL was never investigated in a dedicated study. Based on an information
request, the Applicant assessed the effect of plasma therapy on CL using the developed final
popPK model. The increase in narsoplimab-wuug clearance during plasma therapy was estimated
to be 37.1% or 0.045 L/h for a typical patient. The modified popPK model could estimate the
effect of plasma therapy on narsoplimab-wuug CL with reasonable precision (relative standard
error of 19.9%), with a 95% confidence interval (CI) of the mean plasma effect ranging from
22.6% to 51.6%. However, the precision of the remaining PK parameters substantially
decreased, indicating that the included outliers caused substantial numerical instabilities in the
PK model. Based on a likelihood ratio test, the modified model was fitting the data significantly
better (p-value=0.025) compared to the previously reported final popPK model. However, the
addition of plasma therapy as covariate on CL did not reduce the unexplained BSV on CL. In
addition, single 4 mg/kg dose PK simulations in a reference subject (70 kg, with median albumin
concentrations and no ADA) without or with about 2 hours PLT after 24 hours of narsoplimabwuug treatment initiation showed no noticeable difference in narsoplimab-wuug PK profile
(Figure 24), with overlapping PK profiles. In view of these results the Applicant concluded that
due to the modest effect size and short duration of PLT, no clinically meaningful effects of PLT
on narsoplimab-wuug exposure could be identified that would support supplemental doses
following plasma exchange.
The goodness-of-fit plots from the final popPK model (i.e., without PLT effect), are shown in
Figure 25. The plots of the observed concentrations versus population predicted as well as the
individual predicted concentrations show random normal scatter around the identity lines,
indicating absence of systematic bias except for very few large concentrations (>100 µg/mL)
showing underprediction of these observations by the PK model. The population conditional and
individual weighted residuals versus time after dose or versus population predicted
concentrations also show random normal scatter around zero with no specific trend, indicating no
apparent model misspecifications.

Narsoplimab-wuug
Adequacy of the Proposed Dosing to Standardize Exposure Between Patients Over the
Body Weight Range
The proposed dosing of 4 mg/kg of narsoplimab-wuug capped to 370 mg is considered
appropriate, as it provides comparable steady-state exposure (area under the concentration-time
curve over the dosing interval: AUCss, maximum concentration: Cmax, and trough concentration:
Cmin) across the body weight range. Figure 27 shows the predicted steady-state exposure metrics
from PK simulations using the clinical studies population and the estimated individual PK
parameters from the PK model. The red boxplots represent the exposure metrics if all subjects
received a 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug dose (i.e., without dose capping) and the blue boxplots
represent the exposure metrics if all subjects received a fixed narsoplimab-wuug dose of 370 mg.
The PK simulations demonstrate that capping narsoplimab-wuug dosing to 370 mg, particularly
in heavier subjects (weight >100 kg), provides consistent and comparable narsoplimab-wuug
exposure (mainly Cmax,ss and AUC,ss) to subjects with body weight below 92.5 kg and receiving
4 mg/kg of narsoplimab-wuug (red boxplots).
The administration of a 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug dose in subjects with body weight above
100 kg (red boxplot) or the administration of a fixed dose of 370 mg in subjects with body
weight below 70 kg (blue boxplot) is expected to result in relatively higher exposure compared
to the studied and current proposed dosing.
Figure 27. Predicted Narsoplimab-wuug AUCss, Cmax,ss, and Cmin,ss Under 4 mg/kg and 370 mg in
the Studies Population

Assessment of the Covariate Analysis
•

The PK parameters from the final model and from the nonparametric bootstrap were
estimated with acceptable precision, except for the estimated effect of health status on
Km. Deletion of health status as a covariate on Km did not change the objective function
value (OFV), with an OFV decrease of 0.7 points. The unexplained BSV on Km remained
unchanged as well, indicating that health status is likely not a relevant covariate on Km.
The effect of health status on Vmax was found not statistically significant as well, after
backward deletion of health status as a covariate on Vmax from the Applicant full covariate
model. The deletion of health status as a covariate on Vmax increased the objective
function by 2.8 points. The unexplained BSV on Vmax remained unchanged.

•

Among the PK parameters that are affected by the identified covariates, the ETA
shrinkage was acceptable for reliable assessment of covariate effects. According to the
base PK model, the ETA shrinkage was low and ranged from 3.7% to 17.7% for CL, V1
and Q. The ETA shrinkage for the remaining parameters was relatively higher for V2
(32.5%), Km (41%) and Vmax (38.7%), which makes the graphical assessment of
covariate on these parameters less reliable.

•

Individual dose was found to be a statistically significant covariate on the central volume
of distribution (V1) and was considered directly related to body weight as narsoplimabwuug dosing was a weight-based dosing. The reviewer’s evaluation of body weight
(included as power model standardized to 77.2 kg) instead of dose as covariate on V1
resulted in an increased OFV (by 34 points).
183

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
•

The presence of ADA was estimated to increase Vmax by 143% according to the popPK
model, which is predicted to result at steady-state exposure in about 22% decrease in
AUC,ss and 52% decrease in Cmin,ss.

•

PLT and more specifically plasma exchange (PE) is expected to remove large molecules
such as proteins and monoclonal antibodies from the systemic circulation. Five patients
received PE during narsoplimab-wuug treatment, and were used to evaluate the effect of
PE on narsoplimab-wuug PK. The median duration of PE in these 5 patients was 1.8 h
(ranging from 1.4 h to 2.9 h).

The reviewer’s reassessment of the effect of PE on narsoplimab-wuug CL, after appropriate
modification and rearrangement of the PK dataset, found that PE increased narsoplimab-wuug
CL by an average of 47% (with an OFV decrease of 4.5 for 1 degree of freedom). However, the
estimated effect on narsoplimab-wuug CL could not be precisely estimated, with a 95% CI of the
mean effect ranging from 24% to 124% (determined from bootstrap, n=200). In addition, the
covariate effect was sensitive to the initial value in the PK model.
The difficulty in reliably estimating the effect of PE on CL using the population PK model is
most likely related to the lack of informative PK data. There was no PK sampling performed
concurrently immediately pre- and post-PE amongst the 5 patients who received PE during
narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The potential estimated effect of PE on the PK is likely driven by 3
of the 5 patients, who had post-PE PK samples collected (Figure 28). Besides, the duration of the
procedure was not found to impact the estimated effect.

14.3.3. Applicant’s Population PK-PD (C4d Inhibition)
Analysis
Narsoplimab-wuug is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that binds to and inhibits MASP-2.
Inhibition of ex-vivo lectin-mediated C4d (complement component 4d) formation was assessed,
as a PD biomarker of the mechanism of action, and the PK/C4d relationship across all studies
was analyzed. The ex-vivo assay of C4d inhibition was used to justify the selection of 4 mg/kg
IV dosing in the Phase 2 studies as it was expected to maintain mean lectin pathway inhibition
>80% for about 168 hours, the proposed dosing interval. However, C4d inhibition was not used
as an efficacy endpoint in evaluating treatment response.
The majority of the C4d inhibition records had a time-matched PK observation, and the observed
PK data were used to characterize C4d inhibition, rather than model predicted concentrations.
PK concentrations below the limit of quantification were set to lower limit of quantification
(LLOQ)/2 (25 ng/mL). The C4d assay depends on the availability of mannan-binding lectin. In 4
(b) (6)
subjects
(b) (6)
the baseline mannan-binding lectin levels were below the
limit of quantification (<35.2 ng/mL). These subjects were excluded from the subsequent PK/PD
model development, as the C4d assay depends on the availability of mannan-binding lectin.
Some C4d inhibition values were measured outside of the stability window of 836 days. These
records were flagged and tested for exclusion during model development. Recorded C4d values
were capped at -50% or +200% to reduce the huge variability in these data and improve the PK-

189
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
PD model stability. C4d observations ranged from -1359% to +2430%, however large values
were relatively rare (Figure 31).
Figure 31. Distribution of Measured C4d Inhibition Values

Source: Applicant’s Population PK Report, Figure 29, page 80

Base PK-PD Model
A direct link Emax model was used to describe the relationship between narsoplimab-wuug
concentrations and C4d inhibition. The Emax model was parameterized in terms of baseline C4d
inhibition at zero narsoplimab-wuug concentration (E0), maximum C4d inhibition response
(Emax), and narsoplimab-wuug concentration required to achieve half (50%) of the maximum
C4d inhibition effect or change from baseline (EC50). A Hill coefficient or shape parameter
(Gamma) was estimated to be close to 1 and subsequently fixed to 1. BSV was estimated on all
parameters, except for Gamma, with an additive random effect of E0 and Emax and exponential
random effect on EC50. The residual error model consisted of an additive error model. The
parameter estimates from the base PK-PD model are listed below in Table 107.
Table 107. Parameter Estimates of Base Population PK-PD Model

Source: Applicant’s Population PK Report, Table 36, page 80.

Narsoplimab-wuug
Covariates Analysis
A stepwise covariate modeling procedure was used in order to build the covariate model, with a
significance level of 0.05 for the forward inclusion and 0.001 for the backward deletion step.
Tested potential covariates were gender, patient or health status, diseases, race, ethnicity, ADA
status, age, body weight, baseline albumin, creatinine concentration, and platelet counts
The following covariates were included in the final model:
•

Body weight on Emax, as power function centered at 77.2 kg

•

Body weight on E0, as proportional linear function centered at 77.2 kg

•

Baseline albumin on E0, as power function centered at 36 g/L

•

Health status on E0, as percent difference using proportional linear model

•

IgAN (IgA Nephropathy) disease on E0, as percent difference using proportional linear
model

Final Model
The parameter estimates from the final PK-PD model are listed in Table 108, and the modelpredicted C4d inhibition versus narsoplimab-wuug concentrations is presented in Figure 32.
According to the model, Narsoplimab-wuug plasma concentration responsible for 50% of the
maximum C4d inhibition effect (EC50) was estimated to be 664 ng/mL (0.664 µg/mL). Typical
parameters were estimated with good precision while covariate effect parameters had standard
errors up to 40% and 57.9% in the case of albumin on E0. The ETA shrinkage ranged from 10.4
to 11.1% and the residual error (Epsilon) shrinkage was 4.2%.
The goodness-of-fit plots from the final PK-PD model, are shown in Figure 33. The plots of the
observed C4d inhibition versus the population predicted or the individual predicted C4d
inhibition show random normal scatter around the identity lines, indicating absence of systematic
bias. The population and individual conditionally weighted residuals versus population predicted
C4d inhibition also show random normal scatter around zero with no specific trend, indicating no
apparent model misspecifications.

14.3.4. Reviewer’s Assessment on the PK-PD Model
Adequacy of the PK-PD Model in Describing PD Data
•

The Applicant’s PK-PD model adequately describes the measured C4d inhibition from the
narsoplimab-wuug clinical studies.

•

The Applicant’s PK-PD modeling was not a simultaneous or sequential modeling PK-PD
approach. The measured PK data were used directly to characterize the PD model, since
the majority of the C4d inhibition records had a time-matched PK observation (the below
quantification limit PK values were set to LLOQ/2). The reviewer re-evaluated the PK-PD
model using a sequential PK-PD modeling approach, where the individual predicted PK
concentrations (estimated using the developed PK model) were used to characterize the
PD model. The PK-PD model parameters using either the observed PK data (i.e.,
195

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Applicant’s model) or the individual predicted PK data (i.e., reviewer’s model) were
comparable, when considering the base and the full covariate model.
•

The recorded C4d inhibition data were highly variable, and the Applicant truncated the
C4d values at -50% or +200% to reduce the huge variability in the data and improve the
PK-PD model stability. The reviewer re-evaluated the PK-PD model using after
considering the C4d inhibition values between 0 and 100% only. The resulting PK-PD
model parameter estimates were comparable to those observed from the Applicant’s PKPD model.

•

There was no observed delay in the C4d inhibition response, with maximum C4d
inhibition occurring around peak narsoplimab-wuug concentrations. In addition, the
evaluation of an effect compartment in the PK-PD model did not improve the model.

Assessment of the Covariate Analysis
•

Among the PD model parameters that are affected by the identified covariates, the ETA
shrinkage was acceptable for reliable assessment of covariate effects. According to the
Applicant’s base PK-PD model, the ETA shrinkage was 7.8%, 12.5%, and 25.6% for E0,
EC50 and Emax, respectively.

•

The PD parameters from the Applicant’s final PK-PD model and from the nonparametric
bootstrap were estimated with acceptable precision, except for the estimated effect of
baseline albumin and IgAN disease on E0, with the bootstrap 95% CI including 0. The
precision around the estimated mean effect of body weight on E0 and Emax was relatively
high (>30%).

The evaluation of the individual ETA for E0 versus covariate plot from the base model did not
show a trend between E0 and baseline albumin. No clinical justification was provided for
including the effect of albumin on the baseline levels of C4d inhibition (E0). The deletion of
baseline albumin as a covariate on E0 from the final model did not result in a statistically
significant change of the OFV at a 0.001 significance level (OFV increased by 5.8 points for 1
degree of freedom).
The univariate deletion of IgAN or health status as a covariate on E0 from the final model
resulted in a statistically significant change of the OFV at a 0.001 significance level (OFV
increased by 16.8 and 18.5 points for 1 degree of freedom, respectively). The covariate plot from
the base model showed a slightly lower median E0 in healthy subjects and IgAN patients
compared to the other disease conditions. However, these differences in E0 were not expected to
be clinically relevant given the high and overlapping variability in E0 between healthy subjects
and patients, as well as between the various disease conditions among patients. In addition, if we
consider the mechanism of action of the drug, baseline C4d inhibition (i.e., in the absence of
drug) would be anticipated to be either lower in patients compared to healthy subjects or
comparable to healthy subject due to other standard of care procedure pre-enrollment.
Although, body weight remained statistically significant covariates on E0 and Emax, after
univariate deletion from the Applicant’s full covariate model, the ETA versus covariate plots for
E0 and Emax did not show a trend with body weight. In addition, the estimated effect of body
196
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
weight on E0 and Emax has low precision (35.4% and 39.7% respectively) and were close to zero.
Therefore, body weight is not expected to be clinically relevant covariate on E0 and Emax.
Finally, the univariate covariate analysis using the Applicant’s base model found that only health
status on E0 and body weight on Emax were statistically significant at a 0.05 significance level
(decrease in OFV by -4.2 and -10.3 for 1degree of freedom, respectively).
•

Covariate analysis performed on the reviewer’s PK-PD model, which linked the
individual predicted PK concentrations (from the developed PK model) instead to the
current observed concentrations to C4d inhibition, found that baseline albumin on E0 and
body weight on Emax were not statistically significant covariates at a 0.001 significance
level after univariate deletion from the full covariate model (in agreement with the ETA
versus covariate plots showing the absence of trends).

This was also in agreement with the univariate covariate analysis using the reviewer’s base
model, which found that only health status on E0 and body weight on Emax were statistically
significant at a 0.05 significance level (decrease in OFV by -4.9 and -6.4 for 1degree of freedom,
respectively), but were not statistically significant at significance level <0.01.
In conclusion, the investigated covariates on the Applicant’s and the reviewer’s base PK-PD
models are not considered clinically relevant due to:
•

The weak statistical significance of the included covariates in the Applicant’s and the
reviewer’s full covariate PK-PD models.

•

The absence of a relationship between the PD parameters and the covariates from the ETA
versus covariate plots from the Applicant’s and the reviewer’s base PK-PD models.

•

The absence of clear physiological relevance of these covariates.

Association between narsoplimab-wuug concentrations and the level of C4d inhibition during
narsoplimab-wuug treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA amongst treatment responders and
non-responders
The ex-vivo assay of C4d inhibition was used to justify the selection of 4 mg/kg IV dosing in
Phase 2 studies as it was expected to maintain mean lectin pathway inhibition >80% for about
168 hours, the proposed dosing interval. However, C4d inhibition was not used as marker of
efficacy or treatment response in the study.
However, no association was observed between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough
concentrations or the extent of C4d inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in
patients with HSCT-TMA. As depicted from Figure 34, almost all non-responder to
narsoplimab-wuug treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than treatment
responders, with trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations consistently above IC80 for the
majority of non-responders. Conversely, amongst the treatment responders, many patients had
their trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations just above IC50 for C4d inhibition.

14.4. Summary of Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
Concentrations of narsoplimab-wuug in human serum were determined using a validated ELISA
(b) (4)
method at the Bioanalytical Laboratory of
. A Nunc 96 well ELISA plate
was coated with MASP-2A and incubated overnight at 4 ºC. After incubation, the plates were
washed and blocked. Diluted narsoplimab-wuug–containing serum samples were transferred to
the coated plate and incubated for 2 hours at room temperature. Mouse antihuman IgG4
horseradish peroxidase-conjugated antibodies were added to the wells after washing. The
detection substrate TMBred was added after a final wash and detection of TMBox was used to
determine narsoplimab-wuug concentrations. The review team has determined that the method
validation and sample analysis are acceptable.

16. Efficacy: Additional Information and
Assessment
Patient Narratives from Study TMA-001
Patient

(b) (6)

The patient was a 48-year-old Caucasian male who received a matched unrelated donor
transplant after failed induction for biphenotypic acute leukemia. He had complicated postHSCT course. On Day 5 post-HSCT, he developed a productive cough with fever and diarrhea.
He was treated empirically with gentamycin and tazocin. Blood cultures were positive for
Streptococcus oralis. Sputum culture grew Candida krusei. On Day 6, his creatinine increased
and cyclosporin dose was reduced. On Day 9, he developed hypoxia requiring supplemental
oxygen, anti-infectives were changed to meropenem and caspofungin. On Day 11, his creatinine
continued to increase, believed to be related to sepsis and intravascular fluid depletion. The
mucositis and pneumonia slowly improved allowing pain medications and oxygen to be
decreased. Neutrophil engraftment occurred on approximately Day 21.
On day 17, the patient had fever and diarrhea. A diagnosis of liver GVHD was made and the
patient was started on methylprednisolone. On day 18, vancomycin was started. The patient
improved but developed posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES) on day 22 and
cyclosporin was stopped. It was at this time the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA was made on day 23.
The TMA diagnosis was based on elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), schistocytes,
undetectable haptoglobin, and an elevated creatinine. Laboratory markers at the time of study
entry and shown in Table 114. On day 33, the patient received his first dose of narsoplimabwuug, the following day the investigator was informed the patient had E. coli 0157. The patient
was asymptomatic at that time and therefore no treatment was given. In total, the patient received
8 weekly doses of narsoplimab-wuug. The patient continued to have neutropenia and
thrombocytopenia during treatment, this was thought to be secondary to mycophenolate mofetil
and valganciclovir, or graft failure. The patient did not engraft platelets and remained platelet
and blood transfusion dependent. The patient met a response based on LDH and renal response.
Response based on TMA markers was noted 15 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. The
patient ultimately died on day 215 due to neutropenic sepsis.
Reviewer comment
This is a complex patient with recent diagnosis of PRES, liver GVHD, and infections in relation
to HSCT-TMA. While GVHD and infections are risk factors for HSCT-TMA often, the first step
in treatment of HSCT-TMA is to treat the underlying cause and/or withdrawal or reduction of
calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs) and sirolimus (Rosenthal 2016; Khosla et al. 2018; Elemary et al.
2019). Therefore, the impact of treating these risk factors of liver GVHD and infections make it
difficult to interpret if the patient’s clinical improvement was due to narsoplimab-wuug or
treating the underlying cause. The patient also had cyclosporin discontinued at the time of
HSCT-TMA diagnosis, 10 days before starting narsoplimab-wuug. The Bone Marrow Transplant
Clinical Trials Network Toxicity Committee consensus summary recommended CNI cessation as
the first line management (Ho et al. 2005; Epperla et al. 2020). Although there is debate if
205
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
removal of CNIs and sirolimus improves HSCT-TMA, as some recent large cohort. studies have
not demonstrated a benefit (Epperla et al. 2020).
The confounders described above make the interpretation of narsoplimab-wuug treatment effect
difficult, the clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not meet
all three components of the predefined response definition. While the reviewer understands the
patient never engrafted platelets, this is a critical component of the response definition and
therefore response is less compelling. When looking at the patient’s TMA markers it is quite
apparent that the haptoglobin improved after starting narsoplimab-wuug, this could be a sign of
response to narsoplimab-wuug, but this was not included in the primary composite endpoint
definition and how this translates to clinical benefit is not clear. While the LDH improved on
narsoplimab-wuug, appears to have been down trending prior to narsoplimab-wuug
administration, see Figure 35 below. Also, the patient never achieved blood transfusion freedom.
Of note, the patient was also reported to have a positive E. coli 0157. The clinical reviewer was
not able to confirm this as it was stated in the narrative, but not documented elsewhere. E. coli
0157 can cause typical hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS), another form of TMA.

Patient

(b) (6)

The patient was a 58-year-old Asian male with a history of myelodysplastic syndrome that
evolved into acute myeloid leukemia (AML). After achieving a complete remission, he had a
matched related HSCT. Following transplant, the patient developed mucositis requiring total
parental nutrition. On day 5, his creatinine increased. He developed possible typhlitis and
cefepime and metronidazole was started, with amphotericin B added shortly after. He was
requiring both blood and platelet transfusions. On day 12, he required intubation for respiratory
distress and was diagnosed with diffuse alveolar hemorrhage (DAH). Vancomycin and steroids
207
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
were added to his treatment regimen. Fungal pneumonia was also a concern. He was diagnosed
with cytomegalovirus (CMV) reactivation on day 15 and was started on ganciclovir. Neutrophil
engraftment occurred on day 15, the patient never engrafted platelets. He developed fluid
overload and was started on dialysis. He was extubated on day 19. Tacrolimus and sirolimus was
temporarily discontinued from day 15 and day 21, respectively.
The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 21, laboratory makers supporting this
diagnosis were schistocytes, thrombocytopenia, and elevated LDH. Laboratory markers at time
of study entry on shown in Table 115. The patient received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug
on day 36. He received 8 doses total. Response based on TMA markers was noted 7 days after
starting narsoplimab-wuug, the patient met response based on LDH and renal response. He never
engrafted platelets.
The patient was discharged home, but subsequently readmitted on day 39 for GI-GVHD and
persistent pancytopenia. Tacrolimus, solumedrol, basiliximab, ruxolitinib, and budesonide were
added to the patient’s treatment plan. On day 48, encephalopathy was noted and tacrolimus was
discontinued. On day 54, acyclovir was held for worsening renal function. The patient suffered
from encephalopathy, sepsis and additional viral infections. Graft failure was noted on day 96
and the patient died on day 100 due to septic shock.
Reviewers comments
This patient had a complicated post-HSCT course. Around the time of his HSCT-TMA diagnosis
the patient was also diagnosed with possible pneumonia, typhlitis and GVHD. The patient was
subsequently treated with antibiotics, antivirals and antifungal agents, in addition to steroids for
the GVHD. The treatment of these underlying risk factors may impact the interpretability of
narsoplimab-wuug.
While the confounders described above make the interpretation of narsoplimab-wuug treatment
effect difficult, the clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not
meet all three components of the predefined response definition. While the reviewer understands
the patient never engrafted platelets, this is a critical component of the response definition and
therefore response cannot be substantially determined. In addition, the patient was noted to have
renal response, but never had a doubling of creatinine from pretransplant level. In fact, at the
time of screening, the patient’s serum creatinine was 0.76 mg/dL which was down trending (see
Figure 36, while the creatinine further decreased on therapy, this magnitude of change is difficult
to interpret. The patient did have a reduction in LDH, although the LDH was already down
trending prior to narsoplimab-wuug administration (see Figure 36). Of note, the patient had a low
haptoglobin that drastically increased to normal while receiving therapy, this may provide
evidence of narsoplimab-wuug effectiveness, but it is unknown how this translates to clinical
benefit. It is worth noting that while it is unlikely the patient had TTP, and ADAMTS13 was not
documented, this is typically standard to rule out when considering TMA.

Patient

(b) (6)

The patient was a 22-year-old Hispanic female with a history of relapsed B-acute lymphoblastic
leukemia (ALL) who received a matched sibling HSCT. On day 18, she developed a persistent
fever, she was treated with micafungin, cefepime and vancomycin. Her blood cultures were
positive for Haemophlius influenzae. On day 20, she was diagnosed with GI-GVHD and was
treated with solumedrol, basiliximab, and budesonide. Day 24 bronchoscopy was significant for
aspergillus and amphotericin B was initiated. On day 32, she had a GI bleed likely related to
GVHD and blood counts were decreasing. Schistocytes were noted on day 36. On day 40, she
had CMV reactivation and ganciclovir was added. Ruxolitinib was also added for GVHD. At this
time her creatinine was increased, thought to be secondary to medications. She continued to have
multiple episodes of bacteremia for which gentamicin was added. For her GVHD she also
received photopheresis and infliximab. On day 69, she was transferred to the intensive care unit
(ICU) for increased abdominal pain and GI bleeding. Echocardiography showed pulmonary
arterial hypertension. On day 92 liver GVHD was suspected. She developed Klebsiella
pneumonia on day 95. She had a colonoscopy on day 113 which showed mild GVHD and
HSCT-TMA.
Her diagnosis of HSCT-TMA was based on colonoscopy results, in addition to
thrombocytopenia, anemia, schistocytes, increased creatine and elevated LDH, Table 116 shows
the patients screening labs. Tacrolimus was discontinued on day 121 and the patient started
narsoplimab-wuug that same day. The patient received 8 weekly doses of narsoplimab-wuug,
and initial response based on TMA makers occurred approximately 11 days after starting
therapy. Response was based on LDH and renal response, as the patient never engrafted platelets
and remained blood transfusion dependent.
Shortly after staring narsoplimab-wuug, the patient developed fever, she was found to have
CMV reactivation, in addition to positive blood cultures for fungal and subsequently bacterial
infections. She was treated with various antibiotics and antifungals. On day 166 her kidney
injury improved. Unfortunately, the patient continued to have a complicated course including
viral infections, possible pneumonia, GI-GVHD, transfusion dependence, renal dysfunction,
catheter related thrombosis and sepsis. The investigator noted on day 220 there were no signs of
HSCT-TMA. The patient ultimately died on day 291 due to GVHD, respiratory failure and
sepsis.
Clinical reviewer comment:
This patient had very complicated transplant course, most notable for steroid refractory GVHD
and recurrent infections for which she was treated with a variety of medications. Tacrolimus was
discontinued the same day of narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The treatment of these underlying
risk factors may impact the interpretability of narsoplimab-wuug. While not mentioned in the
patient narrative, the Applicant was able to confirm the patient did receive a plasma exchange
while receiving narsoplimab-wuug therapy. Plasma exchange is not standard of care for HSCTTMA and response rates have been reported to be 27-80%, but in select patients it may be of
benefit (Rosenthal 2016; Jodele 2018; Khosla et al. 2018). Of note, the patient did not have an
ADAMTS13 reported.

understands the patient never engrafted platelet this is a critical component of the response
definition and therefore response cannot be substantially determined. Of note, the renal function
acutely worsened on therapy, but then significantly down trended as shown in Figure 37. LDH
improved on therapy. As for other TMA markers, blood transfusions were continued but this
could have been due to poor graft function. Haptoglobin was never decreased.

Patient

(b) (6)

The patient was a 34-year-old male with a past medical history significant for ALL that was
refractory to treatment. He received a matched sibling donor transplant. The patient had a
complicated post-transplant course. On day 3, he developed a fungal rash for which micafungin
was started and on day 6 he developed neutropenic sepsis (blood culture was positive for
coagulase negative Staphylococcus). Platelet engraftment was noted on day 9 and neutrophil
engraftment occurred on day 12. The patient had persistent fevers for which meropenem was
added. Engraftment syndrome was diagnosed on day 13 and steroids were started. Cefepime and
vancomycin were replaced with linezolid. Upper and lower endoscopy showed esophageal
212
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
candidiasis, GVHD, and CMV colitis. On day 30 he developed acute kidney injury suspected to
be from dehydration. He was treated with a variety of antibiotics for fevers.
On day 35 he had thrombocytopenia, anemia and elevated LDH concerning for microangiopathic
hemolytic anemia. Cyclosporin was discontinued on day 40 and tacrolimus was started. The
patient was screened for Study TMA-001 on day 49 but had a positive direct antiglobulin test
(DAT) therefore, he was not eligible. He was treated with a reduction in tacrolimus dose and
intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG). The patient was re-admitted on day 71 for weakness,
elevated creatinine, and mild jaundice. The patient received plasma exchange 12 times without
resolution of the TMA and additional IVIG.
On day 91 he was screened again for enrollment and the DAT was negative. His diagnosis of
HSCT-TMA was based on thrombocytopenia, anemia, LDH, and schistocytes, his screening labs
are shown in Table 117. On day 107 he had acute kidney injury due to poor oral intake. On day

on LDH and platelets occurred 228 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. There were no clinical

The remainder of his post-transplant course was significant for herpes ophthalmicus, chronic
GVHD of the skin, and pneumonia.
Clinical reviewer comment:
This patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 35 following transplant but, was unable to
enroll in Study TMA-001 due to a persistently positive DAT (a key exclusion criteria). The
patient received IVIG and plasma exchange but the TMA did not improve. He was ultimately
started therapy approximately 80 days after diagnosis. It is unusually to have a positive DAT and
HSCT-TMA as this is not immune related process. A positive DAT could indicate an autoimmune
hemolytic anemia or Evan’s syndrome (Dvorak 2019). Although, at the time of rescreening the
investigator did note the patient had schistocytes, which does suggest a microangiopathic
hemolytic anemia process. In addition, DAT can become positive with recurrent blood
transfusions. The positive DAT certainly complicates the patient’s diagnosis.
The clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not meet all three
components of the predefined response definition. His response was based on platelets and LDH
alone. The patient was never evaluated for clinical improvement as these components were not
evaluable, in addition he had a normal creatinine. The Applicant’s rationale for including him as
a responder was due to an increase in hemoglobin, but this was not a component of the primary
endpoint and it’s not clear if the magnitude of hemoglobin response is significant with a baseline
hemoglobin around 10 g/dL. The other concern was response based on TMA markers (LDH and
platelets) was not documented until 228 after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was discussed
with clinical pharmacology colleagues who agreed that based on the half-life of narsoplimabwuug its to too difficult to determine if this response was related to narsoplimab-wuug. The
Applicant states the delay of response was secondary to an incurrent infection but, most patients
in Study TMA-001 had infection without a delayed response. Of note, the patient did not meet
the inclusion criteria of doubling of creatinine.

4. He achieved neutrophil engraftment on day 17. After having intermittent diarrhea, he was

Clinical reviewer comment
Around the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis the patient also had a diagnosis of acute and chronic
GVHD, Evan’s syndrome, CMV reactivation, and GBS. The patient was actively treated for
Evan’s syndrome which has overlapping characteristics of HSCT-TMA, such as
thrombocytopenia, anemia, and elevated LDH. The clinical reviewer is not convinced that the
patient had a diagnosis of Evan’s syndrome given the DAT was negative and schistocytes were
presents, this is consistent with microangiopathic hemolysis. The patient did receive rituximab
for Evan’s syndrome shortly before narsoplimab-wuug, there have been a few small studies and
case reports suggesting that rituximab may be an effective treatment for HSCT-TMA (Kim et al.
2015). With that being said, timing of response does favor narsoplimab-wuug. Overall, the
concurrent disease diagnosis and treatments obscure the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug,
although favors that this patient responded to narsoplimab-wuug.
The patient was noted to have a renal response (this was not required to meet the responder
definition), but the patient did not meet the renal inclusion criteria of doubling of serum
creatinine. With a baseline creatinine of 0.94 mg/dL (screening creatinine was 0.61 mg/dL) and
without doubling of the pretransplant creatinine, the significance of the magnitude of reduction is
unclear.

Patient

(b) (6)

The patient was a 32-year-old male with a history of high-risk ALL who underwent a matched
related donor transplant. Day 1 post-transplant, he developed mucositis requiring parental
nutrition and opioid management. On day 6, he developed bacteremia (blood culture was
positive for staphylococcal aureus), he was treated initially with meropenem. The patient
achieved neutrophil and platelet engraftment on day 18 and 22, respectively. On day 88, he was
diagnosed with oral GVHD. His cyclosporine was discontinued on day 145. On day 159, he had
elevated liver function tests, liver biopsy demonstrated severe panlobular hepatitis with
hepatocyte necrosis, concerning for aGVHD. The patient was treated with prednisone. He also
had CMV reactivation. Around day 178, he was diagnosed with acute skin and liver GVHD, in
addition to chronic GVHD of the eye.
On day 194 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA, based on thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH,
elevated creatinine and anemia. The patient had been restarted on cyclosporin two weeks prior
but this was discontinued once HSCT-TMA was diagnosed. At this time, he was also diagnosed
with PRES. With blood pressure control the patient recovered. He also had a low-grade fever. He
received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 201, screening labs are shown in Table 119.
He received 8 total doses. Response occurred 153 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug and was
based on improvement in LDH, platelets, neurologic dysfunction and transfusions. See TMA
marker changes in Figure 40. Following initiation of narsoplimab-wuug he had recurrent
episodes of delirium and confusion, this was thought to be secondary to steroids, and/or selfdiscontinuation of lorazepam and haloperidol. He was last seen on day 297 in outpatient clinic.
Clinical reviewer comment
The patient was diagnosed and treated for CMV reactivation, fever and GVHD around the same
time has his HSCT-TMA diagnosis and starting narsoplimab-wuug. In addition, the patient was
diagnosed with PRES at the same time as HSCT-TMA. Neurologic deficits have been reported in
up to half of patients with HSCT-TMA and can include confusion, headaches, hallucinations and
seizures (Jodele et al. 2015). The most common of CNS injury is likely due to uncontrolled TMA
associated hypertension, including PRES (Jodele et al. 2015). While the reviewer acknowledges
that PRES can be a manifestation of HSCT-TMA is difficult to tell if improvement was based on
hypertension control or narsoplimab-wuug. The timing of PRES improvement is not clear. In
addition, the patient had delirium and confusion, but this occurred after the patient had already
been started on narsoplimab-wuug. Treatment of concurrent disease diagnosis make it difficult
to interpret the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug.
The patient did not meet the responder definition until 153 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug.
It is difficult to determine if a response this far out from treatment can be attributed to
narsoplimab-wuug. The clinical pharmacology team also agreed it is difficult to determine given
the half-life of narsoplimab-wuug.
Of note, the patient also did not meet inclusion/exclusion criteria. The patient had a weakly
positive Coombs test (although the repeat central laboratory test was negative) and a positive
shiga toxin. Most likely, these tests were false positives given repeat Coombs test was negative
and the patient did not have diarrhea.

Patient

(b) (6)

The patient is a 34-year-old African American male who had a history of S/β° thalassemia and
underwent a matched sibling HSCT, but experienced late graft failure. He then developed
myelodysplastic syndrome and underwent a second matched sibling HSCT. On day 10 posttransplant, he had a fever and was treated with antibiotics. On day 14, he had increasing blood
pressure and CT of the chest consistent with an atypical infection. Neutrophil engraftment
occurred on day 20. On day 23, he developed hematuria and was BK virus positive. He was
subsequently diagnosed with CMV reactivation and repeat chest CT showed multifocal infection.
He was treated with vancomycin. Platelet engraftment was achieved on day 36. His course was
further complicated by head CT showing a subdural hematoma on day 43.
On day 66 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA, supporting his diagnosis was thrombocytopenia,
elevated creatinine, increased LDH, anemia, and schistocytes. See screening labs in Table 120.
He received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 85. Tacrolimus was discontinued on day
89. He received 4 doses per protocol. His response based on TMA markers occurred 28 days
after starting narsoplimab-wuug. Response was based on improvement in creatinine, platelets
and LDH (see Figure 41).
Following response to narsoplimab-wuug the patient’s course was complicated by GVHD.
Clinical reviewer comment
The patient had tacrolimus discontinued at the time of narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The impact
of tacrolimus on this patient’s response appears less likely as the patient did not meet the
response definition until 28 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. Therefore, it appears
narsoplimab-wuug most likely caused an improvement in TMA markers and serum creatinine.
The patient’s LDH was just above the upper limit of normal at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis
at 221 U/L (normal range: 100-200U/L). The highest the LDH increased was to 278 U/L and the
baseline LDH level was 237 U/L. Therefore, while the LDH may have decreased further on
narsoplimab-wuug, it is difficult to determine the significance of magnitude of change. To meet
the LDH component of response the patient had to have and LDH <1.5x upper limit of normal
(ULN), but this patient never had an LDH >1.5x ULN. The clinical reviewer remains convinced
that this patient responded to narsoplimab-wuug given improvement in platelet count and serum
creatinine. This is further supported by an increase in hemoglobin (while not receiving
transfusions) and improvement in haptoglobin.

While the patient should still be considered a responder, the issues outlined below make the
interpretation of the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug difficult.
The patient was determined by the Applicant to have a GI response. The improvement in melena
does seem to correlate temporarily to narsoplimab-wuug administration, but this is confounded
by the fact the patient also had GI-GVHD and was being treated with steroids during that time. It
does not appear that the steroid treatment could account for the GI response alone as the dose
had not significantly changed and the patient was on a low dose of prednisone (25 mg).
Therefore, it is likely narsoplimab-wuug was responsible for GI improvement.
The patient was also determined to have a neurologic response based on improvement in
polyneuropathy per the Applicant. Polyneuropathy during transplant can be multifactorial
including medications. It is difficult to confidently determine if the patient’s polyneuropathy is
related to HSCT-TMA and if improvement was due to narsoplimab-wuug.
The patient’s response was also confounded by a decreasing cyclosporin dose during
narsoplimab-wuug treatment. Of note, the cyclosporin was already at a low dose when it was
reduced.
Based on Figure 42, the patient did have a significant reduction in LDH, and improvement in
platelets and haptoglobin.
Of note, the patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria for study entry, although the renal
component of response was not needed to meet the response definition.

underwent an unrelated 10/10 matched HSCT. On day 41 post-transplant, he developed
thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, decreased hemoglobin, and decreased haptoglobin.
These findings supported a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA. The cyclosporin dose was tapered
but continued through his transplant course. He received his first dose of narsoplimabwuug on day 62 and received 16 twice weekly 4 mg/kg doses. The patient was considered
a responder to narsoplimab-wuug due to improvement in TMA laboratory markers
(platelet count, LDH, and haptoglobin) and achieving transfusion independence.

•

Patient
: The patient is a 20-year-old Caucasian female who underwent a related
matched HSCT for B-cell ALL. Her transplant course was complicated by gastroenteritis,
febrile neutropenia, and micronodular liver lesions. She developed skin and GI-GVHD
and day 118. She was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 184 post-transplant,
demonstrating thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, decreased hemoglobin and blood
transfusions. The patient had GVHD at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis. She received
narsoplimab-wuug on day 196 and received 18 once weekly 4 mg/kg IV dosing initially.
Later the frequency of narsoplimab-wuug was increased to twice weekly dosing based on
an initial poor response. The patient was considered a responder due to decreased LDH,
and discontinuation of blood and platelet transfusion support. She was alive at their last
site contact on day 568.

•

Patient
: The patient was a 48 years old Caucasian male with a history of human
immunodeficiency virus-1 (HIV-1), latent tuberculosis, renal failure, chemotherapy
induced cardiotoxicity and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, who underwent an
unrelated mismatched HSCT for Hodgkin’s disease with nodular sclerosis in CR3. The
patient developed HSCT-TMA on day 0, demonstrating thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH,
elevated creatinine, and decreased hemoglobin. His transplant course was complicated by
multiple infections, hemodialysis and transfusions. The patient received the first
narsoplimab-wuug dose on Day 6 and received 8 twice weekly doses of 4 mg/kg. The
patient did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug and died of a cardiac arrest on day 31.

•

decreased hemoglobin. Cyclosporin was stopped at that time. The patient received 3 doses
of narsoplimab-wuug (4 mg/kg) on days 20 through 27. She received 12 plasma

Clinical reviewer comment
In total, the Applicant states 3 out of 5 compassionate use patients responded to narsoplimabwuug therapy. Compassionate use patients did not use the same response criteria, although
based on the narrative descriptions, narsoplimab-wuug did seem to have a positive clinical
impact. It is also important to note that compassionate use patients did not receive the same
dosing frequency as patients in Study TMA-001. In fact, all patients received more frequent
dosing than once weekly. Data collected from the compassionate use patients is limited and the
sample size is small (n=5) therefore it is difficult to make conclusions based on the
compassionate use program. The compassionate use program will not be included in the United
States Prescribing Information (USPI) as efficacy will be determined from Study TMA-001.

(b) (6)

The investigator

Clinical reviewer comment
There were 4 patient deaths in the adverse event (AE) evaluation period. Of those deaths, 2 were related to infections and considered possibly
related to narsoplimab-wuug. The other two deaths during the AE evaluation period were unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug, and more likely
related to the underlying disease process such as HSCT-TMA, GVHD, or leukemia. Ultimately, more than half of patients treated on TMA-001 died.
This is not unexpected given the patient population consisted of complex, critically ill patients on immunosuppressive therapies, and concurrent
disease processes thus increasing their risk for death. In addition, HSCT-TMA has a high mortality rate.
Supportive Safety Population
In patients with aHUS and TTP, 3 of 30 patients (10%) in Study TMA-001 and 1 of 6 patients (16.7%) in Study OMS-HUS-002 died during the
study (Table 126). The most common cause were infections, including Escherichia sepsis and pneumonia. There were two deaths in compassionate
use patients. Narratives are described below.
There were no deaths in the NHV studies, OMS721-GNP-001 or OMS721-IGA-001.
Patient Narratives
•

Patient
: The patient was a 54-year-old female in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, asthma, heart failure, and arterial hypertension who
died due to the treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) of pneumonia. The patient had received 7 doses of 4 mg/kg IV narsoplimab-wuug
when she developed grade 4 pneumonia supported by a bronchoscopy which demonstrated suppurative pneumonia. Respiratory cultures were
positive for Burkholderia cepacia and she was treated with antibiotics. During this time narsoplimab-wuug was continued. Shortly after the
patient developed respiratory failure with cardiac arrest and died. The patient’s death occurred 64 days from the first dose of narsoplimabwuug.

•

Patient
: The patient was a 43-year-old female in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, hypertension, and gastritis, who died of a TEAE of
sepsis. The patient received 5 scheduled doses of narsoplimab-wuug, in addition to supplemental doses while receiving plasma exchange
therapy. During hospitalization the patient hallucinated and experienced convulsions. A CT scan was negative, and the patient was started on
valproic acid and haloperidol. Following the initiation of valproic acid the patient developed pancytopenia, but this resolved after
discontinuation. A little over two weeks after recovery from the pancytopenia the patient developed an infection, blood cultures grew
Escherichia coli and Klebsiella pneumonia. Despite treatment with IV antibiotics and immunoglobins the patient deteriorated and died.

(b) (6)

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

•

Patient
: The patient was a 73-year-old male in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, chronic parenchymal liver disease, and
hyperammonemia, who died of a TEAE of sudden death. After 5 doses of narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV the patient was admitted to the
hospital for hematemesis. An endoscopy showed esophageal varices and portal hypertensive gastropathy. He continued on dialysis for his endstage renal disease. Approximately 2.5 months later the patient developed massive ascites. The patient recovered from the event.
Approximately 10 days after recovery and discharge from the hospital the patient had lost consciousness at home and was pronounced dead at
the hospital. At the time of death, the patient had received 19 doses of narsoplimab-wuug. Cause of death was not determined.

•

Patient
: The patient was a 40-year-old male in Study OMS721-HUS-002, with aHUS, epilepsy, arterial hypertension, renal
failure, type 2 diabetes mellitus, mediastinal abscesses, and pancreatitis, who died due to TEAEs of Escherichia coli and intracerebral
hematoma. Prior to starting narsoplimab-wuug the patient was hospitalized for seizures. A CT scan showed a new left subdural hematoma and
bilateral hygroma. Approximately a week later a repeat CT showed bilateral chronic subdural hematomas with active bleeding with mass
effect. The patient had neurologic deterioration. Shortly after this event narsoplimab-wuug was initiated. Approximately 14 days after starting
narsoplimab-wuug the patient became unresponsive. Head CT showed numerous hypodense foci in the white matter of both hemispheres,
followed by a repeat CT which showed evolution of the subdural hematomas. In addition, the patient developed GI bleeding and blood culture
was positive for E. coli. Despite antibiotic therapy the patient died of sepsis and intracerebral hematoma. It was also noted the patient had
grade 4 thrombocytopenia which likely contributed to the bleeding events.

•

Patient
: The patient was a 48-year-old male with HSCT-TMA treated on compassionate use. The patient had a complicated medical
history consisting of HIV-1, latent tuberculosis, Hodgkin’s lymphoma with nodular sclerosis, renal failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary
disease and cardiotoxicity. He received an unrelated mismatched donor allogeneic transplant. The conditioning regimen included,
cyclophosphamide, fludarabine, busulfan, and thymoglobulin, and cell source was umbilical cord. Tacrolimus was started prior to transplant,
the patient developed HSCT-TMA on day 0 (same day as HSCT). He started narsoplimab-wuug on day 6. Post-transplant course was
complicated by cocaine and heroin abstinence syndrome, human papillomavirus, E. faecalis bacteremia, and CMV viremia on day 15. Shortly
after he developed febrile neutropenia and severe esophagitis. Bronchoalveolar lavage was positive for aspergillus. He died of cardiac arrest on
day 31. He received 8 twice weekly narsoplimab-wuug doses of 4 mg/kg from day 6 to day 31.

•

Patient
: The patient was a 31-year-old female with HSCT-TMA treated on compassionate use. The patient had a history of severe
aplastic anemia. She received a matched unrelated donor allogeneic transplant. She received reduced intensity conditioning with fludarabine,
cyclophosphamide, antithymocyte globulin, and total body irradiation. She was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 11. She received 3 doses
of narsoplimab-wuug on day 20 to 27 and plasma infusions. She required blood and platelet transfusions, in addition to hemodialysis. She
experienced a left temporal hemorrhagic cerebral infarction on day 28 and died on day 31. Cause of death was listed as HSCT-TMA, acute
GVHD, and postallogeneic stem cell transplant aplastic anemia.

90-Day Safety Update
The 90-Day Safety Update presents additional safety data collected from the 2 ongoing clinical trials from December 21, 2019 to August 31, 2020
(Studies OMS721-HUS-002 and OMS721-IGA-001) and safety data from compassionate use patients with HSCT-TMA from December 21, 2019, to
October 31, 2020. Deaths, study drug discontinuations and serious adverse events (SAEs) were reviewed. Overall, there was no change in the
risk/benefit assessment based on the 90-day safety update. There were four deaths described in the 90-day safety update. This included one sudden
death in study OMS721-IgA-001 and three deaths in compassionate use patients. The narratives are described below.

18. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance:
Additional Information and Assessment
Narsoplimab-wuug is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks MASP-2, the effector
enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement. Further review of the mechanism of action of
narsoplimab-wuug was undertaken by the clinical team.
An information request was sent to the Applicant to further evaluate the mechanism of action in
terms of the HSCT-TMA. The Applicant provided an additional document pertaining to the
mechanism of action (MOA) of narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of HSCT-TMA on March
19, 2021. The Applicant also submitted responses to additional information requests (IRs) on
August 4, 2021, August 6, 2021 and August 12, 2021 which were also reviewed.
The Applicant states that there are several different lines of evidence that provide confirmatory
support of the mechanism of action of the drug to include in vitro models of HSCT-TMA that
demonstrate that narsoplimab-wuug inhibits complement deposition, endothelial cell injury, and
apoptosis. In addition, the Applicant provided data that the lectin pathway involvement in
HSCT-TMA is demonstrated by immunohistochemistry findings of deposition of lectin pathway
components in affected tissue of patients with HSCT-TMA.
The Applicant cites that separate studies of ongoing immunohistochemical analyses from skin
biopsies of patients with persistent HSCT-TMA show positive staining for MASP-2, the effector
enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement in 6 of 7 patients (Gavriilaki et al. 2017). In
patients who did not develop TMA, there was no MASP-2 staining in the biopsies suggesting
that TMA-related complement activation involves the lectin pathway. The Applicant states that
by blocking MASP-2, narsoplimab-wuug blocks activation and complement activation.
In plasma samples from patients with acute TMA arising from a variety of clinical settings to
include aHUS, HSCT-TMA, narsoplimab-wuug showed evidence of blocking microvascular
endothelial cell apoptosis. In this model of microvascular injury, primary human microvascular
endothelial cells grown in tissue culture media were exposed to control plasma or plasma from
individual patients with active HSCT-TMA, in the presence of narsoplimab-wuug or isotypematched control. Endothelial injury was evaluated by quantification of caspase 8 activity.
239
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Caspase 8 activation was observed in 14 out of the 22 TMA patient’s plasma. Blockade of
MASP-2 by narsoplimab-wuug led to a decrease in caspase 8 activity for all 14 patient plasma
suggesting that narsoplimab-wuug suppressed TMA plasma-mediated caspase 8 activation in
microvascular endothelial cells. This suggests that there is a role in for narsoplimab-wuug to
block MASP-2 however it is not known how much of this blockade impacts the downstream
signaling of the complement cascade.
The Applicant also provided data from an ex-vivo assay using HSCT-TMA patient serum and
the effect of narsoplimab-wuug. Serum from patients with acute HSCT-TMA had higher
complement (C5b-9) deposition on human microvascular endothelial cells and this response was
suppressed by addition of narsoplimab-wuug (Galbusera M et al. 2021). However, this is ex-vivo
data and it is unclear how much of suppressive response narsoplimab-wuug induces.
The Applicant provided data indicating that host responses to COVID-19 share aspects of
HSCT-TMA pathophysiology which can be improved upon with the use of narsoplimab-wuug.
According to the Applicant, COVID-19 and HSCT-TMA share a similar pathophysiology and
are endothelial injury syndromes. MASP-2 has been described in lung injury in patients with
coronavirus infection with MASP-2 and mannan-binding lectin staining in immunohistochemical
analysis of lung tissues from patients who died of COVID-19 (Magro et al. 2020). In addition,
microvascular deposits of MASP-2, C4d, C3d, and C5b-9 in some organ systems have been
described (Magro et al. 2020). The Applicant provided data from an initial cohort of 9 patients
with COVID-19 treated with narsoplimab-wuug under a compassionate use program. According
to the Applicant, patients had elevated circulating endothelial cells counts at baseline, which
decreased after the first week of narsoplimab-wuug. An additional cohort of 10 patients was
treated with narsoplimab-wuug under compassionate use. Eight of 10 patients recovered and
survived and were discharged following 5-8 doses of narsoplimab-wuug. The D-dimer levels
rapidly normalized following administration of narsoplimab-wuug and correspond with clinical
status/recovery. The Applicant states that severe COVID-19 is pathogenetically similar to
HSCT-TMA. Narsoplimab-wuug shows effects on TMA-associated pathophysiological
processes and clinical outcomes in critically ill patients with COVID-19.
Brief Summary of Nonclinical Data (Taken From Summary by Pharmacology-Toxicology
Review Team)
TMA Study in Mice
In vivo efficacy of OMS721 was assessed in a mouse model of TMA in which localized
thrombosis was induced by photo-activation of fluorescein isothiocyanate-labeled dextran in the
microvasculature of the cremaster muscle of C57Bl/6 mice.
Administering OMS721 one hour before induction of thrombosis delayed the initiation of
thrombus formation and the time to complete vessel occlusion, demonstrating a contribution of
the lectin complement pathway to the thrombotic response to injury. However, over time,
thrombus formation still occurred in a similar percentage of mice regardless of treatment,
suggesting that pathways in addition to lectin complement activation contribute to and eventually
result in microvascular thrombosis.

240
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4873032

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
Human aHUS Study
Serum was taken from healthy people or from aHUS patients and incubated with endothelial
cells in culture. MAC (C5b-9) deposition and platelet adherence to the EC were subsequently
measured.
•

Serum from aHUS patients robustly caused MAC deposition and platelet adherence to
ECs.

•

Narsoplimab partially prevented MAC deposition and platelet adhesion, indicating some
contribution of lectin, but also showing contribution by other C-activation pathways.

Narsoplimab is specific for MASP-2 and selectively blocks lectin-initiated complement
activation. The lectin pathway contributes to C-activation and thrombus formation in defined
models of endothelial injury, which presumably reflect a key element of TMA. Narsoplimab
inhibited this contribution. However, other C-activation pathways are also contributory; thus,
vascular occlusion appeared delayed, but occlusion still occurred.
There is insufficient mechanistic evidence to provide confirmatory evidence for this application.
There does appear to be sufficient literature to document the presence of MASP-2 and lectin
components in TMA and HSCT-TMA. However, the Applicant has not provided mechanistic
data to demonstrate a beneficial clinical outcome in TMA by blocking MASP-2. Although there
appears to be partial mitigation of C-activation and a delay in thrombotic onset by narsoplimabwuug in animal and in vitro models, the Applicant has not shown sufficient evidence that this
translates into a measurable clinical benefit, either directly or indirectly. It is possible that
narsoplimab-wuug’s inhibition of the lectin pathway is not sufficient to alter the outcome in
HSCT-TMA in a sufficient magnitude to demonstrate clinical benefit.

20. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
The Division requested that Office of Scientific Investigations inspect a clinical study clinical
investigator site, and the Applicant were inspected in support of BLA 761152. Based on these
inspections the conduct of the Study TMA-001 appears to be adequate. The study data derived
from the clinical investigator’s site and the Applicant are considered reliable.
For the clinical investigator site, a FDA Form 483, Inspectional Observations, was issued at the
end of the inspections for failure to prepare or maintain adequate case histories with respect to
(b) (6)
observations and data pertinent to the investigation. Specifically, a Subject
had a serious

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

BLA 761152
Narsoplimab-wuug
adverse event (worsening hospitalization for acute Graft Versus Host Disease) that was not

(b) (6)

Subject

system in a timely manner. The adverse events were coagulase negative staphylococcus
bacteremia due to a central line infection, metabolic acidosis secondary to renal dysfunction and
elevated alkaline phosphatase levels.
The response from the clinical site to FDA Form 483, dated January 12,2201, appeared adequate.

submission and these findings do not appear to have significant impact on the safety assessment
of the study.

inspection and no Form FDA-483, Inspectional Observations were issued.

23. Financial Disclosure
The Applicant provided certification on FDA Form 3454 to indicate that there were no financial
arrangements between the Applicant and Investigators that could be affected by the outcome of
Study TMA-001. The Applicant did not provide the number of investigators and an IR was sent
to the Applicant in which the Applicant stated that the total number of investigators identified
was 163. They also stated that two of the 163 investigators received payments that reached a
cumulative monetary value that exceeded $25,000 and that this occurred in 2021. The value did
not exceed $35,000. Per 21 CFR 54.2 significant payments of other sorts includes cumulative
monetary value that exceeds $25,000. The Applicant provided Form FDA 3455 for each
investigator that included the disclosable financial interest/arrangements and a description of the
steps taken to minimize potential bias.
